Qualcomm's Oryon 3 Reaches the Top
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
03:43:24 UTC
12:43:24 KST

Qualcomm’s laptop effort had humble beginnings. Their early Arm-based laptops used cell phone SoCs, and suffered from noticeably poor performance even against lower end Intel chips. But breaking into the PC market was going to be a marathon rather than a sprint, and Qualcomm has been persistent. Qualcomm created higher performance Snapdragon variants targeted at laptops, which became increasingly competent with each generation. Now, Qualcomm’s Snapdragon X2 Elite Extreme can deliver CPU performance that challenges the best of its x86-64 competition. To deliver that performance, Qualcomm relies on the third generation of their in-house Oryon architecture, which I’ll call Oryon 3 for short.
The Snapdragon X2 Elite deploys Oryon 3 in both E-Core and P-Core variants, which Qualcomm confusingly calls “performance” and “prime” cores, respectively. P-Cores are the star of the show in the Snapdragon X2 Elite, where they provide both maximum single threaded performance while also providing the bulk of multithreaded throughput. That’s because Qualcomm has P-Cores outnumber their E-Cores by a factor of two, creating an inverted hybrid strategy compared to Intel’s. Thanks to this strategy, Qualcomm’s Oryon 3 P-Core variant shoulders most of the burden when it comes to Qualcomm’s PC market ambitions.
Oryon 3 takes on this burden by deploying an architecture with a strong resemblance to the first Oryon generation. Qualcomm technically has a second generation Oryon core, but only used it in phones. Their website also suggests it’s a very minor change over the first generation, so I’ll be treating first generation Oryon as Oryon 3’s direct predecessor.
Similarity to Oryon 1 is a good thing, because that gives Oryon 3 just about everything you could ask for from a 2026-era high performance design. From Oryon 1, Oryon 3 inherits an out-of-order window in excess of 600 micro-ops. Qualcomm has also carried forward a two-level cache setup that emphasizes speed over capacity. Compared to its predecessor, Oryon 3 takes things up a notch by pushing clock speeds to 5 GHz. It’s a huge 16% increase over the 4.3 GHz speed that Oryon 1 could reach, and is enough to offer a generational performance gain on its own. But Qualcomm doesn’t stop there. Oryon 3 increases core width to 9 micro-ops, up from 8 in its predecessor, and makes a number of improvements throughout the pipeline. Qualcomm brings SVE2 and SME support to the new core as well, keeping it up-to-date with the evolving aarch64 architecture and opening up new software optimization opportunities. In its role as a maximum performance architecture, Oryon 3 faces off against the likes of AMD’s Zen 5 and Intel’s Lion Cove. Arm’s Cortex X925 is another competitor, thanks to Nvidia’s laptop push with N1X.
A Faster Frontend
Setting aside clock speed, Oryon 3’s headline architectural improvement is a core width increase from 8 to 9 ups. At the frontend, Qualcomm supports this by adding another decode slot. The fetch and decode pipeline otherwise looks mostly unchanged. Oryon 1 already had over-provisioned instruction cache bandwidth at 64 bytes per cycle, which is more than sufficient to feed a 8-wide or 9-wide aarch64 decoder. Qualcomm has also kept a 192 KB, 6-way instruction cache with hardware coherency, as well as a 256 entry 8-way instruction TLB. These large structures should help the core use its fastest instruction delivery path more often than its peers, which have smaller instruction caches and instruction TLBs. Impressively, Qualcomm managed to keep these large structures and add an extra decode slot while keeping a 13 cycle branch mispredict penalty. Clock speed increases often come with compromises like longer pipelines or smaller structures, but here Qualcomm is able to have their cake and eat it too.
Testing instruction bandwidth with a stream of NOPs validates the increased decoder width when the test loop fits within the instruction cache. Improvements continue at larger instruction footprints. Oryon 3 is able to sustain about 4 NOPs/cycle from L2, compared to 2 per cycle on Oryon 1. Qualcomm might have changed something with instruction prefetching from L2, or they might have widened datapaths between the instruction cache and L2. Whatever the case, Oryon 3 now matches the likes of AMD’s Zen 5 and Intel’s Lion Coves in terms of streaming code from L2, while boasting a much larger L2. AMD and Intel’s cores do have a L3 that provides more capacity than Qualcomm’s L2, but fetching code from that L3 will cause another drop in instruction throughput.
High core throughput places pressure on the branch predictor, which must follow the instruction stream fast enough to keep the core fed. Oryon 3 improves by expanding its predecessor’s ability to handle taken branches back-to-back. Oryon 1 could only do “zero-bubble” taken branches within a 8 KB instruction footprint. Oryon 3 can do so within a 2048 branch footprint, regardless of branch spacing and code footprint. Perhaps Qualcomm switched to using a fully decoupled 2048 entry BTB. Realistically, that means Oryon 3 should be able to do zero-bubble taken branches over a branch footprint 2x to 4x larger than its predecessor, considering typical branch spacing.
Compared to AMD and Intel’s latest cores, Qualcomm lacks any ability to handle more than one taken branch per cycle. Arm’s newer cores are also capable of doing two taken branches per cycle. I suspect AMD, Arm, and Intel decided that handling multiple taken branches per cycle became more profitable once their cores got wide enough, so seeing Qualcomm build a very wide core without that ability is surprising. Perhaps clock speed factors into that decision, because going from 4.3 to 5 GHz is a huge jump. If so, Qualcomm’s choice would be justified because a clock speed increase of that magnitude probably offers a larger performance increase than going for two taken branches per cycle. Of course, it’s impossible to know if branch predictor paths presented a timing challenge for Oryon.
For branches with dynamic taken/not-taken behavior, Oryon 3 has a 2-cycle conditional predictor. Testing with branches whose outcomes correlate with increasingly long history lengths shows an average 0.5 cycle increase in time per branch over an always- taken reference run, as long as the test stays within the conditional predictor’s capabilities. Because I randomly generate patterns so that each branch is taken roughly 50% of the time, a 0.5 cycle average penalty suggests Qualcomm only incurs the 2-cycle timing if the direction predictor disagrees with a base prediction. Direction prediction starts falling apart with more than 32K patterns in play, which is an improvement over the approximately 24K patterns that first generation Oryon could handle in this test.
For comparison, Zen 5’s conditional predictor can handle roughly 48K patterns before falling apart. Unlike Oryon 3, and unlike some older Zen generations, Zen 5 does not show an increase in branch latency compared to an always-taken pattern.
Indirect branches can go to multiple targets, and present another level of difficulty because the branch predictor has to select between previously seen targets, and must store more than one target per branch. Oryon 3 has a 2-cycle indirect predictor that can track 2048 indirect targets, matching its predecessor.
A single branch can jump to 256 targets without incurring a significant penalty. This could mean at least eight of the bits that Oryon 3 uses to index into its indirect target array are influenced by branch history.
A 50 entry return stack handles return predictions, just as on Oryon 1. However, testing shows that Oryon 3 can use all 50 entries before hitting a performance degradation. When call depth exceeds return stack capacity, Oryon 3 degrades gracefully rather than immediately taking a drastic penalty as Oryon 1 does. Oryon 1’s performance drop is heavy enough to suggest it’s mispredicting every return after the return stack overflows.
Perhaps Qualcomm implemented a new mechanism to keep a single overflow from messing up the entire return stack, and lets Oryon 3 only mispredict returns whose addresses get forced out on an overflow. Whatever this optimization is, it gives Oryon 3 return prediction behavior in-line with many other cores, including AMD’s Zen 5 and Intel’s Skymont. In capacity terms, AMD’s Zen 5 has a slightly larger 52 entry return stack.
Rename/Allocate Optimizations
Optimizations at the rename and allocate stage on Qualcomm’s big core are remarkably similar to those on its little core counterpart. Both cores can eliminate up to two register-to-register MOVs per cycle. They can also eliminate up to two add-immediate instructions, as long as their immediates sum to 255 or less. It’s slightly strange that Qualcomm has kept the same optimization capability even though the big core is more than twice as wide as its little core companion. That results in degraded microbenchmark figures when I chain dependent MOVs or add-immediate instructions without a break, because the wider core can get more of those instructions into the same rename group, which means the two-per-cycle restriction causes more missed optimization opportunities.
Backend: Large, and Largely Unchanged
One of the best strategies in CPU architecture is to avoid changing something that isn’t broken. Oryon 3’s backend at a high level is basically identical to the one in its predecessor. That makes sense because if Oryon 1 encountered performance issues, they likely had little to do with its backend strategy. Qualcomm specifies a reorder buffer capacity of over 650 micro-ops for both Oryon 1 and Oryon 3. Microbenchmarking puts capacity at 675 micro-ops for Oryon 3, and 680 on Oryon 1. Register files and memory ordering queues see little to no movement in size as well.
Qualcomm’s choice to avoid major backend changes is justified, because Oryon 3’s backend structures remain competitively sized even against emerging competition like AMD’s Zen 6. Execution throughput also remains competitive. Oryon 3 does have slightly smaller memory ordering queues, and weaker vector execution throughput compared to its x86-64 counterparts. The latter comes down to having a smaller 128-bit vector width, which is especially a disadvantage against Zen 5. However, that should only affect a small minority of applications.
Even though Oryon 3’s backend is similar to its predecessor, there’s still plenty of change under the hood. Predication is a central feature in SVE2 and SME, so Oryon 3 gets a new 120 entry predicate register file. The predicate registers themselves only have to be 16 bits wide because of Oryon 3’s 128-bit vector length. But while the predicate register file doesn’t need a lot of storage, it does need to be hooked up to every major execution unit block. Predicate generation can happen from both the scalar and vector side. All four vector pipes and four load/store pipes support predication, meaning a lot of reads from the predicate register file.
New SVE2 and SME instructions also require changes to backend logic. SVE’s vector length agnostic design rides heavily on predicate generation instructions like those of the WHILE* variety (like WHILELO). Oryon 3 breaks WHILE* instructions into two micro-ops, which execute through the integer pipes to deliver a throughput of one WHILE* instruction per cycle. Predicate generation instructions that don’t require a GPR input, like PTRUE and CMPEQ, execute through two FP/vector pipes. Besides predication focused changes, SVE2 adds new math instructions like CDOT, meaning more execution unit functionality behind the vector/FP ports.
Load/Store
Like Oryon 1, Oryon 3 has a four-pipe load/store unit. Memory dependencies are handled by a robust store forwarding mechanism, which can do fast forwarding for all cases where a load overlaps a prior store. Oryon 3 is able to do thai even when the load isn’t contained within the older store, meaning the load/store unit is able to combine data from both an in-flight store and data fetched from the L1 data cache. A dependent store+load pair has a latency of 6 cycles on the integer side, or 8 cycles on the vector/FP side. While Oryon 3 can’t do zero-latency store forwarding the way AMD’s Zen 5 and Intel’s Lion Cove can, the ability to avoid the 14 or 19 cycle penalty those cores take in partial overlap cases is quite nice. Arm’s Cortex X925 has the weakest store forwarding capabilities of the bunch, with no zero-cycle capability and a 12-13 cycle partial overlap penalty.
For independent accesses, Oryon 3 prefers 16B alignment for stores. Stores that cross a 16B boundary take a slight penalty, though curiously there’s no penalty for crossing a 64B cacheline boundary. Perhaps Oryon 3 has a special optimization for speeding up line-crossing stores. The same characteristics apply on the vector side.
SVE introduces scatter and gather functionality to the load/store unit, forcing it to handle possibly non-contiguous accesses from a single instruction. Testing with LD1W gathers (32-bit elements) achieves a core-wide throughput of 1-2 elements per cycle. Gathers therefore have lower throughput not just compared to contiguous loads, but also equivalent scalar loads. However, Oryon 3 has an optimization that allows repeated gather loads to the same address to execute at 4/cycle. Gathers therefore seem capable of using all four LSU pipes, and don’t go down a smaller subset of specialized pipes. However, the general case seems to rely on a complex flow that involves several micro-ops, resulting in lower throughput.
Dependent gathers have 14 cycle latency, which is acceptable for a non-contiguous load operation. Zen 5 handles vaguely equivalent AVX2 gathers with 19 cycle latency.
The load/store unit also has to translate program-visible virtual addresses to physical addresses that correspond to DRAM locations (or possibly MMIO). Qualcomm sized up the L1 DTLB from a 224 entry 7-way structure on Oryon 1, to a 256 entry 8-way one on Oryon 3. Perhaps Qualcomm always intended for the L1 DTLB to be 256 entries and 8-way set associative like the instruction-side L1 TLB, but couldn’t fit the last way into Oryon 1. A 256 entry L1 DTLB covers 1 MB of address space using 4 KB pages, which is a lot compared to the smaller L1 DTLBs on other designs. An 8K entry L2 TLB handles larger memory footprints, and serves both instruction and data accesses. The L2 TLB’s capacity is also impressive compared to the competition.
Comparing with Zen 5 can be complicated because AMD can opportunistically use a single TLB entry to cover up to four consecutive 4 KB pages, provided the translations have the same attributes and lead to contiguous physical pages. Zen 5 can cover more address space with its TLBs despite a lower entry count if it can make maximum use of that page coalescing capability, though I don’t have data on how effective that is in practice.
Testing with performance counters validates Qualcomm’s large TLB sizes. The core’s PMU indicates a rise in L1D TLB misses after 1 MB, when testing accesses with a 4 KB stride. L2 TLB misses rise after 32 MB, though they only sharply increase after 64 MB.
Qualcomm specifies 2-cycle latency for L2 TLB accesses, which would be incredible for a 8K entry L2 TLB. However, comparing L2 latency with 4 KB and 2 MB pages suggests that a L2 TLB hit costs at least an extra 6-7 cycles of latency.
Cache and Memory Access
Qualcomm’s Snapdragon X Elite used a two-level caching strategy that emphasizes speed over the ability to scale last level cache capacity and the number of cores served by each last level cache instance. For the Snapdragon X2 Elite Qualcomm upgraded the cache subsystem by increasing L2 capacity from 12 to 16 MB for the P-Core clusters. This cache capacity increase boosts the amount of cache a single core can allocate into. It also helps account for each cluster having six cores instead of four, though cache per core still drops slightly from 3 MB on Oryon 1 to 2.66 MB in Oryon 3.
16 MB of CPU-side last level cache isn’t large by 2026 standards. AMD’s standard Zen 5 configurations use 32 MB of L3 cache, while Intel’s Arrow Lake has 36 MB of L3. However, Qualcomm’s caching capacity is fine next to the 16 MB of L3 that AMD puts in their mainstream Strix Point mobile chip’s performance cluster, and Qualcomm makes their L2 fast for the capacity it provides. With 20-23 cycle latency depending on which core is making the access, Qualcomm’s L2 can return data faster than the 46 cycle latency that AMD’s optimization guide specifies for Zen 5’s L3. AMD’s 46 cycle figure seems to apply to configurations with 32 MB of L3, like on the Ryzen 9 9900X. A Zen 5 variant with 96 MB of L3 incurs several extra cycles of latency, showing how a triple level cache subsystem can enable last level cache capacity scaling because the core-private L2 cache reduces sensitivity to L3 latency. Intel’s Lion Cove is Oryon 3’s opposite with respect to caching strategy, with four cache levels to enable capacity/speed tradeoffs at L2 and L3.
20+ cycles of L2 latency is still high compared to 14 cycles on AMD’s Zen 5, so Oryon 3 uses a large 96 KB 6-way L1D. Unusually for a high frequency design, Oryon 3 achieves 3 cycle L1D latency. It’s an impressive achievement, because even Arm’s lower clocked Cortex X925 has 4 cycle latency to a smaller 64 KB L1D.
Oryon 3 only shows a slight latency increase as a pointer chasing test spills out of the L1D when using a 64 byte stride. Using a shorter stride increases the length of the pointer chasing chain, and more clearly shows the L1D’s capacity. Qualcomm seems to have a “correlated miss caching” (CMC) style prefetcher like many of Arm’s designs, which tracks when a cache miss for one address is likely to be followed by a second miss to another address. This type of prefetcher can follow pointer chasing chains and defeat latency testing if the tested chain falls within what the prefetcher can remember. If Qualcomm is doing this, and they do suggest it by indicating they have “pointer” prefetching algorithms, then the data above points to it being a L1D prefetcher.
Arm in contrast places their CMC prefetcher at L2, which lets it be more aggressive because a larger L2 cache is less sensitive to having some capacity wasted by useless prefetches. For example, the CMC prefetcher on Arm’s Cortex A78 is able to reduce latency across the Mediatek Genio 1200’s 2 MB L3 when testing with a 64B stride compared to a 8B stride (at least 32K pointer destination predictions compared to 8K on Oryon 3).
Bandwidth
Oryon 3’s L1D can service four accesses per cycle, all four of which can be loads, and two of which can be stores. Compared to Zen 5 and Lion Cove, Oryon 3’s maximum load bandwidth is held back by its 128-bit vector width. However, Oryon 3 should remain very competitive when running code that can’t be well vectorized, or vectorized code that doesn’t scale to longer vector lengths.
L2 bandwidth is just under 32B/cycle for reads, in terms of how much data the core can get into registers. Qualcomm states that each core has a 64B/cycle datapath to L2. This can be validated by only accessing the first 128 bits of each 64B line, and treating that as loading the entire line because cache fills happen at 64B line granularity. Doing so gives about 48B/cycle of inferred L2 to L1D bandwidth. A read-modify-write pattern that creates equal traffic in both the L1D fill and writeback directions also reaches 48B/cycle.
I have a separate piece for more on the Snapdragon X2 Elite’s system level memory subsystem characteristics. Overall though, there’s a lot to like about Oryon 3’s memory subsystem. As long as you don’t benefit from having more than 16 MB of last level cache in each core cluster, Qualcomm’s cache setup should be more than a match for the triple level setups used by Intel, AMD, and other Arm cores.
Performance: SPEC CPU2026
Because running Linux on the Snapdragon X2 Elite Extreme is complicated at the moment and difficult considering other time constraints (Chips and Cheese isn’t a job after all, and I have a real job to pay attention to), I tested SPEC CPU2026 using Windows Subsystem on Linux. I’m pinning the vmmemWSL process to a P-Core for predictability. Results show the Oryon 3 P-Core delivering desktop-level SPEC CPU2026 integer scores, even though it’s implemented in a laptop. That’s an impressive showing because desktop chips enjoy advantages like higher cache capacity, lower DRAM latency, and higher clock speeds. A comparison against a typical AMD or Intel mobile part would almost certainly hand Oryon 3 a victory rather than a tie.
Qualcomm does score slightly behind Zen 5 in SPEC’s floating point suite, but manages to stay ahead of Intel. Individual subtests show modest differences between the cores, though there are outliers. 706.stockfish for example hands Zen 5 a large win, because GCC 14.2.0 was able to vectorize a large part of it to use AVX-512.
However, AVX-512 does not give AMD a huge lead in SPEC’s floating point workloads. 749.fotonik3d and 765.roms execute a lot of AVX-512 instructions on Zen 5, but Oryon 3 is able to compensate with higher IPC. Both workloads tend to be more memory bound as well, reducing the benefit of AMD’s higher vector throughput.
Further performance analysis is difficult without more work because WSL only passes through a subset of performance monitoring events. However, there’s enough data to show that Qualcomm’s branch prediction accuracy is up there with the best. Overall branch prediction accuracy is actually a bit better than that of AMD’s Zen 5. Oryon 3 even scores a win in 723.llvm, which is SPEC CPU2026’s toughest branch prediction workload.
Performance counters also show the merit of Oryon 3’s large L1D. L1D cache sizes have remained stubbornly stagnant outside of Qualcomm (and Apple) cores. In 1998, AMD rolled out their Athlon core with a 64 KB L1 data cache. No current cores from AMD, Intel, or Arm have a larger L1D, and I don’t put Lion Cove’s 192 KB “level 1 of the level 1 cache” in the same category as a conventional L1D because of its higher latency.
Each vendor’s performance monitoring unit accounts for L1D misses differently, but the differences are large enough to show that Qualcomm can service a lot more requests out of its fastest cache level than Intel or AMD.
Final Words
Third generation Oryon is a top-notch core with everything needed to succeed on a level playing field. Qualcomm has given their core a large out-of-order engine, is able to run it at 5 GHz, and feeds it with a competent branch predictor and a low latency cache setup. Oryon 3 joins Arm’s Cortex X925 in delivering desktop-level performance, and both Arm cores should be seen as serious pacing challenges for Intel and AMD.
Oryon 3 isn’t perfect of course. A wider SVE vector width could improve its situation relative to Zen 5 in vectorized workloads. Going to six vector/floating point pipes like in Arm’s Cortex X925 is an option too, though it may be difficult to pull off without regressing clock speeds. Qualcomm’s branch predictor could pick up some of the two taken branches per cycle goodness that’s now common on AMD, Arm, and Intel cores. Cache capacity could be an issue too, because a two-level cache setup forces a tightly coupled L2 design to meet latency requirements. That could make cache capacity difficult to scale.
All of those are areas for improvement, rather than significant weaknesses. SPEC scores for example show that a core doesn’t need to sustain two taken branches per cycle to be up there with the best, at least not in this generation. As for cache capacity, I can’t think of a consumer scenario where that makes a huge impact except for gaming. Games of course struggle with a binary translation issue, which is outside Qualcomm’s control.
Binary translation does bring up a problem for Oryon 3 though, because having a good core is only one piece of the puzzle. The software ecosystem that Qualcomm has to work with is a persistent sticking point, because while Windows on Arm has improved over the years, it’s still not as polished as a native x86-64 setup. Qualcomm has also struggled with Linux support. It’s a missed opportunity, because Linux is increasingly being seen as a strong alternative to Windows due to Windows 11’s misplaced priorities. Linux applications are also more likely to have aarch64 versions, minimising the importance of binary translation. Beyond the software ecosystem, Qualcomm’s laptop push faces headwinds because high DRAM costs push consumers to defer buying new devices. Laptops with soldered memory are especially vulnerable, because there’s no chance of buying a device with less memory with hopes of upgrading it later once prices drop. These challenges must be frustrating for hardware engineers who worked on Oryon 3, because they’ve built their most competitive CPU architecture yet, but need more puzzle pieces to fall into place.
I hope Qualcomm continues to run the marathon of breaking into the PC market, and the puzzle pieces line up soon to enable success. Oryon 3 is an excellent core. I’ve used the Zenbook A16 laptop sampled by Asus on a recent casual trip, and used it as a daily driver for a few weeks. The laptop is subjectively as fast as my desktop back at home, and shows just how far Qualcomm has gone. I want to see the people who worked on it rewarded for what they achieved. I also hope to see the Oryon line grow with future generations, and keep pushing the leading edge of single threaded performance.

OpenAI cuts GPT-6.2 usage: Astra hits limits 40% faster, Sol 25% faster
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
03:36:58 UTC
12:36:58 KST

We made a billing mistake at launch of GPT-6.2 that incorrectly discounted GPT-6.2 Astra and Sol usage for most users. 
We’ve fixed this now, so the same work will use more of your plan’s usage allowance. Comparing to last week, you will reach your limits 40% faster on GPT-6.2 Astra and 25% faster on Sol. You should still see these go further in usage compared to 6 Astra and 6.1 Sol. Other models weren’t affected, and we won’t charge you for the difference on past usage.
As a small token to apologize for the mistake, we’ll be adding one banked reset for everyone on Go, Plus, Pro, and Business.

World Has Six Consecutive Days 2C Above Preindustrial Levels For First Time
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

World Has Six Consecutive Days 2C Above Preindustrial Levels For First Time (theguardian.com) 13
			
		 	
				Longtime Slashdot reader AmiMoJo shares a report from The Guardian: For the first time, the world has experienced six consecutive days of temperatures 2C warmer than preindustrial levels, the Guardian has learned. This record hot streak, which is continuing, is too brief to signify that the upper range of the Paris climate agreement target has been breached, but scientists said it was an early indication of what is to come unless fossil fuel emissions are rapidly eliminated. It is also likely to feed into growing scientific concerns that global heating may be accelerating. The daily global surface temperature rose to its highest ever point above preindustrial levels, 2.24C, on Monday, the most recent day for which data is available, and is forecast to persist at similarly high levels for at least several more days. "This will amplify the buildup of CO2 in the atmosphere driven by fossil fuel burning," said Richard Betts, the head of climate impacts research at the Met Office Hadley Centre. "The CO2 increase from this year to next will be about 3.5 parts per million, a third faster than in recent years and nearly twice as fast as what it should have been limited to if we'd been on track to limit global warming to 1.5C."
Zeke Hausfather, a climate researcher at Berkeley Earth, said: "Another day, another global temperature record," adding that the previous record of 2.05C over the 1850-1900 baseline, set in 2023, has been "smashed."

Minipg: PostgreSQL as a Single Executable
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
03:00:48 UTC
12:00:48 KST

minipg is PostgreSQL as one executable, ready to use, with no server to configure. A .pgdb directory is the database: its data, config, credentials, and Postgres version all live inside it, and minipg app.pgdb opens a psql shell on it. A server starts when a command needs one and stops on its own when it's idle.
minipg embeds Docker Hub's postgres:18-alpine image and runs it with h9, used as a Zig library. h9's Linux personality runs Alpine's own Postgres binaries directly on the host's hypervisor, with h9's Plan 9 kernel answering their system calls. There's no Linux kernel to boot, no VM image, and no Docker.
The demo downloads about 35 MB. It needs JavaScript Promise Integration, which Chrome and Edge have from version 137; other browsers are getting it. It can also be opened on its own page.
The demo runs minipg's Linux build, which runs inside the Postgres image itself. It starts Postgres as ordinary processes and serves on a unix socket, since a page has no TCP. New databases come from a template cluster made at build time, because initdb takes half a minute on v86.
minipg app.pgdb                     # psql shell (creates app.pgdb if missing)
minipg app.pgdb "select 1"          # one statement
minipg url app.pgdb                 # postgres://postgres:...@127.0.0.1:54321/app
minipg serve app.pgdb               # an explicit foreground server
minipg run app.pgdb -- npm start    # your app, with DATABASE_URL set
minipg dump app.pgdb > dump.sql     # and minipg restore app.pgdb dump.sql
A .pgdb holds minipg.toml (settings and the generated superuser password) and pgdata/, Postgres's data directory as plain files. A stopped one can be copied like any directory. New clusters use Postgres's builtin C.UTF-8 locale, which sorts the same on every platform.
In CI, the Linux binary is static and needs no privileges or devices: without /dev/kvm, h9 runs Postgres on its native backend. A test job can be as small as:
minipg init test.pgdb
minipg run test.pgdb -- go test ./...
The README lists every command, and the manual is the full guide.
Exactly one process runs Postgres for a .pgdb, enforced by a lock. It holds the server's h9 machine, whose Linux root is the unpacked image and whose writable working directory is the .pgdb. The image's docker-entrypoint.sh initializes the cluster on first start. Commands never host Postgres themselves. If no server is running, they start one in the background and use it. psql, pg_dump, and the other tools each run in their own short-lived h9 machine (about 90 ms to start) over the same root.
The source is on GitHub, under the MIT license. Building needs Zig 0.16, plus curl, jq, tar, and xz for the root:
git clone --recursive https://github.com/progrium/minipg
cd minipg
make build         # pulls the root for this host's arch, builds ./minipg
make root ARCH=386
make web           # this page's demo, in .zig-out/web
minipg is early, and h9's Linux personality is young. On h9's native backend, Postgres is about 2x slower than native for one client, and slower under load. Don't trust it with data you can't recreate yet.
Please report bugs and send questions through GitHub issues.

Tell HN: Lots of Ask HNs again
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
00:43:34 UTC
09:43:34 KST

Ask HN: Why is Ask HN only showing me 14 posts? - https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49984484 - Oct 2026 (47 comments)
Ask HN: Why is pagination gone on "Ask HN", and showing only 19 items? - https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50280719 - Oct 2026 (3 comments)
I believe I found the problem. The pool of stories for https://news.ycombinator.com/ask has traditionally been drawn from the ranked stories list from which we generate the frontpage. That list is restricted to the top 1500 ranked submissions, and it seems the % of submissions which are text posts (i.e. no linked URL) had declined enough to starve the Ask HN feed. The solution is to compute a separate list of ranked Ask HNs.
p.s. "Ask HN" is the conventional name but it means text posts. The title can also start with "Tell HN" or nothing in particular. They're just posts which were submitted without a URL.

Ask HN: Why is pagination gone on "Ask HN", and showing only 19 items? - https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=50280719 - Oct 2026 (3 comments)
I believe I found the problem. The pool of stories for https://news.ycombinator.com/ask has traditionally been drawn from the ranked stories list from which we generate the frontpage. That list is restricted to the top 1500 ranked submissions, and it seems the % of submissions which are text posts (i.e. no linked URL) had declined enough to starve the Ask HN feed. The solution is to compute a separate list of ranked Ask HNs.

Collection of preserved UI style guides
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
00:40:00 UTC
09:40:00 KST

Marcin Wichary
25 November 2026 / 69 style guides
UI style guides
This is a collection of preserved UI style guides a.k.a. interface guidelines from various companies, starting from the early 1980s and continuing to today.
- Amiga (Commodore) Amiga was a multimedia personal computer particularly popular in the 1980s and 1990s in Europe. Its GUI was called Workbench.
-         
      Amiga User Interface Style Guide
- Android Android is a mobile operating system and a rival to iOS. The first style guide I could locate was in 2012.
-         
      Android Design
-         
      Material Design v1
-         
      Material Design v2
-         
      Design for Android
- BlackBerry BlackBerry was a beleagured maker of mobile handsets.
-         
      BlackBerry Wireless Handheld User Interface Style Guide
-         
      BlackBerry PlayBook Tablet UI Guidelines
-         
      BlackBerry Smartphones UI Guidelines
-         
      BlackBerry 10 UI Guidelines
- CDE Common Desktop Environment (CDE) is a desktop environment for Unix machines, using (and eventually merging with) OSF/Motif widgets.
-         
      OSF/Motif Style Guide
-         
      Common Desktop Environment: Style Guide and Certification Checklist
- CUA Common User Access (CUA) is a late 1980s standard for user interfaces that influenced Windows and OS/2.
-         
        System Application Architecture: Common User Access
-         
        Object-Oriented Interface Design: IBM Common User Access Guidelines
- GEM/TOS Graphics Environment Manager (GEM) is a 1980s operating system/GUI. It was well-known by users of the Atari ST computers, known in these circles as TOS.
-         
      GEM User Interface Guidelines
-         
      The documentation for TOS
- GNOME GNOME is a desktop environment for Linux and BSD operating systems. Note that the versioning number of these guidelines is very confusing.
-         
      GNOME Human Interface Guidelines
-         
      GNOME Human Interface Guidelines
-         
      GNOME Human Interface Guidelines
- iOS and iPadOS iOS (previously iPhone OS) is Apple’s mobile operating system for iPhone smartphones and their siblings.
-         
      iPhone Human Interface GuidelinesCovers the skeuomorphic era of iOS 1–6
-         
      iOS Human Interface GuidelinesCovers iOS 7+
-         
      iPad Human Interface Guidelines
-         
      Apple Human Interface Guidelines
- Java Java is a programming language, which comes equipped with JFC (Java Foundation Classes) and Swing GUI components.
-         
      Java Look and Feel Design Guidelines
-         
      Java Look and Feel Design Guidelines (2nd edition)
-         
      Java Look and Feel Design Guidelines: Advanced Topics
- Macintosh These are some historical Apple GUI style guides, starting with Lisa in the 1980, and continuing to the “classic” Mac OS, with a brief stop in the Apple II corner.
-         
      Apple Lisa
-         
      Apple II
-         
      Human Interface Guidelines
-         
      Macintosh Human Interface Guidelines
-         
      Mac OS 8 Human Interface Guidelines
- Mac More modern Apple Mac style guides, covering Mac OS X/Aqua and beyond.
-         
      Aqua Human Interface GuidelinesCovers early years of Aqua
-         
      Apple Human Interface GuidelinesCovers evolving Aqua
-         
      OS X Interface GuidelinesCovers the era between pure Aqua and Liquid Glass
-         
      Apple Human Interface Guidelines
- Magic Cap An abandoned mobile operating system designed by General Magic.
-         
      Magic Cap Concepts
- Microsoft Office Guidelines specific to Microsoft Office, in particular covering the use of its ribbon.
-         
      Microsoft Office System User Interface Design Guidelines
- NeXTSTEP NeXTSTEP was NeXT’s influential operating system and graphical user interface. It eventually morphed into OpenStep on one side, and modern Mac OS X on another.
-         
        NeXTstep Concepts
-         
        NeXTSTEP User Interface Guidelines
-         
        OpenStep User Interface Guidelines
- Newton Apple Newton was Apple’s PDA launched in 1993 and abandoned in 1998.
-         
      Newton 2.0 User Interface Guidelines
- Nokia Nokia released various style guides for its early smartphones, including its Symbian operating system.
-         
    The User Interface of Nokia 9110 Communicator
-         
      S60 UI Style Guide
-         
      Symbian3 UI Style Guide
-         
      Symbian Belle UI Style Guide
- OPEN LOOK OPEN LOOK is a UNIX graphical user interface, started by Sun and AT&T.
-         
      OPEN LOOK: Graphical User Interface Functional Specification
-         
      OPEN LOOK: Graphical User Interface Application Style Guidelines
- Palm OS Palm OS was the original graphical operating system for Palm’s mobile devices. Eventually, the company moved toward webOS, covered elsewhere on this page.
-         
        Palm OS User Interface Guidelines
-         
        Zen of Palm
- PenPoint PenPoint was an early tablet operating system developed by GO Corporation for its unsuccessful line of devices.
-         
      PenPoint UI Design Guidelines
- RISC OS RISC OS is a British operating system and GUI, developed originally for Acorn computers, and continuing as a community project.
-         
      RISC OS Style Guide
- Samsung Various Samsung style guides.
-         
      The Samsung Gear UI Design Guidelines
-         
      One UI Design Guidelines
-         
      Samsung Smart TV Design Guides
- Silicon Graphics Style guides for various UNIX-based operating system from SGI, or Silicon Graphics Inc. Just like CDE above, these too use the Motif widgets.
-         
      SGI Indigo Magic User Interface Guidelines
-         
      SGI IRIX Interactive Desktop User Interface Guidelines
- Taligent Taligent was a troubled software company, incorporated as an Apple/IBM partnership. One of its fruits was a CommonPoint application framework.
-         
      Human Interface Guidelines
- webOS webOS was a successor to Palm OS that was eventually purchased by HP and later LG Electronics. Today, it is used on smart TVs, refrigerators, and projectors.
-         
        Palm webOS User Interface Design Guidelines
-         
        Palm Design Guide
-         
        HP Touchpad webOS Design Guidelines
- Windows Does Microsoft Windows need an introduction? Note a little hole here around 1990, where Microsoft was preoccupied with CUA framework in partnership with IBM.
-         
      Microsoft Windows Software Development Kit Application Style Guide
-         
      The Windows Interface: An Application Design Guide
-         
      The Windows Interface Guidelines for Software Design
-         
      Microsoft Windows User Experience: Official Guidelines for User Interface Developers and Designers
-         
      User Experience – Official Guidelines for User Interface Developers and Designers
-         
      Windows XP Visual Guidelines
-         
      Windows 8–10 User Experience Guidelines
-         
      Design basics for Windows apps
- Windows Phone Style guides for failed mobile Windows efforts, starting with Windows Mobile 6, and continuing through Windows Phone 7.
-         
      Designing Applications for Windows Mobile Powered Platforms
-         
      UI Design and Interaction Guide for Windows Phone 7
That’s it! Please let me know if you have any thoughts and particularly if you have some style guides that are missing from the above list.
Some of the above mirrors were lightly cleaned up to make their consumption easier – stuff like pixellating images better, inlining images instead of linking to them, or fixing YouTube embeds.
You can also download all the above style guides in one archive.
Thank you to the following people for their collections that contributed to the above – you should check them out, since each one offers a different take and interface:
- Geof Crowl’s
- Matt Sephton’s (with help from Mario Guzmán and Brendan Donohoe)
- Matthias Rauterberg’s
- Přemysl Eric Janouch’s

At Least You’ll Have Equity
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
00:03:28 UTC
09:03:28 KST

I’ve been dipping my toe in the interview process after a year+ on the Pro Leisure Circuit. One of the more recent interviews was with a hardware company out of LA. When we first started chatting, I explained, “I’m a consumer software guy who has been hardware adjacent.”
Their response: “Don’t worry, we have plans; you’d be a great fit.”
Interviews. Then more interviews. I’m intrigued, but not sold.
The robots are excellent at research, so I researched. Interviews, podcasts, press releases, LinkedIn profiles. They wouldn’t tell me why I was a great fit, so I’d use the robots to figure it out. Several hours later, I jokingly realized why they wanted me. I typed this realization into Claude not because I was expecting a response, but… because.
“They want me to build Skynet.”
To which Claude immediately responded, “At least when it achieves consciousness, you’ll have equity.”
R.I.P. that opportunity.

1Password Breach Leaked Customer Names and Emails, But Passwords Are Safe
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
00:00:00 UTC
09:00:00 KST

1Password Breach Leaked Customer Names and Emails, But Passwords Are Safe (techcrunch.com) 10
			
		 	
				Password manager 1Password has confirmed that hackers breached its internal systems on November 19 after gaining access to an employee's corporate GitHub account and pushing malicious code to company servers. The attacker stole customer names, email addresses, account creation dates and last login timestamps, but 1Password said customer password vaults were "at no point" accessible to the attacker and that its apps and browser extensions remain unaffected. TechCrunch reports:  It's unclear how the attacker gained access to the employee's corporate account, and a spokesperson for 1Password did not immediately respond to TechCrunch's questions about the incident. 1Password did not say whether it had notified customers whose information was taken in the breach, or how many customers had information taken. [...] The company touts millions of customers and more than 200,000 businesses who use its password manager products to store their passwords, credentials, payment card information, and more. Security experts have long recommended password managers as a better way of generating stronger credentials and keeping track of online accounts.

Amazon loses its latest attempt to get out of handling more recall notices
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
23:56:44 UTC
08:56:44 KST

Amazon is on the hook to recall more than 400,000 hazardous products after losing its legal battle against the US Consumer Product Safety Commission (CPSC). The US District Court for the District of Maryland ruled in favor of the CPSC, which argued that Amazon is a distributor of goods, not a logistics provider, making it responsible for carrying out recalls of third-party products.
Amazon loses its latest attempt to get out of handling more recall notices
A court ruled in favor of the US CPSC’s argument that Amazon is responsible for recalled products that other companies sell on its platforms.
Amazon sued the CPSC in March 2025 after the agency ordered Amazon to recall over 400,000 “Fulfilled by Amazon” products that pose a “substantial product hazard,” including faulty carbon monoxide detectors, hair dryers without electrocution protection, and children’s sleepwear that doesn’t meet federal flammability standards.
As a result, Amazon will be required to implement the following measures, initially outlined in the CPSC’s January 2025 order:
Notification:
On the day CPSC publishes the recall releases, Amazon must notify the public by posting the recall releases to Amazon.com; notify original purchasers directly via email and by providing information regarding the recalls on each purchaser’s “Your Orders” page.
Amazon must provide one round of notice of the recalls to Fulfilled by Amazon participants that sold the subject products.
Incentive to Remove Products from Commerce:
Amazon must issue a full refund in the amount of the purchase price to any consumer who submits proof of destruction or disposal of a subject product.
Maintaining Records of Recalls:
Amazon must maintain the recall releases on the “Recalls and Product Safety Alerts” webpage on amazon.com for a minimum of five years.
Amazon must submit monthly progress reports for five years and maintain records of its actions to comply with the Order for the same period.
“We have a longstanding commitment to removing unsafe and recalled products from our store and notifying customers, backed by an industry-leading recalls process,” Amazon spokesperson Sammy Brill says in an emailed statement to The Verge. “We’re reviewing our options following the ruling and look forward to continuing to work with the CPSC to put customer safety first.”
Update, November 25th: Added Amazon’s statement.

Suspected Chinese Agents Are Trying to Dupe Americans with Fake Listings on Job Platforms
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
23:00:00 UTC
08:00:00 KST

Suspected Chinese Agents Are Trying to Dupe Americans with Fake Listings on Job Platforms (cnn.com) 13
			
		 	
				"LinkedIn is probably the greatest intelligence tool that's ever been created," CNN heard from Holden Triplett, director of counterintelligence at the National Security Council in the first Trump administration.
A LinkedIn spokesperson responded that "Creating a fake account or misrepresenting your identity is a clear violation of our terms of service."  (And they also told CNN that LinkedIn remains "focused on detecting state-sponsored abuse and will continue to enforce our policies against fake accounts.")  But it's not just happening on LinkedIn...
Suspected Chinese spies have created an elaborate web of fake firms with job opportunities aimed at tricking current and former US officials into divulging government secrets as part of a scheme that is more pervasive than previously reported, CNN has found.  Interviews with a dozen people caught up in the operation — as well as CNN analysis of suspected bogus websites — reveal how there is a much broader target pool for obtaining sensitive US information after the Trump administration laid off thousands of federal employees. [CNN reported in 2025 that "foreign powers such as China and Russia" had directed their intelligence services "to ramp up recruiting of Americans working in national security by targeting those who had been fired or felt they were at risk of losing their jobs".] These workers left their posts with inadequate guidance and protection from spying plots, ex-officials said, creating a potentially valuable resource for Beijing and weak spot for Washington...  In addition to government workers, the plot has targeted journalists, trade consultants and others, who might have knowledge deemed valuable to US adversaries.
The FBI offered a glimpse into the malicious recruiting scam when it seized 13 websites officials say were tied to fake consulting firms in June. China denied the FBI allegations. CNN analyzed the sites' software code, looked at other suspicious job listings, and interviewed people approached by similar companies. CNN identified several more websites that mimicked the style and substance of those disabled by the FBI.   Two more sites that Max Lesser, a senior analyst at the Foundation for Defense of Democracies think tank, discovered and shared with CNN have some of the same web infrastructure or other connections to the seized websites.  In total, Lesser has uncovered more than 100 other web domains, many of which share similar characteristics with the sites taken down by the FBI....
This sprawling network has the hallmarks of a Chinese intelligence operation, Lesser told CNN.  
After creating fake web sites, the operatives "plant vague, well-paying job ads for consulting positions focused on China-related issues" on jobs sites like LinkedIn, CNN reports, asking applicants to produce "research reports". ("[D]ozens or hundreds of such reports, across various topics of interest to Beijing, can start to form a much clearer picture of US national security policy and its vulnerabilities, according to current and former FBI officials.")
CNN sent a detailed request for a comment to a spokesperson for the Chinese embassy in Washington, DC.  "I am not familiar with what you mentioned," they responded, adding that China "opposes vilifying China by peddling the so-called 'Chinese spy' narrative."

Suspected Chinese Agents Are Trying to Dupe Americans with Fake Listings on Job Platforms (cnn.com) 13

(And they also told CNN that LinkedIn remains "focused on detecting state-sponsored abuse and will continue to enforce our policies against fake accounts.")  But it's not just happening on LinkedIn...

====================================================================================================

S&P considers Nvidia credit downgrade over governance and ‘key man’ risks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:44:32 UTC
07:44:32 KST

S&P Global Ratings, one of the three major credit rating agencies, is considering downgrading Nvidia’s rating, citing concerns about the company’s management and governance practices.
Despite Nvidia’s strong financial performance, the ouster and reinstatement of CEO Jensen Huang has raised concerns about “weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning” and “key man risk,” meaning heavy reliance on Huang for its future success, S&P said in a statement.
The agency expects to finish its review within 90 days and could cut Nvidia’s rating from AA to AA-, the fourth-highest on its scale.
Although Nvidia is certain to keep its investment-grade rating, a downgrade could raise its borrowing costs. Because Nvidia backstops neoclouds and other infrastructure companies that lack strong credit on their own, a lower rating could make their debt costlier or force them to post more collateral.
In August, Nvidia announced plans to arrange $500 billion in financing for AI infrastructure with six financial institutions, including Goldman Sachs, Apollo, and Blackstone. Under the original terms, Nvidia would guarantee 25% of the loans. But even before Huang’s ouster and return, lenders had been demanding that Nvidia guarantee more, Bloomberg reported.
A downgrade would likely prompt lenders to demand even larger guarantees.

This Spread Is So “Delicious,” I’m Eating It for Breakfast AND Dessert
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:30:00 UTC
07:30:00 KST

This Spread Is So “Delicious,” I’m Eating It for Breakfast AND Dessert
Nut butter is one of my major food groups. If I don’t have a jar in my pantry, all is wrong in the world. I’d recently tried a new-to-me jar of chocolate almond butter (it tastes like fudgy brownie batter!), and was on the hunt for my next nutty spread. Enter: Big Spoon Roasters.
The small-batch nut butters promise big flavors (think: Lum Lum Thai Curry, Bourbon Pecan, and even a Wag Butter for dogs). To get a tiny taste of what Big Spoon’s working with, I sampled the winter mix variety pack (which is actually available year-round). It contains four mini (3-ounce) jars of seasonal hits: Vanilla Caramel, Chai Spice, Chocolate Sea Salt, and Carrot Cake.
I love options! And after tasting my way through the mini jars, there’s one I can’t wait to reorder — in a much larger size.
What You Should Know About Big Spoon Roasters’ Carrot Cake Almond & Walnut Butter
Big Spoon Roasters was co-founded in 2011 by two nut butter lovers, Mark and Megan, who originally sold their products at bicycle races and farmers markets. The company’s mission is rooted in creating food that’s good for people and the planet. To do so, Big Spoon Roasters sources high-quality ingredients from sustainable farmers that share their values, pays workers fair wages, and touts a “nearly waste-free” production process that includes the use of recycled paper labels and cruelty-free cleaning products.
Each jar (or bar) is roasted, toasted, measured, and mixed by hand, according to Big Spoon Roasters’ website, and is gluten-, soy-, GMO-, refined sugar-, and palm-oil free. The products are also vegan and contain bee-friendly almonds.
Big Spoon Roasters’ Carrot Cake Almond & Walnut Butter first launched in April 2020 as a seasonal item, and has since become a year-round staple. Heirloom mission almonds, English walnuts, organic maple syrup, cinnamon, nutmeg, allspice, and Jacobsen sea salt are ground together to replicate the flavor of the spiced layer cake.
It comes in two sizes — a mini 3-ounce jar ($7.75) and larger 13-ounce jar ($15.99) — which you can find on Big Spoon Roasters’ website or Amazon, and in select retailers across the country, including Eataly, Bi-Rite, Erewhon, and New Leaf Community Markets.
My Honest Review of Big Spoon Roasters’ Carrot Cake Almond & Walnut Butter
A good carrot cake has just the right balance of spice, and is sweet but not overpoweringly so, with a thick layer of rich cream cheese frosting on top. It’s hard to imagine packing all that flavor into a jar, but this nut butter 1000% did. It truly is DELICIOUS.
The warming spices — the cinnamon, nutmeg, and allspice — are prominent, and the almonds and walnuts provide the perfect nutty backdrop for them to shine. The maple syrup adds a hint of sweetness. It’s everything I love about carrot cake (without any actual carrots)!
And the texture! It’s a nice mix of smooth and chunky — there are little pieces of almond and walnut throughout, but it still lusciously coats your tongue.
I drizzled some over maple yogurt with homemade granola, which made for the ultimate fall snack. I’ve also made carrot cake oatmeal before, and a spoonful of this on top would be heaven.
The only downside was how small the mini jar is (the other flavors were delicious too, by the way). I’m already making room in my pantry for a bigger one and planning on what flavors to try next.
Buy: Big Spoon Roasters Carrot Cake Almond & Walnut Butter, $15.99 for 13 ounces
Have you tried Big Spoon Roasters’ nut butters? Let us know in the comments below.

Bare Metal Qwen
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:15:34 UTC
07:15:34 KST

A standalone bootable x86-64 operating system that runs Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B directly on hardware or KVM with no Linux, no Windows, no C, no Rust, and no libc.
- 24,252 bytes stripped static Multiboot 1 kernel (FAST_Q8=1, default: .text = 17,143 B, .rodata = 2,772 B, .bss = 2.1 MiB NOBITS, zero dynamic dependencies), or 23,612 bytes (FAST_Q8=0, legacy Q8_0xFP32).
- 74–94 ms cold boot to interactive asm-llm> multi-turn chat with persistent KV-cache across turns, all SMP cores online, and the .asmbin v2 (614 MiB FP16 scales) or v1 (649 MiB FP32 scales) model bound zero-copy from physical RAM on 2 MiB huge pages.
- 100% token-exact and byte-for-byte parity with Phase 1 across 1..32 vCPUs.
- 134.9 tok/s decode on QEMU KVM (AMD EPYC 7642, 16 vCPUs; 23.7 tok/s at 1 vCPU, 118.1 tok/s at 8 vCPUs, 136.6 tok/s peak) and 55.4 tok/s live on a 0 GB disk Proxmox VE VM (Intel Xeon E5-2670 v3, 12 cores).
Running live on a diskless (0 GB storage) Proxmox VE VM booted from the hybrid BIOS+UEFI ISO (Intel Xeon E5-2670 v3, 12 cores, 2 GiB RAM):
- x86-64 CPU with AVX2 + FMA + F16C.
- Core build & QEMU KVM:
sudo apt install nasm binutils qemu-system-x86
- Optional, for building a bootable BIOS+UEFI hybrid ISO (make os-iso): sudo apt install grub-pc-bin grub-efi-amd64-bin grub-common mtools xorriso
- An exported models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin file (--scale-dtype fp16 v2 recommended, or legacy v1 --scale-dtype fp32; see Phase 1 setup).
From the repository root (FAST_Q8=1 is enabled by default; pass FAST_Q8=0 for legacy FP32-activation mode):
make os FAST_Q8=1
make os-check
Expected output (FAST_Q8=1):
-rwxr-xr-x 1 user user 24252 build/asm-llm-os.elf
build/asm-llm-os.elf: ELF 32-bit LSB executable, Intel 80386, version 1 (SYSV), statically linked, stripped
There is no dynamic section in this file.
(The kernel is linked as 64-bit ELF and wrapped into ELF32 via objcopy -O elf32-i386 for standard Multiboot 1 loaders like QEMU -kernel and GRUB2; boot.asm transitions immediately into 64-bit Long Mode.)
Boot directly in the terminal over COM1 serial (-nographic):
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -kernel build/asm-llm-os.elf \
  -initrd models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin \
  -nographic
Or use the Makefile shortcut:
make os-run MODEL=models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin SMP=16
Run a one-shot batch prompt with clean ACPI shutdown:
make os-run MODEL=models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin SMP=16 \
  CMDLINE='prompt="Explain how a CPU cache works in 3 concise sentences." max_tokens=64 think=0 shutdown=1'
Open a live 80x25 VGA GUI window with PS/2 keyboard input:
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -kernel build/asm-llm-os.elf \
  -initrd models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin \
  -vga std -serial stdio
Packages the 23 KB kernel, GRUB2 Multiboot 1 config, and the 649 MiB .asmbin model into a single self-contained ~661 MiB bootable ISO (build/asm-llm-os.iso):
make os-iso MODEL=models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin
Boot the ISO in QEMU KVM (SeaBIOS or UEFI OVMF):
# Legacy BIOS (SeaBIOS)
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -cdrom build/asm-llm-os.iso -nographic
# UEFI (OVMF)
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -bios /usr/share/ovmf/OVMF.fd \
  -cdrom build/asm-llm-os.iso -nographic
Because the entire OS and model live inside the ISO and run out of RAM, the VM needs zero hard disks.
- Upload build/asm-llm-os.iso to your Proxmox node's ISO storage (local -> ISO Images -> Upload, or scp into /var/lib/vz/template/iso/asm-llm-os.iso).
- Create and start a diskless VM on the Proxmox host (pick an unused <vmid>, e.g. 250):
qm create 250 \
  --name asm-llm-os \
  --machine pc \
  --bios seabios \
  --cpu host \
  --cores 12 \
  --memory 2048 \
  --balloon 0 \
  --vga std \
  --serial0 socket \
  --ide2 local:iso/asm-llm-os.iso,media=cdrom \
  --boot order=ide2
qm start 250
- Interact either through the Proxmox web UI (noVNC Console, VGA + PS/2 keyboard) or over the virtual serial port:
qm terminal 250
(Note: --cpu host is required so the VM exposes AVX2 + FMA, and --balloon 0 ensures 2 GiB of contiguous guest RAM for the 649 MiB model + 449 MiB KV-cache arena.)
By default, the bare-metal console runs in multi-turn chat mode with persistent KV-cache: each new user turn prefills only the new Qwen3 chat tokens starting at g_chat_pos without recomputing earlier turns, closes every assistant turn in the KV-cache with <|im_end|>\n, and displays live context usage (ctx=X/2048) in the decode stats line. When the KV-cache approaches max_seq (pos + n_prompt + 64 >= max_seq), it prints [chat] context full, resetting KV-cache and resets cleanly. If booted with prompt="..." and shutdown=0, the initial batch turn stays in the KV-cache so you can continue the same conversation in the REPL.
Both PS/2 keyboard (VGA) and COM1 serial (115200 8N1) are polled simultaneously. Type any message and press Enter, or use a built-in command:
Pass via QEMU -append "...", make os-run CMDLINE='...', or make os-iso CMDLINE='...':
Phase 2 compiles the exact same shared/math.asm, shared/matmul.asm, shared/tokenizer.asm, and shared/transformer.asm with -DBARE_METAL -DFAST_Q8=$(FAST_Q8) and pairs them with 4,167 lines of bare-metal OS code:
- Boot & Long Mode (boot.asm, linker.ld): verifies Multiboot 1 magic (0x2BADB002), zeroes .bss, builds 4-level 2 MiB huge-page identity tables covering 0..4 GiB (with 0xFE000000..0xFFFFFFFF marked Strong Uncacheable for Local APIC MMIO), enters 64-bit Long Mode, installs a 32-entry exception IDT, verifies XSAVE + AVX + AVX2 + FMA + F16C via CPUID, and initializes CR0/CR4/XCR0=7/MXCSR.
- Zero-Copy Weights & Physical Arena (runtime.asm): binds the .asmbin (v2 FP16 scales or v1 FP32 scales) Multiboot module directly at its physical address (validating "ASML" magic) and carves the 449 MiB KV-cache + scratch arena from the largest usable Multiboot memory-map region, zeroing it with AVX2.
- Bare-Metal SMP (smp.asm): parses ACPI RSDP -> RSDT/XSDT -> MADT ("APIC") with a Local APIC broadcast fallback, installs a position-independent 16->32->64-bit trampoline at 0x8000, wakes all AP cores via Local APIC INIT-SIPI-SIPI, enables AVX2/FMA/MXCSR identically on every core, and dispatches both matmul_q8_0 and gqa_attn_range (FAST_Q8=1) with a lock-free cache-line-isolated spin-barrier.
- Hardware Drivers (drivers.asm):
  - COM1 Serial (0x3F8, 115200 8N1): raw UTF-8 TX and RX.
  - 80x25 Color VGA (0xB8000): colored boot banner, hardware scrolling, batched CRTC cursor updates, and UTF-8 decoding with Cyrillic-to-ASCII fallback on VGA while streaming raw UTF-8 to COM1.
  - PS/2 Keyboard (0x60/0x64): scancode set 1 to ASCII (Shift, Backspace, Enter).
  - PIT Channel 2 + RDTSC: 20 ms hardware PIT calibration for accurate nanosecond boot/prefill/decode profiling.
  - ACPI Poweroff: QEMU/Bochs ACPI S5 ports (0x604, 0xB004, 0x4004) plus isa-debug-exit fallback.
python3 scripts/verify_os.py --model models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin --run-dir runs/os-verify
All 5 parity prompts (English technical, English concise, Russian UTF-8, arithmetic word problem, --think mode) match Phase 1 (build/asm-llm) 100% exact in prompt tokens, generated tokens, and raw UTF-8 bytes.
KVM SMP Scaling (FAST_Q8=1, .asmbin v2 FP16 Scales, AMD EPYC 7642, 34 prompt tok, 64 gen tok, greedy)
(Because boot.asm identity-maps guest RAM with 2 MiB huge pages, the bare-metal kernel avoids 4 KiB page-table walks across the 614 MiB model and outperforms both Linux mmap() Phase 1 and llama.cpp CPU Q8_0 at every thread count.)

  - ACPI Poweroff: QEMU/Bochs ACPI S5 ports (0x604, 0xB004, 0x4004) plus isa-debug-exit fallback.
python3 scripts/verify_os.py --model models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin --run-dir runs/os-verify
All 5 parity prompts (English technical, English concise, Russian UTF-8, arithmetic word problem, --think mode) match Phase 1 (build/asm-llm) 100% exact in prompt tokens, generated tokens, and raw UTF-8 bytes.
KVM SMP Scaling (FAST_Q8=1, .asmbin v2 FP16 Scales, AMD EPYC 7642, 34 prompt tok, 64 gen tok, greedy)
(Because boot.asm identity-maps guest RAM with 2 MiB huge pages, the bare-metal kernel avoids 4 KiB page-table walks across the 614 MiB model and outperforms both Linux mmap() Phase 1 and llama.cpp CPU Q8_0 at every thread count.)

====================================================================================================

Deploying a payphone on a tiny Pacific Island
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:12:25 UTC
07:12:25 KST

For the past few years Australia’s Telstra has run a campaign of “Free calls to Santa” from the universal service obligation funded payphones across the country – It’s a really fun idea and we wanted to replicate it on our network on Norfolk Island where we run the mobile network, but the’s no payphones.*
* There is now a payphone – You probably already guessed this.
Getting a Payphone to a small island in the South Pacific
Step 1 was getting a payphone.
Those who have seen my office / shed / studio / the HackHouse, know that I may have one or two payphones… But this needed a payphone that could stand the elements and be modern ish.
The answer, is of course, to use the sexiest payphone of all time; the Nortel Millennium. Featuring VFD display, durable keypad, card service, coin service, no fancy metering line required.
Unfortunately Nortel’s bankruptcy in 2008 meant I couldn’t phone up an account manager, but with the hookup of some guy with a U-Haul van full of Millennium’s in the US and a bit of a road trip, Cody from our team managed to pickup some really clean GTE branded payphones for the job.
Then Cody flew to Sydney where we drove to the house of someone who was heading to Norfolk the next day to explain they’d need to leave all their baggage at home as all of their baggage allowance was to be taken up with the weight of a payphone – Thanks Paul.
Programming
Eventually with the Payphone on Norfolk, we needed to get it programmed; for that there’s Howard Harte’s great mm_manager, which, with the aid of a USB modem is able to program the phones.
Sidebar: I also got this working with a softmodem (A fork of D-Modem with some minor tweaks I’ve documented here), so we could do it via straight SIP without the USB modem required – but the USB modem was the easier path.
The Philtel blog post covers how to setup your Millennium if you’re looking to play along at home yourself.
I hit a bit of a stumbling block with mm_manager as the software assumed you were part of the North American Numbering Plan (NANP) – as one of the literally dozens of people who live outside of America, having to put a 1 in front of the number wasn’t going to fly.
After some trial and error I sussed out what bits on the config files that get pushed to the phone controls this and submitted a PR to mm_manager which is now merged.
We’re also able to set the messaging that appears on the screen, which I’m very happy with the branding on.
We set it for free-on-net calls, so anyone can call any Norfone number without charge and the Payphone tag number.
Talking to Santa
Once this would’ve been the hard part, I did some work a long time ago with IBM Watson and built some stuff to annoy voters via phone with automated phone calls and capture their human responses and sort them and play the appropriate response. But tech has come a long way since 2019, and this was the easy part of the project.
We setup the payphone to make HOHOHO (464646) a destination that did not require coins when we did the rating table setup when programming the phone.
The ATA the payphone is connected to just comes through to our IMS like any other fixed line subscriber; the TAS is setup to zero-rate the destination and then it’s dialplan forwards it to “Santa Bridge” listening for SIP on port 5090.
I’ve got 4 pre-recorded “Norfolk Specific” greetings to buy us time to get everything setup in the background, once these greetings play (each ends asking the caller their name) then we feed into the OpenAI API.
One of the members from our team attempted to ask Santa to write a Python script to iterate over a for loop, while another team member asked Santa for firearms. In both cases it did a good job of redirecting.
All the code for this is on Github.
Branding
The phone came GTE branded, so we needed to redo the card with the branding on it that goes around the screen. Two screws and some wiggling later we pulled out the card, and then I badgered my partner into using their day off to design a new one, with a retro style I really like.
Installation
The install was beautifully completed by long-time-reader-of-the-blog Minas from Shield Electrical and Air.
He knocked up a housing to Shield it from the rain and elements and installed it outside the office entrance.
Getting it on Payphone Tag
The last step of the puzzle was making the phone so it could be captured by cult game Payphone Tag – I reached out to Alex and gave him the info, and now it’s there in the map for anyone to capture.
Future payphone adventures
So with this now in place, we’ve still got to give it some tinsel and Christmas cheer, and I’ll get some more pics of it in place.
We’re waiting for someone to claim it on Payphone tag, and we’ve shipped 3 of these to another even more remote Island I’ll hopefully be able to share more info on later.
If you’re ever on Norfolk, give it a call.

====================================================================================================

Anthropic, OpenAI Employees Fear Models Are Too Unsafe to Ever Release
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:00:00 UTC
07:00:00 KST

Anthropic, OpenAI Employees Fear Models Are Too Unsafe to Ever Release (axios.com) 66
			
		 	
				Axios reports:
Anthropic and OpenAI are facing an increasingly urgent problem: Internal, unreleased models are becoming so powerful that many employees no longer believe they can be safely released, according to more than two dozen sources familiar with the internal debates...
The challenge could significantly slow the AI boom, as the gap between what the best AI systems can do and humans' ability to control them continues to widen, sources told Axios. 
Researchers haven't found technical solutions for controlling the most advanced models. Some insiders believe it's fixable. Others aren't convinced and say companies should stop training new models entirely... Anthropic and OpenAI have unreleased models that are far more powerful than what they've already released publicly, but the AI companies have struggled to ensure that they are safe enough for public release, sources said.  This has led to debate within Anthropic, OpenAI and the AI research community about whether to stop releasing future frontier models publicly, instead reserving them for internal deployment only. 
There has also been debate around whether to train new models at all, sources inside and outside those companies told Axios.

S&amp;P Warns Nvidia's Board May Be Compromised
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:00:00 UTC
07:00:00 KST

S&P Warns Nvidia's Board May Be Compromised (usnews.com)
			
		 	
				S&P Global Ratings has placed Nvidia's AA credit rating on downgrade watch, warning that Jensen Huang's departure and subsequent reinstatement as CEO exposed weaknesses in the company's corporate governance and board oversight. "We believe the long tenure of certain board members may have compromised their independence. The company's corporate governance practices may also not have kept pace with Nvidia's rapid growth and its role as a critical global technology enabler," the ratings agency said. Reuters reports:  Nvidia currently has an 'AA' rating from S&P, which indicates a very strong capacity to meet financial commitments. The highest rating is 'AAA'. The agency said it could downgrade Nvidia within the next 90 days, but expects any potential downgrade to be limited to one notch, unless investigations and other disclosures harm the company's reputation or future business prospects. Meanwhile, Moody's said on Wednesday that bringing back Huang eases near-term uncertainty around Nvidia's business execution and eliminates execution risk associated with appointing a new leader for the chipmaker. The agency said Nvidia's Aa1 rating and stable outlook remain unaffected, but added that governance and regulatory uncertainty persists.

Google’s patent for custom AI video feeds paints a bleak picture of entertainment’s future
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
21:51:26 UTC
06:51:26 KST

Last year, Google filed a patent for a system called My Channel that’s designed to create feeds filled with personalized, AI-generated videos and advertisements based on information the company knows about its users. The patent has been rejected, but unfortunately, Google might appeal or resubmit an amended version, and we could see this monstrosity come to market.
Google’s patent for custom AI video feeds paints a bleak picture of entertainment’s future
Imagine an endless feed of AI slop in which you are the main character of every story.
First spotted by Patentlyze, the patent — which was filed last May and published last week — describes how My Channel would use a person’s demographic data and viewing history to create its own prompts that would then be fed into an LLM to help generate AI videos tailored to that person’s specific interests. In addition to factoring in what kinds of videos a user tends to watch, the system would capture contextual data from their device like IP addresses, GPS locations, and specific details about the user’s age. My Channel could also base its prompts on current news like sports scores and things happening on social media, and all of the video generation would take place on Google’s servers.
According to the patent, My Channel would be able to churn out entire movies of various genres. If a user watches a lot of videos about Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey, the system might serve up something like Ash Koosha’s Odysseus: The Fall. The patent details how My Channel might be able to connect to other streaming services — which would give it even more information about users and, presumably, access to useful training data for video generation. It all seems like a convoluted way to whip up AI knockoffs of professionally produced art, but what’s most unsettling about the patent are its mentions of My Channel creating digital avatars of users and turning them into “the protagonist or lead character” in the content it pumps out.
Setting aside the fact that My Channel would use photos, videos, and audio clips of people to create these avatars, the system sounds like it is designed to generate a form of “entertainment” that is deeply at odds with what makes engaging with art a worthwhile, enjoyable experience. It can be fun to metaphorically project one’s self into fictional narratives to understand how characters feel, but what Google is proposing is giving people access to an endless supply of bespoke content that doesn’t at all encourage them to use their imaginations to empathize with or relate to the characters. The patent makes it seem like Google thinks that viewers would be entertained enough just by seeing themselves onscreen. That’s bleak in and of itself because My Channel sounds very much like an unholy cross between JibJab and the panoptic elements of Google.
My Channel feels like an even more depressing idea when you consider how the service would make it that much easier for its users to avoid broadening their horizons by being exposed to new things that exist outside their comfort zones. If Google were able to strike the right licensing deals with other streaming services, My Channel users could presumably gorge themselves on artless AI regurgitations of preexisting content. That might keep some people glued to the service — a goal that every streaming platform strives for — but it would also discourage them from engaging with entertainment that isn’t expressly crafted to appeal to their assumed tastes.
When people refer to AI-generated videos as “slop,” they’re talking about the fact that generative models are incapable of generating art with the same kind of creative intention that makes human-made stories compelling. Video generation models can mimic visual elements of the data (read: movies and series) they’re trained on, but they cannot truly think or feel in the way that directors, writers, and actors can. This reality is why it has been so baffling to see AI-boosting entertainment companies, streamers, and studios insisting that the technology will fundamentally revolutionize the industry for the better. It feels much more likely that gen AI is going to keep flooding the internet with garbage no one wants to watch.

Much shorter version of GRH Lean verification from Anthropic
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
21:48:10 UTC
06:48:10 KST

Research artifact. Not maintained and not accepting contributions.
A Lean 4 proof of the Riemann Hypothesis and of the Generalized Riemann
Hypothesis for Dirichlet L-functions, built on Mathlib and using only the
axioms propext, Classical.choice and Quot.sound.
The proof is based on Levent Alpöge's, with Claude (https://alpo.ge/remark.pdf). This repository is a shorter version, produced in collaboration with my colleagues at Anthropic, and Claude.
- grh/: readable edition, about 300,000 lines. Start at grh/GUIDE.md.
- grh-compact/: the same five theorems, about 76,000 lines.
Each folder is its own Lake project with its own README.
Needs Lean v4.35.0 and Mathlib at commit 56a00585. In either folder:
lake exe cache get
lake build
lake env lean scripts/PrintAxioms.lean
A build takes one to two hours on 12 cores with 24 GB of memory (about 85 minutes more if the Mathlib cache is missing).
The proof has passed machine checks only, listed in each folder's README. No mathematician has read it through, and no paper comes with it.
Apache 2.0, Copyright 2026 Anthropic, PBC. See LICENSE, and NOTICE and
ATTRIBUTION.md in each folder.
