World Has Six Consecutive Days 2C Above Preindustrial Levels For First Time
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

World Has Six Consecutive Days 2C Above Preindustrial Levels For First Time (theguardian.com) 13
			
		 	
				Longtime Slashdot reader AmiMoJo shares a report from The Guardian: For the first time, the world has experienced six consecutive days of temperatures 2C warmer than preindustrial levels, the Guardian has learned. This record hot streak, which is continuing, is too brief to signify that the upper range of the Paris climate agreement target has been breached, but scientists said it was an early indication of what is to come unless fossil fuel emissions are rapidly eliminated. It is also likely to feed into growing scientific concerns that global heating may be accelerating. The daily global surface temperature rose to its highest ever point above preindustrial levels, 2.24C, on Monday, the most recent day for which data is available, and is forecast to persist at similarly high levels for at least several more days. "This will amplify the buildup of CO2 in the atmosphere driven by fossil fuel burning," said Richard Betts, the head of climate impacts research at the Met Office Hadley Centre. "The CO2 increase from this year to next will be about 3.5 parts per million, a third faster than in recent years and nearly twice as fast as what it should have been limited to if we'd been on track to limit global warming to 1.5C."
Zeke Hausfather, a climate researcher at Berkeley Earth, said: "Another day, another global temperature record," adding that the previous record of 2.05C over the 1850-1900 baseline, set in 2023, has been "smashed."

====================================================================================================

Defang Security Incident
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
21:47:16 UTC
06:47:16 KST

On November 12, 2026 an attacker copied the credentials we use to run Defang and downloaded a copy of our main database. Here is what happened, what was exposed, what we have done, and what you should do.
On November 12, 2026 an attacker got into the Defang Deploy build system and copied the credentials we use to run Defang. With those they downloaded a copy of our main database. We confirmed this on November 23, 2026. If you have a Defang Portal account, your account is in that database, and this post explains what was taken and what you should do. We are also writing to every affected user by email.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App, some of those credentials could also be used against your GitHub repositories and your cloud account. We have replaced them, but there are checks only you can do. They are listed under "What you should do" below. Please go through them as soon as you can.
What happened
We used a separate GitHub App for local development and testing. It was installed on two of our own repositories and had more permissions than it needed, including permission to change deployment environments. Acting as that app, the attacker pushed a commit to one of those repositories and tagged it as a release. Our production environment trusted release tags, so their code ran in our CI with access to our production credentials, and the attacker copied them.
The activity we can see ran from about 06:00 to 10:30 UTC on November 12. The credentials stayed valid until we replaced them on November 23. We are still checking whether they were used after November 12. Some of our logs are not detailed enough to rule that out, so we will update this post when we know more.
Timeline
All times are UTC.
- November 12, 06:05: the first workflow that copied build credentials ran, against our development and staging environments.
- November 12, 06:26: our production credentials were copied.
- November 12, 07:57 to 08:10: the database was copied.
- November 15: we were made aware of a security issue with a dependency and installed the update.
- November 23: a routine security scan found suspicious activity in our repositories.
- November 23: we confirmed the database copy from our access logs, replaced our production credentials and emailed every affected user.
- November 24: we published this post.
What the attacker could see
- Your name and email address. This is the address only: the attacker did not get into your email account or see any of your messages.
- Your GitHub or GitLab username, and the names of the organizations that account belongs to
- The answers you gave when you signed up
- If you installed the Defang GitHub App: the names of the repositories it can access, including private ones
- The cloud accounts you connected to Defang (account, project or subscription IDs and regions; no credentials)
- The Compose files you deployed or generated with Defang
- Copies of deployment state from deploys between October 12 and November 19. These can include database passwords and any environment variable built from a defang config value, such as a database URL that contains a password.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App: what the attacker could use
The attacker could use the Defang GitHub App's key, and the GitHub sign-in token we keep when you connect GitHub. The token was stored encrypted, but the encryption key was taken too. Until we replaced them on November 23, anyone holding these could do in your repositories what the app is allowed to do:
- push commits and branches
- change workflow files and start workflow runs
- overwrite Actions secrets and variables
- change deployment environments and their protection rules
- open and merge pull requests
- change repository settings, including branch protection, deploy keys and collaborators
A workflow run started this way can receive your cloud credentials, because the role Defang set up for GitHub deploys trusts workflows in your repository.
If you have billing details with us
The copied credentials included a key for our account at Stripe, our payment provider. Until we replaced it on November 23, someone holding that key could read the record Stripe keeps for you:
- your name and email address
- your billing address, or the parts of it you gave us, and your tax ID if you entered one
- the brand, last four digits and expiry date of your card
- your subscription and invoice history
Your full card number and security code were not exposed. Stripe holds those and does not make them available to Defang or to anyone using our key.
What was not exposed
- Passwords. Defang has none for you: you sign in with GitHub, GitLab or an emailed code.
- Your cloud credentials. We never hold them. Your BYOC deployments run in your own account with your own credentials, and Defang's servers have no direct access to that account or to anything running in it. The one indirect path is the GitHub Actions deploy role described above.
- The contents of your repositories. They are not stored in our database.
- Config values you set with defang config. They stay in your cloud account, except for the copies in deployment state described above.
What we have done
- Replaced the Defang GitHub App key and secrets on November 23, and revoked the GitHub sign-in tokens we held.
- Replaced our other production credentials, including database, payment and email credentials, on November 23.
- Signed out every session and invalidated every Defang access token issued before November 23.
- Stopped keeping copies of deployment state on November 19 and deleted the copies we had.
- Cancelled every pending cloud invite link. If you were inviting someone, send a new invite.
- Notified GitHub, who are helping us audit for token misuse.
- Installed the security update on November 15.
- Revoked the development GitHub App's credentials and suspended the app on November 23.
- Reverted the production and staging deployment configurations that were altered by the attacker.
- Notified users and relevant authorities of the data breach.
What you should do
Everyone
- Be careful with email that claims to come from Defang, GitHub or GitLab, especially anything that asks you to sign in, approve an app or share a code. Someone holding this data can write a convincing message that uses your real name and username. We will never ask you for a password or a token by email.
- You will need to sign in to Defang again.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App: in GitHub
- Check your connected repositories for activity you don't recognize since November 11:
  - new commits, branches or tags
  - changes under .github/workflows
  - workflow runs you didn't start
  - changed Actions secrets or variables
  - new deploy keys or collaborators, and changed branch protection rules
- Look in particular at anything from defang-app[bot]. Your security log is at github.com/settings/security-log; organizations have an audit log under Settings.
- Lock down the deployment environments Defang created. In each connected repository, open Settings, then Environments. For every environment whose name starts with defang-, set "Deployment branches and tags" to your deploy branch only. You can also add required reviewers. This stops a workflow on any other branch from receiving your cloud credentials.
- Revoke and reconnect Defang's access to your GitHub account. In GitHub, go to Settings, then Applications, then Authorized GitHub Apps, and revoke Defang. Sign in to Defang again afterwards.
- If you no longer deploy from GitHub with Defang, uninstall the Defang app from your account or organization.
If you deploy from GitHub Actions: in your cloud account
- Check who has used your deploy role since November 11. The role or identity Defang set up for GitHub deploys has administrator access to your account. A token from a branch or workflow you don't deploy from is a sign of misuse.
  - On AWS this is usually defang-cd-CIRole. Search CloudTrail for AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity calls on it.
  - On Google Cloud, check the workload identity pool's token exchanges.
  - On Azure, check the sign-in logs for the managed identity.
- Narrow that role's trust to the branch or environment you deploy from, if it currently allows any branch.
If you deployed with Defang between October 12 and November 19
- Change your database passwords and any secret that was part of another environment variable, then redeploy. Use defang config set <NAME> for each one.
- If your Compose file has secrets written directly in it, change those too.
If you have billing details with us
You do not need to replace your card. The exposed details are not enough to charge it. Watch your statements as you normally would, and be wary of email or calls that quote your last four digits or billing address to appear legitimate.
If any of these checks turns up something you don't recognize
Rotate the secrets in that repository's Actions secrets and environments and the cloud credentials its workflows can reach, then tell us.
Frequently asked questions
Was my GitHub password exposed? Do I need to change it right away?
No. Defang never had your GitHub password. You sign in with GitHub, GitLab or an emailed code, so there is no password for us to lose.
What the attacker could use was the Defang GitHub App's key and the GitHub sign-in token we keep when you connect GitHub. We replaced the key and revoked those tokens on November 23. Changing your GitHub password is not required, but it does no harm, and turning on two-factor authentication is a good idea in any case. The checks that matter are the ones under "If you installed the Defang GitHub App: in GitHub" above: look for activity you don't recognize, lock down deployment environments, and revoke the Defang GitHub App.
Did they take only my information from GitHub, or did they go further? Did they get my email?
They got your email address, but not your email account. The address we have on file for you is in our database, so it was copied. Defang has no access to your mailbox, so the attacker could not read your email or sign in to your email account through us.
From GitHub, what we hold is your username, the names of the organizations your account belongs to and, if you installed the Defang GitHub App, the names of the repositories it can access. The contents of your repositories are not in our database.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App, the attacker also held the credentials that let them act in your repositories until we replaced them on November 23. We are still checking whether they were used after November 12, and we will update this post when we know more. That is why we ask you to review your repositories and your cloud account. "What the attacker could see" and "What the attacker could use" above list everything.
Did this happen only to me, or to all users?
Anyone with a Defang Portal account is in the database that was copied, so your email address, and potentially your name, was exposed along with everyone else's. Beyond that, what was exposed depends on how you use Defang. See above section for details.
How can I protect my data? How do I check that an email really comes from you?
- Don't sign in, approve an app or share a code because an email asked you to. Open the Defang Portal, GitHub or GitLab yourself by typing the address in your browser, rather than following a link in a message.
- We will never ask you for a password or a token by email.
- Someone holding this data can quote your real name, username or the organizations you belong to. A message that knows those details is not proof that it comes from us.
- If you are unsure about a message, forward it to sec@defang.io and we will tell you whether we sent it.
- Work through the checklist in "What you should do" above. In short: review your repositories, review your cloud account, change database passwords and secrets if you deployed between October 12 and November 19, and turn on two-factor authentication on your GitHub and cloud accounts.
How do I delete my account and data?
Open Settings in the Defang Portal and delete your account there, or email sec@defang.io and we will do it for you.
Contact us
If you find anything suspicious, or want an engineer to go through your account, repositories or cloud account with you, write to sec@defang.io. We will update this post as we learn more.
The Defang Team

Defang Security Incident
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
21:47:16 UTC
06:47:16 KST

On November 12, 2026 an attacker copied the credentials we use to run Defang and downloaded a copy of our main database. Here is what happened, what was exposed, what we have done, and what you should do.
On November 12, 2026 an attacker got into the Defang Deploy build system and copied the credentials we use to run Defang. With those they downloaded a copy of our main database. We confirmed this on November 23, 2026. If you have a Defang Portal account, your account is in that database, and this post explains what was taken and what you should do. We are also writing to every affected user by email.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App, some of those credentials could also be used against your GitHub repositories and your cloud account. We have replaced them, but there are checks only you can do. They are listed under "What you should do" below. Please go through them as soon as you can.
What happened
We used a separate GitHub App for local development and testing. It was installed on two of our own repositories and had more permissions than it needed, including permission to change deployment environments. Acting as that app, the attacker pushed a commit to one of those repositories and tagged it as a release. Our production environment trusted release tags, so their code ran in our CI with access to our production credentials, and the attacker copied them.
The activity we can see ran from about 06:00 to 10:30 UTC on November 12. The credentials stayed valid until we replaced them on November 23. We are still checking whether they were used after November 12. Some of our logs are not detailed enough to rule that out, so we will update this post when we know more.
Timeline
All times are UTC.
- November 12, 06:05: the first workflow that copied build credentials ran, against our development and staging environments.
- November 12, 06:26: our production credentials were copied.
- November 12, 07:57 to 08:10: the database was copied.
- November 15: we were made aware of a security issue with a dependency and installed the update.
- November 23: a routine security scan found suspicious activity in our repositories.
- November 23: we confirmed the database copy from our access logs, replaced our production credentials and emailed every affected user.
- November 24: we published this post.
What the attacker could see
- Your name and email address. This is the address only: the attacker did not get into your email account or see any of your messages.
- Your GitHub or GitLab username, and the names of the organizations that account belongs to
- The answers you gave when you signed up
- If you installed the Defang GitHub App: the names of the repositories it can access, including private ones
- The cloud accounts you connected to Defang (account, project or subscription IDs and regions; no credentials)
- The Compose files you deployed or generated with Defang
- Copies of deployment state from deploys between October 12 and November 19. These can include database passwords and any environment variable built from a defang config value, such as a database URL that contains a password.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App: what the attacker could use
The attacker could use the Defang GitHub App's key, and the GitHub sign-in token we keep when you connect GitHub. The token was stored encrypted, but the encryption key was taken too. Until we replaced them on November 23, anyone holding these could do in your repositories what the app is allowed to do:
- push commits and branches
- change workflow files and start workflow runs
- overwrite Actions secrets and variables
- change deployment environments and their protection rules
- open and merge pull requests
- change repository settings, including branch protection, deploy keys and collaborators
A workflow run started this way can receive your cloud credentials, because the role Defang set up for GitHub deploys trusts workflows in your repository.
If you have billing details with us
The copied credentials included a key for our account at Stripe, our payment provider. Until we replaced it on November 23, someone holding that key could read the record Stripe keeps for you:
- your name and email address
- your billing address, or the parts of it you gave us, and your tax ID if you entered one
- the brand, last four digits and expiry date of your card
- your subscription and invoice history
Your full card number and security code were not exposed. Stripe holds those and does not make them available to Defang or to anyone using our key.
What was not exposed
- Passwords. Defang has none for you: you sign in with GitHub, GitLab or an emailed code.
- Your cloud credentials. We never hold them. Your BYOC deployments run in your own account with your own credentials, and Defang's servers have no direct access to that account or to anything running in it. The one indirect path is the GitHub Actions deploy role described above.
- The contents of your repositories. They are not stored in our database.
- Config values you set with defang config. They stay in your cloud account, except for the copies in deployment state described above.
What we have done
- Replaced the Defang GitHub App key and secrets on November 23, and revoked the GitHub sign-in tokens we held.
- Replaced our other production credentials, including database, payment and email credentials, on November 23.
- Signed out every session and invalidated every Defang access token issued before November 23.
- Stopped keeping copies of deployment state on November 19 and deleted the copies we had.
- Cancelled every pending cloud invite link. If you were inviting someone, send a new invite.
- Notified GitHub, who are helping us audit for token misuse.
- Installed the security update on November 15.
- Revoked the development GitHub App's credentials and suspended the app on November 23.
- Reverted the production and staging deployment configurations that were altered by the attacker.
- Notified users and relevant authorities of the data breach.
What you should do
Everyone
- Be careful with email that claims to come from Defang, GitHub or GitLab, especially anything that asks you to sign in, approve an app or share a code. Someone holding this data can write a convincing message that uses your real name and username. We will never ask you for a password or a token by email.
- You will need to sign in to Defang again.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App: in GitHub
- Check your connected repositories for activity you don't recognize since November 11:
  - new commits, branches or tags
  - changes under .github/workflows
  - workflow runs you didn't start
  - changed Actions secrets or variables
  - new deploy keys or collaborators, and changed branch protection rules
- Look in particular at anything from defang-app[bot]. Your security log is at github.com/settings/security-log; organizations have an audit log under Settings.
- Lock down the deployment environments Defang created. In each connected repository, open Settings, then Environments. For every environment whose name starts with defang-, set "Deployment branches and tags" to your deploy branch only. You can also add required reviewers. This stops a workflow on any other branch from receiving your cloud credentials.
- Revoke and reconnect Defang's access to your GitHub account. In GitHub, go to Settings, then Applications, then Authorized GitHub Apps, and revoke Defang. Sign in to Defang again afterwards.
- If you no longer deploy from GitHub with Defang, uninstall the Defang app from your account or organization.
If you deploy from GitHub Actions: in your cloud account
- Check who has used your deploy role since November 11. The role or identity Defang set up for GitHub deploys has administrator access to your account. A token from a branch or workflow you don't deploy from is a sign of misuse.
  - On AWS this is usually defang-cd-CIRole. Search CloudTrail for AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity calls on it.
  - On Google Cloud, check the workload identity pool's token exchanges.
  - On Azure, check the sign-in logs for the managed identity.
- Narrow that role's trust to the branch or environment you deploy from, if it currently allows any branch.
If you deployed with Defang between October 12 and November 19
- Change your database passwords and any secret that was part of another environment variable, then redeploy. Use defang config set <NAME> for each one.
- If your Compose file has secrets written directly in it, change those too.
If you have billing details with us
You do not need to replace your card. The exposed details are not enough to charge it. Watch your statements as you normally would, and be wary of email or calls that quote your last four digits or billing address to appear legitimate.
If any of these checks turns up something you don't recognize
Rotate the secrets in that repository's Actions secrets and environments and the cloud credentials its workflows can reach, then tell us.
Frequently asked questions
Was my GitHub password exposed? Do I need to change it right away?
No. Defang never had your GitHub password. You sign in with GitHub, GitLab or an emailed code, so there is no password for us to lose.
What the attacker could use was the Defang GitHub App's key and the GitHub sign-in token we keep when you connect GitHub. We replaced the key and revoked those tokens on November 23. Changing your GitHub password is not required, but it does no harm, and turning on two-factor authentication is a good idea in any case. The checks that matter are the ones under "If you installed the Defang GitHub App: in GitHub" above: look for activity you don't recognize, lock down deployment environments, and revoke the Defang GitHub App.

We are still checking whether they were used after November 12, and we will update this post when we know more. That is why we ask you to review your repositories and your cloud account. This Luxurious Hand Scrub Actually Removes Garlic Smells
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
21:30:00 UTC
06:30:00 KST

This Hand Soap Is the Only Thing I’ve Ever Tried That Removes the Smell of Garlic
Looking for more great deals? Browse our master list of current sales (updated daily!) and check out our Black Friday & Cyber Monday hub for all our latest coverage.
I’m a garlic-lover. When a recipe calls for two cloves of garlic, you can bet I’m using four. Garlic makes its way into what I’m cooking more often than not, so I am very familiar with its lingering smell on my fingers.
Garlic odor clings to your skin in a way that other kitchen odors do not. Washing my hands with my typical hand soap is fine to remove the fragrant grime from skinning salmon or even chopping onions (with a little elbow grease), but garlic is another story.
I’d just learned to live with it, chalking the persistent odor up to an unavoidable side effect of working in the kitchen. That is, until I tried Naomi After-Cooking Hand Scrub (which is 20% off right now for Black Friday!).
What Makes Naomi After-Cooking Hand Scrub So Great
When I first heard about this pricey, specialty soap I was skeptical. But after using it for a few months, I can confidently say it delivers on its promises. I have never used a product that so completely removes garlic odor (and other unwanted food smells) so easily and completely.
It’s gentle on skin.
I have sensitive skin that is prone to drying and cracking, so I choose what I wash my hands with carefully. As a food professional, I’m in the kitchen constantly for both work and life, so I wash my hands dozens of times a day. I worried that a scrub would be too harsh for my skin. Naomi’s scrub is both gentle and effective. Its ingredients include bread yeast enzymes, which help to break down food odors, and small jojoba wax beads, which are exfoliating but not harsh. Unlike my typical hand soap, I do not need to apply any extra effort or time when washing my hands to remove the scent of garlic. The soap really is doing all the work.
The scent isn’t overwhelming.
There are a few scent options (including unscented). I tested the bergamot and pepper scent, which is undeniably perfumey, but fades relatively quickly. As someone who doesn’t typically love a product with a strong scent, I found it pleasant and fresh-smelling. Importantly, the scent is not simply masking the odor of garlic, or any other food scent — it’s truly gone.
It makes a great gift (even for yourself).
Let’s be honest: At almost $30 a bottle, this is not your typical hand soap price point. But it’s also not the soap I reach for most often. I use my regular (less expensive) daily hand soap most of the time, reserving the Naomi scrub for when I specifically want to remove a strong cooking odor. Its efficacy justifies its cost, in my opinion, especially if you cook with as much garlic as I do. It also lasts a long time. I’ve been using it for almost three months and I’m not even halfway through the bottle.
All of that is to say, this is the perfect gift for the home cook in your life who you know wouldn’t indulge in a spendy luxury soap for themselves. Those are the best kinds of gifts, in my opinion — the ones you would love to have, but are reluctant to buy for yourself. I, for one, would be thrilled to find a bottle of Naomi in my stocking this year.
Buy: Naomi After-Cooking Hand Scrub, $21.60 (normally $27)
Why You Should Trust Me
I am a seasoned food editor who has been working in professional kitchens for almost 15 years. I spend the bulk of my life in the kitchen, washing my hands constantly in the process. In addition to working as a chef and recipe developer, I’m a writer who has been testing and reviewing kitchen products for years.

Saudi National Charged In OpenAI Bugging To Be Released On $1 Million Bond
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
21:00:00 UTC
06:00:00 KST

Saudi National Charged In OpenAI Bugging To Be Released On $1 Million Bond (cnbc.com) 13
			
		 	
				CNBC reports:
Abdulrahman Alfozan, the Saudi national accused of bugging an OpenAI executive conference room, will be allowed to be released from jail on a $1 million bond, a San Francisco federal court judge ruled Tuesday.   Alfozan, 35, is expected to be released on Wednesday and be fitted with an ankle monitor, according to his attorney, David Rizk...  [U.S. District Court Magistrate Judge Alex Tse] ruled in favor of a release motion by Rizk filed last week that said prosecutors had admitted they had "found no new evidence to support the theory that Mr. Alfozan was stealing trade secrets, or to support the speculation that he was acting as an unregistered foreign agent for Saudi Arabia."
The San Francisco U.S. Attorney's Office opposed the former OpenAI engineer's release, saying in its response that he remains a flight risk due to his "considerable resources and ties to Saudi Arabia."  Alfozan's release request "blithely dismisses the seriousness" of the charge he faces, and "appears to presume, without support, that the government's investigation is complete," prosecutors wrote... Prosecutors said they have found hundreds of audio files on Alfozan's personal computer, "including
dozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that occurred at OpenAI throughout September 2026, many of which involve discussions of what may be trade secrets."

FCC approves SpaceX plan to put AI data centers in space
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:56:44 UTC
05:56:44 KST

SpaceX was given conditional approval by the Federal Communications Commission on Wednesday to deploy and operate a constellation of up to 1 million satellites for its orbital data center business, a regulatory victory for Elon Musk and his bid to move AI data centers into space.
The FCC authorization was granted despite objections from Amazon, Viasat, and the American Astronomical Society, which flagged concerns over orbital debris and congestion, radiofrequency interference, and environmental consequences. Amazon, which is building a competitor to SpaceX’s Starlink service, argued in its petition that the application lacked basic details and described it as a “lofty ambition rather than a real plan.” Despite that lack of detail, Amazon also said the proposal “risks real harm.”
The FCC dismissed some of the key concerns raised by opponents, including potential threats to scientific research, because the agency determined they fell outside its regulatory authority.
FCC Chair Brendan Carr said in a statement that the action “opens a bold new frontier for the computing infrastructure needed to power America’s leadership and growth.”
Musk thanked Carr in a post on X, writing “Much appreciate. This will be a great asset to America and the world.”
This initial regulatory approval doesn’t mean orbital data centers are imminent.
SpaceX faces a number of technical and economic challenges, including how to dramatically reduce the cost of manufacturing and launching satellites, cool processors in space, and even how to secure financing for chips that are destined for orbit.
The company must also clear other regulatory hurdles. The FCC has not yet approved SpaceX’s plans to handle orbital debris for satellites that would operate above 600 kilometers, for instance. SpaceX requested a waiver of existing FCC rules, which requires satellites ending missions below 2,000 kilometers reenter Earth’s atmosphere within five years of completing those missions. The company is interested in moving some of these retired satellites into other orbits around Earth or even around the sun, according to the FCC authorization document.
SpaceX has said it plans to deploy the satellites, known as Starmind, at altitudes ranging from 500 to 1,002 kilometers. The FCC’s approval gives SpaceX the authority to use radiofrequency communications to maintain contact with and control its orbital data centers from Earth if their primary communications systems fail. The Starmind satellites would primarily communicate using lasers to connect with other satellites, including SpaceX’s Starlink.
The FCC has given SpaceX time to sort out these issues. Under the approval, SpaceX has seven years to get its first operational satellite into orbit and 14 years to deploy the entire constellation.

====================================================================================================

Slow Ubuntu security response times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:38:00 UTC
05:38:00 KST

Message-ID: <20261125172638.73c0a05b@hboeck.de>
Date: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 17:26:38 +0100
From: Hanno Böck <hanno@...eck.de>
To: oss-security@...ts.openwall.com
Subject: Slow Ubuntu security response times
Hello,
Lately, I'm observing that Ubuntu's response to severe security flaws
is concerning.
On November 16th, details about a remote memory corruption called
NoisyTCP (CVE-2026-99288) in the Linux kernel's TCP stack were
published: https://www.openwall.com/lists/oss-security/2026/11/15/2
Today, nine days later, Ubuntu still has not published a fix yet:
https://ubuntu.com/security/CVE-2026-99288
Other distros had a fix already available when the details were
published.
Likewise, a KVM host escape (CVE-2026-99312) affecting AMD
virtualization published on Nov 18th has no fix yet either [3]:
https://ubuntu.com/security/CVE-2026-99312
Ubuntu has been slow in patching another remote TCP corruption
(CVE-2026-98432) lately, where it took multiple weeks until a fix was
available. See also: https://chalmovsky.com/posts/wtf-ubuntu
Recent news indicates that this is might be exploited in the wild:
https://www.greynoise.io/blog/check-point-security-management-servers-intrusion-campaign
I think everyone understands that these times aren't easy for security
teams. Nevertheless, remote corruption vulnerabilities in the Linux
kernel are the kind of thing where I expect updates to be available
within hours, not days or weeks.
On a recent Ubuntu LTS system, the kernel version is 7.0.0-44. This
seems to indicate that Ubuntu is not relying on longterm supported
kernels from kernel.org, but is instead backporting patches to an old
kernel version. On a Debian stable system, the kernel version is
6.12.118+deb13, indicating that it's based on a very recent longterm
kernel from kernel.org. I would consider the backporting strategy for
the kernel to be hardly viable and probably everyone should base their
Linux kernels on longterm releases supported by the Linux kernel
developers.
-- 
Hanno Böck - Independent security researcher
https://itsec.hboeck.de/
https://badkeys.info/
Powered by blists - more mailing lists
Please check out the Open Source Software Security Wiki, which is counterpart to this mailing list.
Confused about mailing lists and their use? Read about mailing lists on Wikipedia and check out these guidelines on proper formatting of your messages.

Nfs2root: A 12-year-old pre-auth remote root in the Linux kernel's NFS server
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:35:50 UTC
05:35:50 KST

Port 2049 sits open on file servers, NAS appliances, build farms and Kubernetes storage nodes. Since 2014, the Linux kernel listening on it has let anyone who can reach it write into kernel memory before logging in. We pointed OpenSec's Antara at it. It came back with a root shell.
Pre-auth heap overflow in the Linux NFS server (nfsd)
Fixed in Linux 6.12.117, 6.18.61, 7.3.2 and 7.4-rc1. Update the kernel and reboot.
What you need to know
- What: a pre-auth heap overflow in the kernel's NFS server, nfsd.
- Who: any Linux host serving NFSv4.1 or 4.2 (the default) on a reachable tcp/2049.
- Impact: one TCP connection writes 61 bytes past a kernel heap object; chained, that gives a root shell.
- Now: patch and reboot. Until then, firewall tcp/2049.
Key facts
- Severity 9.8 Critical (AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N)
- Attack Remote, unauthenticated, tcp/2049
- Impact Kernel heap overflow → remote root shell
- Bug age 12 years, since Linux 3.16 (2014)
- Affected In-kernel nfsd with NFSv4.1 or 4.2
- Fixed in 6.12.117 · 6.18.61 · 7.3.2 · 7.4-rc1
One connection, no login
The NFS server runs inside the kernel and parses network input before it knows who you are. CVE-2026-99362 lives in that gap: a client can shrink its reply cache to zero bytes, then make the server copy a full reply into it. The copy lands on the kernel heap, and the client picks most of the bytes.
Twelve years in a few lines of C
Timeline
- Commit 47ee52986472 narrows the reply buffer while handling SEQUENCE. It ships in Linux 3.16.
- 12 yearsEvery kernel since 3.16 carries it: LTS, distribution and appliance kernels.
- Jérémy Jean of ANSSI sends a fix upstream with a KASAN report.
- OpenSec's Antara flags the bug, scores it 9.8 and turns it into a root shell on its own.
- CVE-2026-99362 is published with fixes.
Watch it run
A real run, end to end. Left: the victim's console. Right: our client on another machine, talking to the victim only over tcp/2049.
Root cause
Each NFSv4.1 session slot caches its last reply, so a retried request is answered exactly once. The client decides how big that cache is.
Set it to the minimum and every slot's buffer is zero bytes. nfsd4_sequence() then shrinks the reply buffer to that limit before it accepts the slot. Pad the request's tag so the buffer fills right after the SEQUENCE opcode, and the encoder quits without flagging an error. The server believes SEQUENCE succeeded and caches the whole reply, headers and tag included, into a buffer with room for nothing.
That is 80 bytes copied, 61 of them past the end of the heap object, 40 of them chosen by the client.
The fix checks that the reply fits before taking the slot, and makes the encoder report what it can't fit.
From overflow to root
The exploit stays private. Each step is an ordinary NFS request:
- Overflow The write corrupts the neighbouring slot's bookkeeping.
- Leak A replay request returns 3.8 KB of kernel heap, and with it the KASLR slide.
- Free A forged pointer makes the kernel free an object we choose.
- Reclaim We refill that memory with a fake callback built from NFS client names.
- Trigger We hang up. nfsd calls the fake callback, and the kernel runs /bin/sh as root.
In the recording, that command is whoami; id; poweroff -f: the victim prints root, then uid=0(root), and switches itself off.
Found and proven by a machine
Antara, OpenSec's white-box harness, flagged the nfsd reply-cache path, scored it 9.8, and built the leak, the arbitrary free and the root shell shown in the video by itself: end to end, with no human in the loop.
Kernel hardening does not remove the overflow. Patch every host.
Am I exposed?
Tick what's true for your host. All four means exposed, today.
Am I exposed?
What to do
Patch and reboot
On a distribution kernel, install the update that lists CVE-2026-99362.
Can't patch today? Close 2049
Or turn off NFSv4.1 and 4.2
Then restart nfs-server. This turns off pNFS and some 4.2 features.
Hunting for it
The attack is valid NFS, so there is no network signature. Look for:
- Kernel warnings from nfsd4_check_resp_size, on every attempt, successful or not.
- Oopses with nfsd4_*, svc_* or call_usermodehelper* frames.
- Shells spawned by kernel workers on a file server.
- Bursts of short NFSv4.1 sessions from one source.
Disclosure
We reported the bug to the NFSD maintainers and security@kernel.org on 22 September 2026 with the full remote-root exploit, and sent a patch the same day. CVE-2026-99362 was published with fixes on 20 November.
Credits
- OpenSec Intelligence: found the bug with Antara, which escalated it to pre-auth root on its own, and reported it with the full exploit and a patch.
- Jérémy Jean (ANSSI): found it independently and wrote the upstream fix, 5d5a72e965c8 and 241848cc774e.
- Chuck Lever and Jeff Layton: the NFSD maintainers.
References
nfsd: preflight SEQUENCE replies before accepting a slotThe upstream fix for CVE-2026-99362, by Jérémy Jean.github.com nfsd: set op->status when an operation's header cannot be encodedThe follow-up guard in the NFSv4 encoder.github.com
- Introduced by 47ee52986472 (Linux 3.16)
- Patch thread
- OpenSec's patch submission

====================================================================================================

Nfs2root: A 12-year-old pre-auth remote root in the Linux kernel's NFS server
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:35:50 UTC
05:35:50 KST

Port 2049 sits open on file servers, NAS appliances, build farms and Kubernetes storage nodes. Since 2014, the Linux kernel listening on it has let anyone who can reach it write into kernel memory before logging in. We pointed OpenSec's Antara at it. It came back with a root shell.
Pre-auth heap overflow in the Linux NFS server (nfsd)
Fixed in Linux 6.12.117, 6.18.61, 7.3.2 and 7.4-rc1. Update the kernel and reboot.
What you need to know
- What: a pre-auth heap overflow in the kernel's NFS server, nfsd.
- Who: any Linux host serving NFSv4.1 or 4.2 (the default) on a reachable tcp/2049.
- Impact: one TCP connection writes 61 bytes past a kernel heap object; chained, that gives a root shell.
- Now: patch and reboot. Until then, firewall tcp/2049.
Key facts
- Severity 9.8 Critical (AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N)
- Attack Remote, unauthenticated, tcp/2049
- Impact Kernel heap overflow → remote root shell
- Bug age 12 years, since Linux 3.16 (2014)
- Affected In-kernel nfsd with NFSv4.1 or 4.2
- Fixed in 6.12.117 · 6.18.61 · 7.3.2 · 7.4-rc1
One connection, no login
The NFS server runs inside the kernel and parses network input before it knows who you are. CVE-2026-99362 lives in that gap: a client can shrink its reply cache to zero bytes, then make the server copy a full reply into it. The copy lands on the kernel heap, and the client picks most of the bytes.
Twelve years in a few lines of C
Timeline
- Commit 47ee52986472 narrows the reply buffer while handling SEQUENCE. It ships in Linux 3.16.
- 12 yearsEvery kernel since 3.16 carries it: LTS, distribution and appliance kernels.
- Jérémy Jean of ANSSI sends a fix upstream with a KASAN report.
- OpenSec's Antara flags the bug, scores it 9.8 and turns it into a root shell on its own.
- CVE-2026-99362 is published with fixes.
Watch it run
A real run, end to end. Left: the victim's console. Right: our client on another machine, talking to the victim only over tcp/2049.

Set it to the minimum and every slot's buffer is zero bytes. nfsd4_sequence() then shrinks the reply buffer to that limit before it accepts the slot. The server believes SEQUENCE succeeded and caches the whole reply, headers and tag included, into a buffer with room for nothing.
That is 80 bytes copied, 61 of them past the end of the heap object, 40 of them chosen by the client.
The fix checks that the reply fits before taking the slot, and makes the encoder report what it can't fit.
From overflow to root
The exploit stays private. Each step is an ordinary NFS request:
- Overflow The write corrupts the neighbouring slot's bookkeeping.
- Leak A replay request returns 3.8 KB of kernel heap, and with it the KASLR slide.
- Free A forged pointer makes the kernel free an object we choose.
- Reclaim We refill that memory with a fake callback built from NFS client names.
- Trigger We hang up. nfsd calls the fake callback, and the kernel runs /bin/sh as root.
In the recording, that command is whoami; id; poweroff -f: the victim prints root, then uid=0(root), and switches itself off.

Antara, OpenSec's white-box harness, flagged the nfsd reply-cache path, scored it 9.8, and built the leak, the arbitrary free and the root shell shown in the video by itself: end to end, with no human in the loop.
Kernel hardening does not remove the overflow. Patch every host.
Am I exposed?

Am I exposed?
What to do
Patch and reboot
On a distribution kernel, install the update that lists CVE-2026-99362.
Can't patch today? Close 2049
Or turn off NFSv4.1 and 4.2
Then restart nfs-server. This turns off pNFS and some 4.2 features.
Hunting for it
The attack is valid NFS, so there is no network signature. Look for:
- Kernel warnings from nfsd4_check_resp_size, on every attempt, successful or not.
- Oopses with nfsd4_*, svc_* or call_usermodehelper* frames.
- Shells spawned by kernel workers on a file server.
- Bursts of short NFSv4.1 sessions from one source.

====================================================================================================

72-Hour Fasting Can Reset Your Immune System, USC Study Shows
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:35:39 UTC
05:35:39 KST

The USC-led research behind this headline investigated how prolonged fasting and refeeding affected blood-forming stem cells, mainly in mice. Published in Cell Stem Cell on June 5, 2014, it also included preliminary observations from cancer patients receiving chemotherapy.
It did not demonstrate that a single 72-hour fast resets the entire immune system in healthy people. The distinction between mouse regeneration experiments and early human observations is essential to understanding what the paper actually established.
The work remains scientifically interesting because it connected nutrient-sensitive signaling with the behavior of hematopoietic stem cells. These cells help replenish blood and immune-cell populations throughout life.
But a mechanism worth studying is not the same as an established self-treatment. This article examines the original findings, the later human feasibility study and why the word reset can obscure important limits in the evidence.
The immune system is not a single object
The immune system comprises many kinds of cells, tissues and communication pathways. Blood measurements capture part of that system, but they do not provide a complete inventory of every immune cell or its function.
Some cells circulate, while others reside in tissues or move between compartments. A change in the number found in blood can reflect movement as well as production, survival or loss.
That is why fewer circulating cells cannot automatically be interpreted as the removal of old or damaged cells. Likewise, a later increase does not by itself prove that every returning cell has been newly generated.
Immune health also depends on more than cell counts. The ability to respond to infection, maintain useful immune memory and avoid harmful inflammation involves coordinated activity across many populations.
A strong research claim should specify which part of the system was measured and which outcome improved. A complete reset would require a far broader demonstration than a change in white blood cell measurements.
The 2014 study’s focus was the hematopoietic system, especially the response of blood-forming stem cells to experimental stress. That is a substantial topic without expanding it into every feature of human immunity.
What hematopoietic stem cells do
Hematopoietic stem cells are cells with the capacity to maintain themselves and generate the precursors of different blood-cell lineages. They are supported by a local environment often called their niche.
The balance between self-renewal and differentiation matters. A stem-cell population needs to preserve its long-term capacity while producing the cells required for ongoing function.
Aging and injury can alter that balance. Researchers study whether changes in signaling or environment shift the kinds of cells produced, their resilience or their ability to recover after damage.
In this setting, the word regeneration refers to restoring or replenishing aspects of a blood-forming system. It does not necessarily mean replacing every immune cell or erasing a person’s immune history.
Experiments can investigate these processes using cell markers, functional assays and comparisons between treated and control animals. These methods provide more specific information than a simple before-and-after blood count.
However, findings from such experiments still need to be translated carefully. Mouse stem-cell behavior under a defined protocol is not a direct measurement of what happens after an unsupervised fast in an adult human.
What the USC experiment found
The researchers investigated repeated cycles of prolonged fasting and refeeding in mice, including animals exposed to chemotherapy. Their analyses examined blood-cell populations, stem cells and molecular signals related to growth and stress responses.
They reported that fasting reduced circulating IGF-1 and activity in a pathway involving protein kinase A, or PKA. These changes were associated with altered stem-cell behavior and recovery in the experimental setting.
The study also used manipulations of the proposed signaling pathway to investigate mechanism. That is important because changing a candidate pathway can provide stronger evidence than simply observing that it moves alongside an outcome.
The authors reported protection against chemotherapy-related suppression of the hematopoietic system and changes in age-associated lineage balance in mice. The experimental conclusions concern particular models, treatments and measured outcomes.
They do not establish that every cause of immune dysfunction responds the same way. Aging, chemotherapy exposure, infection, autoimmune disease and inherited immune disorders are biologically different contexts.
Nor should experimental fasting durations in mice be converted directly into a universal human schedule. Species differ in metabolism and physiology, so matching hours does not necessarily match biological exposure.
Refeeding was part of the experimental cycle
The study examined cycles rather than an uninterrupted fast considered in isolation. Recovery after food was reintroduced was relevant to the reported replenishment of blood-cell populations.
This matters because a headline can make the period without food sound like the entire intervention. In reality, the physiological response can change between nutrient deprivation and the return of nutrients.
A cell under limited nutrient availability may alter growth, maintenance or stress-response pathways. The subsequent availability of nutrients can support a different set of activities, including proliferation and replenishment.
That sequence is a biological hypothesis to test, not a reason to improvise a protocol. The timing, nutritional context and health of the organism can all affect the response.
Studying a cycle also requires more than taking one blood sample at the end of a fast. Measurements at several points help distinguish a transient shift from a persistent change in production or function.
The research therefore supports interest in the interaction between metabolic state and regeneration. It does not establish a universal moment at which an entire immune system switches from old to new.
What the early human observations could show
The 2014 paper included preliminary hematological observations from a phase 1 study of fasting around platinum-based chemotherapy. It compared observations associated with fasting periods of different lengths.
The authors described findings consistent with protection of some blood-cell measures in the longer-fasting group. They also explicitly stated that these preliminary results needed expansion and confirmation.
This was not a demonstration that healthy volunteers regenerated their whole immune systems. It was a small early clinical investigation in a specific oncology setting, with different chemotherapy combinations among participants.
Phase 1 research commonly asks whether an approach is feasible and tolerable under selected conditions. Such work can identify signals that justify later studies without proving that the intervention improves long-term health.
Patients undergoing chemotherapy are also not interchangeable with the general population. Their treatment, illness, nutritional status and clinical monitoring create circumstances different from those of someone experimenting at home.
Those distinctions should remain visible whenever the human portion of the study is described. Calling it a human trial does not automatically give it the power or scope of a large randomized efficacy study.
The later feasibility report involved 20 patients
A 2016 BMC Cancer report examined the safety and feasibility of fasting in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy in 20 participants. Its purpose and selected population are central to its interpretation.
The investigators excluded patients with diabetes, low body mass index or substantial recent weight loss. Those exclusions are evidence that safety could not simply be assumed for all cancer patients.
The participants also received care within a study framework. Clinical selection and monitoring are part of the conditions under which an experimental dietary intervention is evaluated.
A small study can provide information about common short-term problems and practical adherence. It is less capable of detecting uncommon harms or establishing durable improvements in cancer outcomes, infection resistance or longevity.
The report therefore should not be presented as confirmation that the entire immune system was rebuilt. It addressed a narrower clinical development question and described measurements within that context.
Its findings are relevant to further research, but they do not authorize changing an oncology treatment plan. Nutritional decisions during cancer care can have consequences that require individualized clinical judgment.
Why an immune-cell count is not a functional reset
White blood cell counts are useful clinical measurements, especially during treatments that can suppress blood-cell production. They do not capture every dimension of immune competence.
Two people can have similar counts while differing in the composition, activation state or function of their immune cells. Tissue-resident populations and immune memory add further complexity.
Similarly, a count returning toward baseline after an intervention does not establish that the resulting cells are superior to the earlier population. Demonstrating improved function requires relevant tests and outcomes.
For a proposed infection benefit, researchers would need evidence concerning infections or validated measures of protective responses. For a proposed aging benefit, they would need outcomes appropriate to health and function over time.
The term reset combines these different possibilities into an appealing image. Scientific evaluation needs to separate them so each can be tested rather than assumed.
Other fasting research shows context matters
A 2019 Cell study of dietary intake and inflammatory monocytes investigated how nutrient availability affects circulating immune-cell populations. It illustrates that diet can alter immune distribution and activity without implying universal rejuvenation.
Monocytes are one part of immunity, and changing their presence in blood can have several biological meanings. The relationship between inflammation and host defense depends on the setting.
Reducing excessive inflammatory activity may be useful in one context while weakening an appropriate response could be harmful in another. More immune activation and less immune activation are not automatically better or worse.
This is one reason claims about boosting immunity need precision. A useful intervention should improve a specified outcome rather than merely moving a marker in a direction described as favorable.
It also explains why distinct fasting protocols should not be merged casually. Short-term restriction, time-restricted eating, repeated prolonged fasting and fasting-mimicking diets are different exposures.
Evidence for one cannot establish the effectiveness of all the others. Calories, nutrients, timing and participant characteristics can influence whether the underlying biology is comparable.
What the study does not establish about aging
The immune system changes with age, and hematopoietic stem cells are relevant to those changes. That makes the USC work of interest to geroscience, the study of biological processes associated with aging.
However, an intervention that changes lineage balance in old mice does not automatically extend healthy lifespan in humans. That requires a separate chain of evidence.
Aging is also not a single process. A shift in blood-forming cells cannot establish that damage in the brain, cardiovascular system, muscles or other tissues has been reversed.
The study is therefore best understood as an investigation of particular pathways and outcomes. It contributes to a larger research program rather than providing a complete treatment for aging.
The original publication is from 2014, not a new clinical breakthrough in 2026. Its age does not make it irrelevant, but readers should know when the evidence was produced and how preliminary the human claims were.
Safety cannot be inferred from the word natural
Going without food for an extended period changes nutrient availability and can interact with illness, medications and an individual’s nutritional reserves. Those interactions make prolonged fasting a medical consideration rather than a universally benign experiment.
People receiving cancer treatment may already face appetite problems, weight loss or difficulty maintaining adequate nutrition. A strategy tolerable in selected study participants may be inappropriate for someone with different risks.
The exclusions in the feasibility study make that limitation concrete. A result from a group selected to avoid certain vulnerabilities cannot establish safety in the very groups excluded.
The same reasoning applies beyond oncology. Pregnancy, growth, frailty, metabolic disease and a history of disordered eating create circumstances that cannot be reduced to an internet fasting timetable.
This article is not a fasting protocol. Someone considering prolonged restriction, particularly during treatment for an illness, needs advice from a clinician who can assess their specific circumstances.
Importantly, fasting is not a replacement for chemotherapy, vaccination, infection treatment or other established care. The USC research does not support stopping those interventions in pursuit of an immune reset.
How commercial claims can outrun the evidence
The paper disclosed that senior author Valter Longo had an equity interest in L-Nutra, a company developing medical food. Such disclosures are part of the context readers should have when evaluating potential applications.
A financial relationship does not automatically invalidate the experiments. It does make it important to distinguish the research intervention from claims about commercial programs or products.
The study cannot establish that every fasting-mimicking product reproduces its mouse findings. A different formulation, duration or participant population requires its own evidence.
Likewise, a product described as activating fasting pathways may not produce the same exposure or biological effect. A marketing analogy to a mechanism is not a clinical outcome.
Independent studies, prespecified analyses and complete reporting help evaluate whether a promising idea translates reliably. They are particularly important when the public claim becomes broader than the original experiment.
What a decisive human trial would need to measure
A stronger human study would first define its intended population and goal. Protecting selected patients from treatment-related blood-cell suppression is different from improving vaccine responses or reducing infections in older adults.
The intervention would need a clear description, with appropriate nutritional assessment and monitoring. A comparison group would help distinguish its effects from normal recovery, treatment variation and other changes over time.
Researchers should specify clinical outcomes in advance. Blood counts could contribute to the evidence, but a claim about whole-system improvement would need broader functional measurements.
Longer observation would help establish durability and detect delayed harms. It would also show whether any short-term signal translates into a benefit that matters in everyday life.
Studies should report who was excluded as clearly as who participated. That allows clinicians and readers to understand the boundaries of both the safety findings and the proposed benefit.
Finally, replication matters. An early signal becomes more persuasive when independent teams can reproduce it and when later trials address the uncertainties left by the initial study.
Separating a change from a benefit
When evaluating any dietary experiment, it helps to ask what the measurement actually represents. A lower concentration, a different cell ratio and a change in gene activity are observations; their meaning depends on the biological and clinical context.
The next question is whether the change lasts after ordinary conditions resume. Temporary adaptation can be scientifically informative without demonstrating a lasting improvement in health.
The final question is whether a person fares better because of it. That requires outcomes such as fewer complications or improved function, evaluated in a way that can distinguish treatment effects from chance and alternative explanations.
A valuable mechanism without a proven human reset
The USC work connected fasting-related signaling with blood-forming stem-cell behavior in experimental models. It provided a scientific rationale for further investigation of protection and regeneration in particular settings.
The human evidence in the original report was preliminary, and the later small feasibility study did not establish universal immune regeneration. The supplied headline should be read with that central limitation in mind.
The useful question is not whether a three-day countdown guarantees a new immune system. It is which nutrient-sensitive mechanisms can be modified safely to improve a defined outcome in a defined group.
Keeping that question specific preserves the research’s value. It supports continued investigation without turning an intriguing mouse finding into an unsupported promise or a potentially risky instruction.
The human feasibility design also raises an important comparison problem. If groups follow different fasting durations and receive different chemotherapy combinations, a difference in blood-cell recovery may have more than one explanation. Larger randomized studies can help disentangle those factors.
Selection and adherence require attention as well. People able to complete a restrictive protocol may differ from those who cannot, including in nutritional reserves or illness severity. An analysis that overlooks those differences can overstate how widely a result applies.
For that reason, a complete clinical report should describe withdrawals, deviations and adverse experiences alongside the most encouraging measurements. Understanding who could not follow an intervention is part of evaluating it, especially when the proposed strategy involves restricting nutrition during a demanding treatment.
Featured image: illustrative photograph by manu schwendener on Unsplash, under the Unsplash License. It is not a recommendation to undertake prolonged fasting.

It also explains why distinct fasting protocols should not be merged casually. Short-term restriction, time-restricted eating, repeated prolonged fasting and fasting-mimicking diets are different exposures.
Evidence for one cannot establish the effectiveness of all the others. Calories, nutrients, timing and participant characteristics can influence whether the underlying biology is comparable.
What the study does not establish about aging
The immune system changes with age, and hematopoietic stem cells are relevant to those changes. That makes the USC work of interest to geroscience, the study of biological processes associated with aging.
However, an intervention that changes lineage balance in old mice does not automatically extend healthy lifespan in humans. That requires a separate chain of evidence.
Aging is also not a single process. A shift in blood-forming cells cannot establish that damage in the brain, cardiovascular system, muscles or other tissues has been reversed.
The study is therefore best understood as an investigation of particular pathways and outcomes. It contributes to a larger research program rather than providing a complete treatment for aging.
The original publication is from 2014, not a new clinical breakthrough in 2026. Its age does not make it irrelevant, but readers should know when the evidence was produced and how preliminary the human claims were.
Safety cannot be inferred from the word natural
Going without food for an extended period changes nutrient availability and can interact with illness, medications and an individual’s nutritional reserves. Those interactions make prolonged fasting a medical consideration rather than a universally benign experiment.
People receiving cancer treatment may already face appetite problems, weight loss or difficulty maintaining adequate nutrition. A strategy tolerable in selected study participants may be inappropriate for someone with different risks.
The exclusions in the feasibility study make that limitation concrete. A result from a group selected to avoid certain vulnerabilities cannot establish safety in the very groups excluded.
The same reasoning applies beyond oncology. Pregnancy, growth, frailty, metabolic disease and a history of disordered eating create circumstances that cannot be reduced to an internet fasting timetable.
This article is not a fasting protocol. Someone considering prolonged restriction, particularly during treatment for an illness, needs advice from a clinician who can assess their specific circumstances.
Importantly, fasting is not a replacement for chemotherapy, vaccination, infection treatment or other established care. The USC research does not support stopping those interventions in pursuit of an immune reset.
How commercial claims can outrun the evidence
The paper disclosed that senior author Valter Longo had an equity interest in L-Nutra, a company developing medical food. Such disclosures are part of the context readers should have when evaluating potential applications.
A financial relationship does not automatically invalidate the experiments. It does make it important to distinguish the research intervention from claims about commercial programs or products.
The study cannot establish that every fasting-mimicking product reproduces its mouse findings. A different formulation, duration or participant population requires its own evidence.
Likewise, a product described as activating fasting pathways may not produce the same exposure or biological effect. A marketing analogy to a mechanism is not a clinical outcome.
Independent studies, prespecified analyses and complete reporting help evaluate whether a promising idea translates reliably. They are particularly important when the public claim becomes broader than the original experiment.
What a decisive human trial would need to measure
A stronger human study would first define its intended population and goal. Protecting selected patients from treatment-related blood-cell suppression is different from improving vaccine responses or reducing infections in older adults.
The intervention would need a clear description, with appropriate nutritional assessment and monitoring. A comparison group would help distinguish its effects from normal recovery, treatment variation and other changes over time.
Researchers should specify clinical outcomes in advance. Blood counts could contribute to the evidence, but a claim about whole-system improvement would need broader functional measurements.
Longer observation would help establish durability and detect delayed harms. It would also show whether any short-term signal translates into a benefit that matters in everyday life.
Studies should report who was excluded as clearly as who participated. That allows clinicians and readers to understand the boundaries of both the safety findings and the proposed benefit.
Finally, replication matters. An early signal becomes more persuasive when independent teams can reproduce it and when later trials address the uncertainties left by the initial study.
Separating a change from a benefit
When evaluating any dietary experiment, it helps to ask what the measurement actually represents. A lower concentration, a different cell ratio and a change in gene activity are observations; their meaning depends on the biological and clinical context.
The next question is whether the change lasts after ordinary conditions resume. Temporary adaptation can be scientifically informative without demonstrating a lasting improvement in health.
The final question is whether a person fares better because of it. That requires outcomes such as fewer complications or improved function, evaluated in a way that can distinguish treatment effects from chance and alternative explanations.
A valuable mechanism without a proven human reset
The USC work connected fasting-related signaling with blood-forming stem-cell behavior in experimental models. It provided a scientific rationale for further investigation of protection and regeneration in particular settings.
The human evidence in the original report was preliminary, and the later small feasibility study did not establish universal immune regeneration. The supplied headline should be read with that central limitation in mind.
The useful question is not whether a three-day countdown guarantees a new immune system. It is which nutrient-sensitive mechanisms can be modified safely to improve a defined outcome in a defined group.
Keeping that question specific preserves the research’s value. It supports continued investigation without turning an intriguing mouse finding into an unsupported promise or a potentially risky instruction.
The human feasibility design also raises an important comparison problem. If groups follow different fasting durations and receive different chemotherapy combinations, a difference in blood-cell recovery may have more than one explanation. Larger randomized studies can help disentangle those factors.
Selection and adherence require attention as well. People able to complete a restrictive protocol may differ from those who cannot, including in nutritional reserves or illness severity. An analysis that overlooks those differences can overstate how widely a result applies.
For that reason, a complete clinical report should describe withdrawals, deviations and adverse experiences alongside the most encouraging measurements. Understanding who could not follow an intervention is part of evaluating it, especially when the proposed strategy involves restricting nutrition during a demanding treatment.

WhatsApp’s new scheduled messages let you choose the right time to send

2026-11-25
20:31:48 UTC
05:31:48 KST

WhatsApp’s 3 billion users are getting an option that Meta says is one of the most requested features, with the ability to schedule messages in advance for both private conversations and group chats. Most messaging platforms have had this for a very long time, but now it’s on WhatsApp too.
WhatsApp’s new scheduled messages let you choose the right time to send
You can schedule WhatsApp texts you might forget to send up to two weeks ahead of time.
If you think of a text at an inconvenient time or don’t want to forget to fire off a birthday message at 4AM, you can draft it and set it to send anywhere from 10 minutes to two weeks later. The feature works with photos and videos, too.
Meta:
To schedule a message, press and hold the send button and pick the date and time you want it to arrive. It will look like any other message to the person receiving it. You can see all future messages you have lined up, and delete them beforehand if you change your mind.
Even if your phone is off or out of battery, your scheduled message will still show up at the time you chose, and it remains end-to-end encrypted like all your personal messages, so no one else can read it, not even us.
Scheduled messages work with text, photos and videos, and are rolling out globally over the coming weeks. It’s one more way to show up for the people who matter to you, now at exactly the right time.

The Big Idea: Alma Alexander
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:12:36 UTC
05:12:36 KST

The Big Idea: Alma Alexander
Posted on November 25, 2026 Posted by Athena Scalzi 1 Comment
Real life mythos and legends often play a role in creating a fantasy world 0f one’s own, and author Alma Alexander has an especially unique myth0logy (one that is personal to her) that she is drawing from for the first book in her new trilogy, The Shadow Before the Dawn.
ALMA ALEXANDER:
Back in the 12th century, a quondam poet wrote an epic that contained the line, “There are only three subject matters for any discerning man: that of France, that of Britain, and that of Great Rome.” Possibly the most immediately familiar one of these would be the Matter of Britain – you will know it as the body of medieval literature associated with Great and with Less Britain (Brittany, for those who recognize it better by that name), and concerned with the legendary kings and heroes associated with it, King Arthur, Lancelot, the Knights of the Round Table, the Holy Grail, all that (mostly, blame Geoffrey of Monmouth). The Matter of France deals with Charlemagne and his paladins, and their wars with Moors and Saracens. The Matter of Rome, or Res Romana, is a more rarefied elixir of Greek and Roman mythology, which includes the Matter of Troy (hence the Odyssey and the Iliad), and which focused on military heroes like Alexander the Great and Julius Caesar.
But although those three were “anointed”, as it were, there are very much other “Matters” out there, for various countries and cultures. We might argue about whether Wagner muddied the waters with the Ring of the Nibelungen and twisted Matters of Norse origin and of German origin together in a Gordian knot; the Matter of Spain could be – well – maybe El Cid (Spanish people, feel free to correct me); we might definitely argue about the Matter of America, and about ideas it might contain, such as Manifest Destiny. The Matters of Countries are the invisible backbones upon which the shape of their existence hangs.
There is a Matter hidden deep in Eastern Europe, my own Matter, which talks of a line of Kings who walked into being a line of Saints, who created a kingdom out of a smattering of feeble little fiefdoms, and then forged it into an empire, and then fought a Last Battle in which everything they wrought in the Earthly realm was destroyed but which wrote their lineage into the Heavenly ledgers forever and gave their people the light they needed in order to survive the 500 years of slavery that followed their loss in that Last Battle. I am talking about the Nemanjic Dynasty in the 12th/13th centuries Balkans.
For a long time I have wanted to go back to that ancient tree and pick its fruit. This is my own Matter, the one that belongs to my own background, to my own people. It is a period stuffed with history, mystery, miracles, myths, legends, and larger-than-life characters, ripe for the plucking, and one which is rather less well known to the English-language reading public than the more easily accessible Matters out there. I started on a version of this story years ago, and wrote about 20,000 words of it, and then Life fell on me like a landslide and I lost in quick succession my husband, my mother, and a 19-year-old cat (don’t just dismiss this last; I had raised her from a 9-week-old kitten who weighed less than what it would take to make a decent hamburger when she came into my hands, and her loss HURT).
For six years I was a caregiver and then (in increasingly dense layers) someone who mourned and grieved, and all of that leaves little room for telling stories… so I kind of stopped writing. At all. And then, somehow, I picked up those twenty thousand words and looked at them again. And realized that I needed to tell another story before the story that I set out to tell in the first place. And then I realized that I needed to tell another story before THAT, to give weight and stability to the Matter I was addressing. And just like that, I had a trilogy on my hands.
The Shadow Before the Dawn, book 1 in what became that trilogy, was finally fully formed in 2026. The other books are in process. But that first book… this story matters to me, right from that beginning, deeply, organically. Sometimes I think everything I have ever written was training for this story, these books, to which I feel a true responsibility and which I am dedicated to giving the respect and dignity that they deserve.
Because I am writing a Matter. A story that shaped a nation.
You might not know this story.
Come with me, and let me open the doors into a world full of a magic which may be rich and strange and unfamiliar to you. It isn’t to me. This is my Matter, and it runs in my blood. I hope, when you are done reading this, that you will judge that I have done justice to it.
The Shadow Before the Dawn: Amazon

OpenAI says heavy ChatGPT users may hit limits sooner as it merges Chat and Work
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:03:27 UTC
05:03:27 KST

OpenAI has started rolling out a unified Chat and Work experience in ChatGPT to a few of its users, amid criticism from some subscribers that the merger could restrict their usage.
The ChatGPT maker began introducing the update to a small group of Pro subscribers, product lead Thibault “Tibo” Sottiaux said in a post on X on Tuesday. The rollout follows OpenAI’s earlier announcement of the merger in an email to users. An OpenAI spokesperson told TechCrunch that while most users are not expected to face more frequent usage resets, a small fraction of its heaviest users may hit their limits sooner.
That’s among the concerns raised by some ChatGPT subscribers on social media and OpenAI’s community forum recently. The users fear that merging the usage limits for Chat and Work could leave them with less access to advanced reasoning models than they currently have, even as they continue paying the same subscription fees.
Under the new system, Chat and Work will share a single usage meter across ChatGPT, the OpenAI spokesperson told TechCrunch. Conversations using the Instant model will, however, remain unlimited and will not count toward the shared allowance. Users can also continue using Instant after exhausting their usage limits. The spokesperson added that OpenAI would continue working to improve efficiency so subscribers could get more out of their plans.
While the rollout has started for a small group of users, OpenAI plans to bring shared usage limits more broadly in December, per its support page.
Similar to OpenAI, rival Anthropic also moved to bring its conversational chat service Claude and work-focused Cowork under one interface in September.
The changes come as AI companies face mounting infrastructure costs and growing demand for increasingly capable models, putting pressure on their computing resources. Bringing conversational and work-related tools into a single interface could also make AI chatbots simpler to use, allowing users to move between chats and more complex tasks without switching tabs.
