How Can Anything Beat n log n?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:54:29 UTC
02:54:29 KST

How Can Anything Beat n log n?
Section: Home / Post
Categories: Computer Science,
Tags: Algorithms, Entropy, Information Theory,
Recently, headlines have kept making the rounds about sub-$n \log n$ algorithms—first Duan et al. breaking Dijkstra’s $O(m + n \log n)$ sorting barrier for directed shortest paths, and then a preprint claiming sub-$n \log n$ integer multiplication.
My first reaction was skepticism. If a problem cares about ordering $n$ items, don’t you need $\log n$ steps to uniquely place each item, repeated $n$ times? That gives $n \log n$. How could you possibly do better?
The short answer is that $n \log n$ is not a universal speed limit. It is the lower bound of a specific model of computation. To see how algorithms beat it, we need to look at what $n \log n$ actually measures—and which assumptions those algorithms drop.
What $n \log n$ Actually Looks Like
Think of sorting $n$ items as routing each item down a decision tree into its final slot.
If you are only allowed to take 2-way comparison steps (< or >), every fork in the tree has 2 branches. Each step cuts the remaining slots in half. To route one item to its unique leaf among $n$ slots, it has to travel down roughly $\log_2 n$ levels.
Send all $n$ items down a tree of depth $\log_2 n$, and you pay $n \log_2 n$ total steps.
The Hidden Assumptions
That intuition feels airtight: if characterizing or placing a single item among $n$ slots takes $\log_2 n$ steps, and there are $n$ items to process, then any task requiring ordering or classification across $n$ items seems forced to pay $n \log_2 n$. Under two specific conditions, that reasoning is airtight:
- Black-box items: You cannot peek inside an item. Your only tool is a 2-way yes/no step (like is a < b?) that yields at most 1 bit of information per step.
- Factorial-scale outcomes: The number of possible final answers grows like $n!$ (or $n^n$)—meaning each of the $n$ items could end up in one of roughly $n$ distinct roles or positions. Not every task on $n$ items has that many outcomes: finding the maximum has only $n$ possible answers and assigning yes/no labels has $2^n$, compared to $n!$ orderings for sorting.
How Existing Algorithms Break $n \log n$
Counterexamples already exist in every algorithms textbook. Two classic ones—Bucket Sort and Radix Sort—sort $n$ numbers in $O(n)$ time when the numbers come from a bounded range or fixed digit width:
- Bucket Sort computes a bucket address directly from each number’s value, drops the item straight into that bucket in a single step (when numbers map cleanly into $n$ slots, that is a direct $n$-way lookup), and then reads the buckets back in order.
- Radix Sort processes numbers one digit at a time. In Most-Significant-Digit (MSD) Radix Sort, 3-digit numbers are first routed into 10 buckets (0–9) by their hundreds digit; only buckets holding more than one item are split again by their tens digit, and so on—without ever comparing two numbers against each other.
Neither algorithm breaks mathematics. Both drop the black-box assumption—and flatten the tree.
Imagine lining up 30 kids by birth month. If you treat them as black boxes and only compare pairs of kids (“who was born earlier?”), you are stuck with a 2-way tree of depth $\log_2 n$. Instead, put 12 labeled boxes on the floor (Jan through Dec). Each kid looks at their own birth month and walks straight to their box in one step.
Computers can do the same thing because numbers aren’t opaque black boxes—they are digits and bit patterns, and hardware can jump directly to a memory address in a single step. Direct indexing widens the branching factor all the way to $n$, collapsing the tree to depth 1. MSD Radix Sort makes the intermediate case just as clear: it routes the exact same items down a tree, except each node has 10 branches (0–9) instead of 2, flattening the depth from $\log_2 n$ down to the number of digits—with zero comparisons. (As we will see next, when numbers are allowed to grow arbitrarily large with $n$, escaping $n \log n$ asymptotically requires choosing the branching factor as a function of $n$ rather than keeping it fixed at 10.)
The Information-Theoretic View
Why does widening the branching factor break $n \log n$, and when is a lower bound truly unavoidable? Information theory puts both assumptions into a single equation.
In an earlier post on entropy, I framed entropy $H$ as the minimum number of binary choices (bits) needed to describe categories of events:
$$ H(\text{outcomes}) = -\sum_i p_i \log_2 p_i $$
That same lens gives us a hard floor for any algorithm.
Let $N$ be the total number of possible final answers to a problem—a function of the input size $n$, not $n$ itself. When every answer is equally likely (the worst case, $p_i = 1/N$), the entropy of the answer space simplifies to:
$$ H(N) = -\sum_{i=1}^{N} \frac{1}{N} \log_2 \frac{1}{N} = \log_2 N $$
Why is $H(N) = \log_2 N$ a hard floor for 2-way steps? Let $q$ be the number of 2-way (yes/no) steps an algorithm takes. Those $q$ steps can produce at most $2^q$ distinct transcripts of answers. If $2^q < N$, at least two different final answers must share the exact same transcript, and no algorithm can tell them apart. Therefore:
$$ 2^q \ge N \implies q \ge \log_2 N = H(N) $$
Where does the $\log_2 n$ in $n \log_2 n$ come from? Logarithms turn multiplication into addition:
- When each item has 2 outcomes ($N = 2^n$), taking the log yields $H(2^n) = \log_2(2^n) = n$—linear, with no $\log n$.
- When all $n$ items can be ordered in $N = n!$ ways, you are multiplying $n$ factors that grow with $n$. Taking $H(n!) = \log_2(n!)$ adds up $\log_2 1 + \log_2 2 + \dots + \log_2 n$, which lands at roughly $n \log_2 n$.
Now look at what happens when you widen each step’s branching factor to $k$. Remember from the entropy post that the base of the logarithm corresponds to how many outcomes a single step can distinguish, so $q$ $k$-way steps yield $k^q$ transcripts:
$$ k^q \ge N \implies q \ge \frac{H(N)}{\log_2 k} = \log_k N $$
Notice a crucial distinction here. Any constant $k$-way step only differs from a 2-way step by a constant factor ($1/\log_2 k$): a fixed 10-way decimal lookup trims the work by $\log_2 10 \approx 3.32\times$, and a fixed 256-way byte lookup trims it by $8\times$, yet as $n$ grows without bound (and distinct numbers need at least $\log_k n$ digits), both stay locked to $O(n \log n)$ forever.
To actually escape $n \log n$, $k$ must be a function of $n$ (for example, $k = \sqrt{n}$ or $k = n$). Once $k$ scales with $n$ as $k = n^\alpha$, $\log_2 k = \alpha \log_2 n$ cancels the $\log_2 n$ in $n \log_2 n$, so the total number of steps is only off from a one-shot $n$-way lookup by a constant factor ($1/\alpha$)—that is, linear $O(n)$. Radix Sort achieves this when its base $k$ is chosen relative to the size and range of the numbers (e.g., $k = n$ or $k = \sqrt{n}$): we are using the numerical value of the input itself to inform the algorithm’s addressing, piercing the black box.
Entropy Is a Floor, Not a Guarantee
One important nuance: having $H(N) = \log_2 N$ bits of entropy does not guarantee you can solve a problem in $\log_2 N$ steps (or $\log_k N$ steps with a $k$-way step).
Compare searching a sorted list against finding the maximum of an unsorted list. Both have $N = n$ possible answers, so both have the exact same entropy: $H(n) = \log_2 n$ bits. Yet finding the max takes $n - 1$ comparisons, because each step only knocks out one loser—it cannot cut the remaining candidates in half.
More generally, to reach $q \approx \log_k N = H(N) / \log_2 k$ steps with a $k$-way step over $N$ possible outcomes, each step must partition the remaining candidates symmetrically (evenly) into $k$ disjoint buckets of roughly $N/k$ outcomes. That is what lets every step shrink the answer space by division ($N \to N/k$) and extract the full $\log_2 k$ bits toward the answer.
When a step is asymmetric—say, checking “is item $i$ the target?” on an unsorted list, where one branch isolates $1$ outcome and the other leaves $N - 1$—the worst-case branch shrinks by subtraction ($N \to N - 1$) instead of division, so you never reach $\log_k N$. Finding the maximum runs into a related failure: a > b has two equally likely outcomes, yet on the answer space of $N$ candidate maxima it only discards a single loser, leaving $N - 1$ candidates alive either way because the remaining information is spent on relative order we do not need. You only hit the entropy floor when the problem’s structure lets each step carve the remaining answers into roughly equal, disjoint fractions.
Bringing It All Together
Every complexity claim sits on two axes: how fast the answer space $N$ grows, and how much information a single $k$-way step can extract.
So whenever you see a headline announcing a sub-$n \log n$ algorithm, there are only two ways it happened: either the problem never actually had a factorial-scale answer space in the first place (computing shortest-path distances doesn’t require sorting every vertex, and multiplying two $n$-bit integers only has $2^{2n}$ possible outputs—an entropy floor of $2n$, never $n!$), or the algorithm found a way to look inside the box and widen the branching factor $k$ with $n$.

====================================================================================================

FCC signs off on SpaceX’s plan to put up to 1 million data center satellites into space
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:44:57 UTC
02:44:57 KST

In an announcement on Wednesday, the FCC says it authorized SpaceX to deploy up to 1 million AI chip-equipped satellites as part of the Elon Musk-owned company’s “Starmind” initiative. With several others also pursuing the idea of putting AI data centers in space, including Google and Blue Origin, this is the first approval the agency has granted for a full “constellation” of AI satellites.
FCC signs off on SpaceX’s plan to put up to 1 million data center satellites into space
SpaceX aims to power AI capabilities here on Earth with its ‘Starmind’ constellation.
The FCC’s approval requires SpaceX to deploy at least one satellite by 2033, and meet the 1 million satellite milestone by 2040. The FCC also deferred SpaceX’s request to dispose of satellites located above 600km in space “graveyard” orbits, rather than having them burn up in the Earth’s atmosphere, saying the company hasn’t provided “sufficient information” about its disposal process plans.
SpaceX has described its Starmind project as a way to “capture solar energy in space to power” AI capabilities on Earth. The company says Starmind will transmit data to Earth using high-speed lasers that connect to Starlink. It plans to produce chips for its satellites at an upcoming Terafab facility.
Google has already taken its first steps toward putting compute into space, with the launch of a “Project Suncatcher” AI server satellite last month. Jeff Bezos’ Blue Origin will carry out the first dedicated heavy-lift launch for Project Suncatcher in 2029.

Britain's ghost stations
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:43:46 UTC
02:43:46 KST

When I stepped off a train at Achanalt station into the frosty Highlands one morning last March, I did notice that it wasn’t quite Waterloo in the rush hour, but I didn’t realise what an exclusive club I had just joined. According to a list of Britain’s most and least-used stations published this week, I was one of only 352 people to board or get off a train there in the entire 12 months from April 2025 to March 2026.
Flanders and Swann wrote ‘The Slow Train’ about the Beeching closures, lamenting the rural stations which were to close. They needn’t have worried: over half a century on, such places still exist.
My instinct is to attack the waste of public money in keeping a station open when hardly anyone uses it, but the absurdity of Britain’s ‘ghost’ stations fascinates me more than angers me. In any case, having paid £11 to travel 20 miles down the line from Strathcarron, I did rather feel that I had single-handedly covered the annual cost of maintaining what is really little more than a platform and Perspex shelter.
Achanalt, it turns out, is only the 15th least-used station in Britain. The honour of being the least-used this year is taken by Reddish South, on an obscure line from Stockport to Stalybridge in Greater Manchester, which was used by only 94 passengers. It is an achievement it will be hard-pressed to repeat next year, as the country’s least-used station invariably receives a leap in custom as railway fanatics descend on it just for the sake of being there: station-visiting has apparently taken over from train-spotting now that few trains have proper locomotives any more. Last year’s winner of the title for least-used station, Elton and Orston, in Nottinghamshire, saw its annual passenger numbers jump from 68 to 102, demoting it to third place. I wouldn’t be surprised if every single one of the extra passengers wore an anorak and carried a bag of Marmite sandwiches.
There are two types of ghost station. Firstly, there are those such as Reddish South which are the product of public opposition to the Beeching closures. Some time in the 1970s, British Rail stopped closing stations, reckoning that the bureaucracy and expense of going through the consultation procedure required to remove them from the railway map altogether was greater than simply running down the service to a minimal level. As a result, Reddish South receives one train a week in each direction, on a Saturday morning. You can’t even get a day out in Stockport: catch the 8.46 from Reddish South and you only get five minutes before the train home.
The second type of ghost station is those like Achanalt, which offer a full service of several trains a day; it is just that there aren’t many people who want to travel there. In Achanalt’s case, it isn’t hard to wonder why. The ‘village’, if it can be called that, consists of four houses. Nor is the station all that much good for doing what I was doing: accessing the nearby hills, as a river cuts them off to the south and a thick forestry plantation lies in the way to the north. If you want to go for a walk, it is almost certainly going to involve a few miles’ romp along the A832.
It is pretty absurd that Dr Beeching left a station like this open while depriving Dudley (population 335,000) and Mansfield (114,000) of a rail service – although the latter has subsequently reopened. But at least Achanalt serves four properties. Even more bizarre is Altnabreac station on the Far North Line between Inverness and Wick.
Maybe Dr Beeching couldn’t even find it on the map. It lies up a track seven miles from the nearest public road and there is precisely one house within five miles: the former station house. Trouble is, owners, who presumably could have bought a remote property anywhere in the empty vastness of Caithness, don’t seem to like trains very much. In 2023, a gang of workers from Network Rail turned up to repair the platform and found the gate locked. After a stand-off lasting many months, involving many lawyers and a court case, and a threat by Network Rail to compulsorily purchase the property so that access could be restored, an injunction finally gave the work gang access.
In the meantime, no trains ran at all: the official passenger numbers for the year 2024/25 were zero. Happily, services have now been restored and the walkers and bird-spotters have returned to the platform. Er, all 172 of them last year.

====================================================================================================

Why aren't we working 2-days a week?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:37:17 UTC
02:37:17 KST

Why aren't we working 2-days a week?
Okay, AI is here to replace us. At this point the dream is that corporations save billions through layoffs, productivity gains and increasing the precarity of the existing workers, while a few people at AI companies profit from the technology and the hype. There are AI tools being baked into every process whether they work or not and the hocus-pocus words of the hours automation, knowledge work, singularity and efficiency.
So why is everyone in white collar jobs still working 40+ hour days as the norm? Why aren't these productivity gains being passed on to the employees who can in turn use it for leisure or whatever it is that they fancy? Rather with the advent of AI, the focus seems to be working more. Jobs are slashed and the existing workers take the work of more people for the same pay and even more precarious conditions.
At some point there needs to be a bigger talk about the societal impact of AI and how it should lead to more free time if not less. The times of factory work and the mandated 5 day work weeks feel archaic in this new paradigm, less commutes, lesser pollution offsetting for the greater energy consumption from the datacenters. Let the rich get rich and the poor have fun, let laziness roost and 21st century be one of rest and relaxation.

====================================================================================================

DOJ accuses former cyber executive of defrauding ransomware victims by secretly paying hackers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:36:51 UTC
02:36:51 KST

The U.S. Department of Justice has indicted a former executive of a ransomware recovery firm after he allegedly defrauded victims of cyberattacks by claiming the company could get their encrypted files back.
According to federal prosecutors, Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, 52, was the chief technology officer at RansomwareHelp, which purported to offer data recovery services to victims of ransomware. The company’s website, which currently reads as “under maintenance,” claimed it could decrypt hacked data by using its proprietary technology, according to an archived copy of the site from earlier this year. RansomwareHelp touted a success rate of “over 99%.”
But the company allegedly had no technical ability to unscramble data and instead “negotiated with and paid a ransom to the ransomware actors to decrypt the victim data,” while charging the ransomware victim more and keeping the difference,” per the Justice Department’s statement.
Palacio Escobar then allegedly used the funds to buy several properties in Florida.
The DOJ said the FBI carried out an undercover operation in June, in part posing as a company that claimed it had been hit by ransomware. The company claimed to the undercover agents that it never negotiated with hackers or paid a ransom. But then Palacio Escobar paid the apparent hackers some $6,000 in cryptocurrency, without the knowledge that FBI agents were also posing as the ransomware hackers.
404 Media first reported the indictment last week against Palacio Escobar. He faces three charges of wire fraud, and could receive 20 years in prison on each count.
Palacio Escobar is the latest, albeit rare, example of cybersecurity professionals going rogue. Earlier this year, two ransomware negotiators working for cybersecurity companies were jailed on hacking and extortion charges, after being caught moonlighting with the ransomware gangs that they were ostensibly investigating. A third ransomware negotiator was also convicted of helping hackers deploy ransomware against several companies.
Last month, independent cybersecurity journalist Brian Krebs reported that FBI agents arrested a Canadian cybersecurity executive in Philadelphia on allegations that he was involved with the theft of FBI personnel records by the hacking and extortion gang ShinyHunters. The hacking group told TechCrunch that they took credit for the theft of FBI data in September, which they said they would not publicly release, a departure from its earlier extortion-based cyberattacks.
The federal charges against the Canadian cybersecurity executive relate to conspiracy to commit extortion, though much of the court docket — and his alleged links to the hacking group — remains under seal.

====================================================================================================

State AGs fight Character.AI’s plan to auction off users’ private chats
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:35:58 UTC
02:35:58 KST

What happens to your private conversations when an AI company goes bankrupt? If it’s Character.ai, apparently, the plan is to auction them off to the highest bidder.
Now, a coalition of 42 state attorneys general is intervening in Character.ai’s bankruptcy proceedings in an effort to stop the chatbot maker from selling off sensitive user data, which includes the private conversations of millions of users.
The attorneys general involved in the matter have put out individual press releases, claiming that Character.ai’s dataset includes chat histories from more than 21 million U.S. accounts, more than 2.1 million of which belong to minors. In addition, the sale would involve other personal data, like IP addresses, the statements said.
Character.ai, which filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy in October, previously told consumers that their personal information would not be sold, the statements also noted. (In a blog post, Character.ai assured its users that their information would still be protected under its privacy policy.)
Only one anonymous bidder for the chat data has been disclosed so far: Smirks and Pangs Acquisition LLC (Smirks), which was set up as a Delaware company on November 13, 2026. The following day, Character.ai picked it as the base bid for the auction, scheduled for December 1, California Attorney General Rob Bonta noted. It is unclear what the buyer’s plans are for the chats.
The AGs are opposing this sale on the grounds that there are no protections for users — whoever buys the data can do as they like, including reselling the data without disclosure, they said. Buyers also won’t have to honor Character.ai’s privacy policy. Plus, Character.ai hasn’t provided the court with as much information as the bidders have been given about any potential safety or legal issues following the sale, the AGs claim.
In addition, some states, including California, Colorado, New York, and Connecticut, have enacted chatbot safety legislation that will go into effect on January 1, 2027, which could trigger further violations if the sale goes through.
“Character.AI promised consumers that their data would not be sold. This kind of behavior is illegal under federal and California law and bankruptcy should not be used to sidestep these consumer protections,” Bonta said in a statement.
Connecticut Attorney General William Tong also criticized Character.ai’s business model.
“Their chatbots drove vulnerable youth down a rabbit hole of emotionally volatile and sexually inappropriate chats,” Tong said. “Their reckless misconduct and broken business model drove their company into the ground. Now they want to abuse the bankruptcy process to do exactly what they said they wouldn’t do — auction off those private chat records and personal details to a shady buyer.”
Tong added: “We objected to the Disney sale, and we’re calling on the bankruptcy court to reject this sale too as yet another guise to eviscerate legal liability and consumer protection.”
The opposition to the Disney sale was led by a similar but not identical coalition of state AGs. They fought against a proposed $20 million licensing deal that would give Disney access to Character.ai’s intellectual property, but not to users’ chats. The deal has not yet received final court approval. (Disney also agreed to hire most of Character.ai’s remaining staff.)
Character.ai suffered after its co-founders, Noam Shazeer and Daniel De Freitas, returned to Google, when the tech giant signed a non-exclusive license to use the AI startup’s tech. The company also faced lawsuits related to child safety — one involved the suicide of a 14-year-old Florida boy whose mother claimed he had become obsessed with a chatbot on the platform.
California AG Bonta is joined in the effort to oppose the sale of Character.ai user data by attorneys general from Alaska, Arkansas, Colorado, Connecticut, the District of Columbia, Delaware, Florida, Georgia, Hawaii, Idaho, Illinois, Indiana, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Michigan, Minnesota, Mississippi, Missouri, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Utah, Virginia, Vermont, Washington, Wisconsin, and West Virginia.

X’s latest update for creators focuses on vertical video
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:28:29 UTC
02:28:29 KST

X announced new creation tools Wednesday, many of them focused on short-form vertical video. The updates are part of the platform’s effort to compete with social networks such as YouTube and Instagram as the format continues to grow in popularity.
The tools are designed to help creators make professional-quality vertical videos, share original content more easily, and earn more. They also give creators more ways to record, edit, and publish content directly on the platform without relying on third-party apps.
A key addition is an upgraded media editor that lets creators produce polished vertical videos without leaving X. Recording features include the ability to capture video in segments and use green-screen effects, the company said.
The editor also offers a range of postproduction tools. Creators can convert horizontal videos to vertical format, automatically generate captions, trim footage, add text overlays, choose custom thumbnails, and adjust photos before publishing.
Thanks to a recently announced update, users can now add up to 20 photos and videos to a single post, which audiences can swipe through horizontally. X says the feature is intended for sharing event recaps, behind-the-scenes moments, and product launches.
Availability of the new features varies by region, X said.
Earlier this month, the company introduced a clipping feature that lets users take snippets from longer videos, add their own commentary, and post the clips without leaving X. Clips credit and link to the original video, plus the person who creates a clip and the original poster can earn from its views.
Another feature, “React with Video,” launched earlier this year. Similar to TikTok’s “stitch” feature, this lets creators respond to posts with video commentary while displaying the original post in the background.
X has also introduced new earning opportunities and updates to its creator monetization programs in recent months. These include the “Video Kickstart Bonus,” an invitation-only pilot that rewards selected creators for publishing original vertical videos under three minutes long and without watermarks.
The platform has also launched “Subscriber-only Group Chats,” which let creators host private, real-time conversations with paying subscribers. The feature is currently open to creators with 800 or fewer subscribers, and each group chat can accommodate up to 1,000 members.

====================================================================================================

1Password Security Incident
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:18:26 UTC
02:18:26 KST

Update on a security incident involving 1Password’s backend systems. Customer vaults were not affected and all vault contents remain secure.
by 1Password
November 25, 2026 - 4 min
Related Categories
What happened
On November 19, 2026, 1Password began investigating indications that an attacker gained access to an employee’s corporate GitHub account. The actor used the account to access source code and push malicious code to backend services, which downloaded administrative data pertaining to our customers. This data included customer names and email addresses, in addition to non-secret internal metadata about the account. At no time were customer vault contents accessed by, or available to, the attacker.
What information was affected
The attacker downloaded account records for a subset of our users. These records include:
- Names and email addresses
- Account and user identifiers, and account creation and last sign-in times
- Public keys (which are public by design, this has no impact to user security)
- Secure Remote Password protocol parameters (which cannot be used to access your account)
- Tokens present in certain emailed links for invitations, provisioning and account recovery
- Other non-secret account metadata relative to our internal architecture (e.g. version numeric identifiers, file numeric identifiers)
The attacker also copied source code from private repositories and gained access to our CI/CD system. Our source code is not customer data, and contains no user secrets. CI/CD contains infrastructure related secrets in order for our product to work. We have triggered a rotation of all credentials which may have been exposed, and there is no impact to 1Password’s services, availability, or product security.
When we send an email to a customer to accept a provisioning invite to an account, or to accept recovery of their account, it contains a token. A provisioning invite is specifically used for our SCIM Bridge or Hosted Provisioning. During this incident there were a small number of users in this state, who have been specifically notified. This token is used to complete the invitation or recovery process, but only when combined with an SSO sign-in or a six digit code sent to the user's registered email address. The token is not able to get access to the account by itself. In order to reduce the ability for a malicious third party to successfully guess the code, we’ve expanded the code from six numeric digits to ten alphanumeric digits. This makes brute force guessing practically impossible.
What was not affected
- Your vaults and the items in them
- The keys that protect your vaults 
  - 1Password does not store your account password or secret key
- Account recovery keys
- 1Password apps and browser extensions
What we’ve done
- Disabled the affected GitHub account, revoked the stolen token, and removed the access the attacker created.
- Rotated or revoked all internal credentials which may have been exposed.
- Lengthened the codes used in invitation and recovery emails from six digits to ten characters as a precautionary measure.
- Tightened internal GitHub, CI/CD, and endpoint controls, and added new detections for this kind of attack.
- Validated no internal evidence of user account takeovers or compromise using any exposed account provisioning tokens.
- Brought in independent cybersecurity experts to validate our containment approaches and remediation efforts.
What you can do
- Be cautious about phishing attempts in the form of unexpected emails, texts or calls that mention 1Password, invitations, or account recovery attempts.
- 1Password will never contact you to ask for your account password or Secret Key. Only enter them in the official 1Password app, or on our official website, and only when you’ve opened the app or typed the address yourself. Your Secret Key is only needed the first time you sign in on a new device or browser.
- Keep your Secret Key and account password safe.
- If you see something suspicious, tell us at support@1password.com.
Our commitment to you
You trust us with some of the most sensitive information you have. 1Password is designed so that a breach of our systems can’t expose your vault contents. Your vault secrets are encrypted with keys that only you hold, so we can’t access them, and neither can an attacker who gets into our servers.
We are committed to transparency in the interests of our customers' security and the security of the broader industry, which is why we have published and maintained our Security Whitepaper since 2015. We’ll keep this blog post current, and we’ll update it if we find anything that changes what we’ve said here.

====================================================================================================

We have triggered a rotation of all credentials which may have been exposed, and there is no impact to 1Password’s services, availability, or product security.
When we send an email to a customer to accept a provisioning invite to an account, or to accept recovery of their account, it contains a token. A provisioning invite is specifically used for our SCIM Bridge or Hosted Provisioning. During this incident there were a small number of users in this state, who have been specifically notified. This token is used to complete the invitation or recovery process, but only when combined with an SSO sign-in or a six digit code sent to the user's registered email address. The token is not able to get access to the account by itself. In order to reduce the ability for a malicious third party to successfully guess the code, we’ve expanded the code from six numeric digits to ten alphanumeric digits. This makes brute force guessing practically impossible.
What was not affected
- Your vaults and the items in them
- The keys that protect your vaults 
  - 1Password does not store your account password or secret key
- Account recovery keys
- 1Password apps and browser extensions
What we’ve done
- Disabled the affected GitHub account, revoked the stolen token, and removed the access the attacker created.
- Rotated or revoked all internal credentials which may have been exposed.
- Lengthened the codes used in invitation and recovery emails from six digits to ten characters as a precautionary measure.
- Tightened internal GitHub, CI/CD, and endpoint controls, and added new detections for this kind of attack.
- Validated no internal evidence of user account takeovers or compromise using any exposed account provisioning tokens.
- Brought in independent cybersecurity experts to validate our containment approaches and remediation efforts.
What you can do
- Be cautious about phishing attempts in the form of unexpected emails, texts or calls that mention 1Password, invitations, or account recovery attempts.
- 1Password will never contact you to ask for your account password or Secret Key. Only enter them in the official 1Password app, or on our official website, and only when you’ve opened the app or typed the address yourself. Your Secret Key is only needed the first time you sign in on a new device or browser.
- Keep your Secret Key and account password safe.
- If you see something suspicious, tell us at support@1password.com.
Our commitment to you
You trust us with some of the most sensitive information you have. 1Password is designed so that a breach of our systems can’t expose your vault contents. Your vault secrets are encrypted with keys that only you hold, so we can’t access them, and neither can an attacker who gets into our servers.
We are committed to transparency in the interests of our customers' security and the security of the broader industry, which is why we have published and maintained our Security Whitepaper since 2015. We’ll keep this blog post current, and we’ll update it if we find anything that changes what we’ve said here.

Deriving Black-Scholes
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:18:15 UTC
02:18:15 KST

Deriving Black-Scholes
Option price from the no-arbitrage hypothesis.
Even if it feels unintuitive, the price of an option does not depend on whether you expect the underlying to moon or tank. Under some reasonable market hypotheses, we can build a fully hedged portfolio by holding the underlying and shorting the option. Since it is riskless, by no-arbitrage assumption, it must return the same as the risk-free interest rate. That allows to put together an equation (the Black-Scholes equation), and solve for the option price (the Black-Scholes formula).
Model §
A European call gives the right, but not the obligation, to buy an underlying at a fixed price \(K\!\), the strike, on a fixed date \(T\!\), the maturity. Its value \(V\!\) is the premium you need to pay for having that optionality, and it depends on time and on the underlying stock price \(S\!\). At maturity it is the payoff1 1 For example, pay $5 today for the right to buy one AMZN share for $200 in a month. If AMZN then trades at $230, you buy at $200 and the option is worth $30. If it trades at $180, you walk away and the option is worth nothing.
\[V(T,S)=\max(S-K,0).\]
Stock moves scale with price, so it is common to model the returns instead of the price. Over a short time the return is a small drift plus noise. The noise is the sum of many small, unpredictable2 2 Weak-form efficient market hypothesis: past prices do not predict future moves. sources. Such sum can be modeled as a Brownian motion \(W\!\),3 3 By the central limit theorem, the sum of many small independent variables is normal. so the return is
\[\frac{dS}{S}=\mu\,dt+\sigma\,dW\!,\]
with drift \(\mu\) and volatility \(\sigma\!\). For our model, we will assume constant drift, volatility and interest rate \(r\!\). We will also assume “perfect” trading, with fractional shares allowed, no fees, no taxes, no dividends, and no arbitrage.
Hedged portfolio §
The trick of Black-Scholes is to build a fully hedged portfolio combining the option and the underlying in the right proportion. By the no-arbitrage hypothesis, its returns must be the same as the interest rate \(r\!\), and that way we can put together an equation.
When the stock moves a little, the option moves by \(\Delta\) times as much, with
\[\Delta=\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}.\]
If you short one option and hold \(\Delta\) shares, then the two moves cancel. This is a delta hedge.
Note that \(\Delta\) changes with the stock price and time. The portfolio is riskless only over an instant, and stays so only if it is rebalanced continuously.
Black-Scholes equation §
Use the Taylor expansion of the option value and keep the terms up to order \(dt\). Since \((dW)^2=dt\), the \((dS)^2\) term is one of them,
\[dV=\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}dt+\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}dS +\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}(dS)^2.\]
Substituting the stock model gives
\[dV=\left(\mu S\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}+\frac{\partial V}{\partial t} +\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2S^2\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}\right)dt +\sigma S\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\,dW.\]
Now, we can use the same hedged portfolio \(\Pi\) as before, short one option and long \(\Delta\) shares, which is worth \(-V+\Delta S\!\). Holding \(\Delta\) fixed over an instant,
\[d\Pi=-dV+\Delta dS,\]
and substituting \(dS\) and \(dV\!\) gives
\[d\Pi=\left(-\frac{\partial V}{\partial t} -\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2S^2\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}\right)dt.\]
See how noise and drift cancel. No \(dW\!\) term is left, and everything else is known at time \(t\), so over the next instant the portfolio change is fixed.4 4 A known return with no randomness is precisely what riskless means. We’ve built a riskless portfolio, that by the no-arbitrage assumption, it must earn the risk-free rate, \(d\Pi=r\Pi\,dt.\) Equating both expressions gives us the Black-Scholes equation
\[\frac{\partial V}{\partial t}+rS\frac{\partial V}{\partial S} +\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2S^2\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial S^2}=rV.\]
Note that \(\mu\) is gone, so the option price does not depend on the drift.
Black-Scholes formula §
I won’t bore you with the technical details,5 5 For the details, see John C. Hull, Options, Futures, and Other Derivatives, 11th edition, Pearson, 2021. The Bible of derivatives. but the above equation can be solved by a change of variables to turn it into the heat equation, whose solution is known. Define
\[\tau=T-t,\quad x=\ln S+\left(r-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2\right)\tau,\quad V(t,S)=e^{-r\tau}w(\tau,x),\]
where \(w\) is the option value at maturity. Substituting, all terms but two cancel, and we get the heat equation
\[\frac{\partial w}{\partial\tau}=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2\frac{\partial^2w}{\partial x^2}.\]
With the payoff as initial condition, the solution is
\[w(\tau,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2\tau}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \max(e^y-K,0)\,\exp\left(-\frac{(y-x)^2}{2\sigma^2\tau}\right)dy.\]
Completing the squares gives the Black-Scholes formula
\[V(t,S)=S\,N(d_1)-Ke^{-r\tau}N(d_2),\]
where \(N\) is the standard normal CDF and
\[d_1=\frac{\ln(S/K)+\left(r+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^2\right)\tau}{\sigma\sqrt{\tau}}, \qquad d_2=d_1-\sigma\sqrt{\tau}.\]
- \(N(d_1)\) is the hedge ratio \(\Delta\), so the first term represents the price of an asset-or-nothing call, which pays the share if the call ends in the money.
- \(N(d_2)\) is the risk-neutral probability of exercise, so the second term is the present value of the expected strike payment.
That’s all, lads, go make Ken Griffin broke.

====================================================================================================

Those Five Tons of Bomb-Making Chemicals in Iowa Will Be Detonated Wednesday Morning
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:00:00 UTC
02:00:00 KST

Those Five Tons of Bomb-Making Chemicals in Iowa Will Be Detonated Wednesday Morning (iowacapitaldispatch.com) 71
			
		 	
				The detonation is back on.  Nearly five tons of unstable lead azide explosives will be detonated Wednesday morning at a U.S. Army ammunition plant in Middletown, Iowa, reports the Iowa Capital Dispatch (part of the nonprofit States Newsroom network 
"The scheduled time follows numerous delays over the past week from the Army, a lawsuit, intervention from the president, and widespread public concern of the health and environmental effects associated with detonating a lead-based explosive."
Lead deposition maps released by the Army Tuesday morning show lead released by the detonation could be dispersed in the air as far as 100 miles southwest of Middletown and cross into Missouri. An additional map shows there is a potential for lead surface dust to settle as far as 50 miles southwest of the detonation site.   The Army has recommended, and the Federal Emergency Management Agency is supporting, the evacuation of Iowans in Middletown and the immediate area — a 5-mile cone to the southwest of the detonation site — during the detonation period. Soldiers, environmental teams and other federal officials are in the area helping to sample soils and alert residents of the detonation plans... 
A slew of federal officials from the Army, the EPA, Department of Health and Human Services, Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, and Firearms, Department of Agriculture and more gathered at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant Tuesday for a press conference and shared new information with the public about the response... Speaking at the press conference Tuesday, Sean Kaufman with HHS said the highest risk is for pregnant individuals, children, or people with chronic illnesses who live or plan to be in the areas the Army forecasts will be impacted.  "You definitely want to be out of that area," Kaufman said of the at-risk groups. "I would evacuate that area that's signaled in the model... "    As many as 100 Iowa National Guard soldiers will be available to aid local, state and federal agencies in their public safety plans [according to a news release from the governor]. They will also help provide guidance and assistance to around 150 households with a temporary evacuation...
At the news conference, acting Secretary of the Army Hon. Adam Telle emphasized that the government's priority was the "health and safety of the citizens of this region."  Telle said those involved have sought advice from "every expert" in and outside of the federal government and have considered all viable options and data before reaching the conclusion to proceed with detonation.
"There is consensus across the experts across the country that a controlled, mitigated detonation of the lead azide here at Iowa Army Ammunition Plant, is far preferable to an uncontrolled spontaneous detonation of that substance," Telle said.  Some of those mitigations include: sand around the structure holding the barrels; two walls of four, 16,000-gallon water tanks; a plan to "flood" the exterior of the storage area with aqueous foam to help absorb particulate matter from the blast; and modeling to forecast impacted areas based on weather conditions.

WhatsApp now lets you schedule messages in group and individual chats
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
16:57:51 UTC
01:57:51 KST

WhatsApp on Wednesday started rolling out a much-sought-after feature: the ability to schedule messages in both individual and group chats.
The feature is rolling out globally to all users across iOS and Android. Once it becomes available, you can type a message and long-press the send button to schedule it. You can choose to send a message anywhere between 10 minutes and 14 days later.
Users will be able to see all their scheduled messages and delete them if needed. It’s not clear if there is a way to edit the schedule of a message.
Notably, scheduled messages will be sent even if your phone is off or outside network coverage. You can schedule messages containing text, photos, and videos.
The feature could prove useful for wishing someone a happy birthday on time or chatting with people across time zones. Businesses may also find it easier to schedule texts and updates to their customers, and people who use the app to coordinate work will likely find themselves using scheduled texts to update their teams.
WhatsApp rivals like Telegram and Signal have had the ability to schedule messages for a while now, so the Meta-owned chat app is late in rolling it out.

Anthropic migrated Messages API to Rust with no Rust developers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
16:28:28 UTC
01:28:28 KST

Many problems have a generator–verifier gap: it’s often far easier to verify that a solution is correct than to come up with one. Sudoku is a classic example. It’s simple to check the solution (just count the numbers in each row, column, and box), but finding that solution can take a long time. Many ambitious Claude projects have relied on this gap: formalizing proofs in mathematics or migrating large, well-tested codebases are tasks Claude can take on as long as it has a reliable way to verify its work.
But as Claude has become more capable, it’s begun to take on projects in live systems, where generating the work is easier than verifying it. In these systems, users often rely on edge cases and quirks that fall outside the test suite, and it’s hard to write a definition of “correct” that covers everything. Without a reliable way to check Claude’s work, the migration of a live system is hard to trust—however quickly Claude can generate the code for it.
We ran into this problem when we migrated our Messages API from Python to Rust. This API is the primary way that customers—as well as our own apps, like Claude Code and Cowork—reach Claude. From early 2024 to fall 2026, API traffic grew roughly 1,800 times. Rust would allow us to use far less CPU per request. Claude wrote a first version of the API’s core request path in about a week. But building trust in the migration required us to find a way to verify that our Rust API was at parity with our production API. This proved hard for several reasons, including that the Python API continued changing throughout the migration, so parity was a moving target; Claude can reply nondeterministically to the same request, so a difference between two outputs didn’t necessarily mean a bug; and we couldn't read customer requests, so we weren't able to easily see where the two versions diverged.
In this post, we share how we turned the production Messages API itself into a verifier—and how that helped us test the new Rust version on production traffic, find where it differed, and automatically dispatch agents to fix those differences. This allowed us to ship an API on Rust that uses about 70% less CPU per request and cuts median time to first token by about 20% without ever taking the service offline or freezing feature development.
Why migrate?
We built the Messages API in 2023 and wrote it in Python, which was widely used internally for much of our research code. At first, it was simple. The Messages API passed requests to the model and sent back what the model wrote. (One engineer at the time called it “basically a request forwarder.”) But as the Messages API became more capable and deeply integrated with our codebases, it also developed shared dependencies with Python libraries that were owned by our research and inference teams, which made it hard to move. If we ever wanted to move to another language, it would mean building logging, metrics, and tools all over again.
This calculus gradually began to change. By late 2025, every Messages API request had to be validated, converted into the prompt, tokenized, and screened by our safety classifiers. As each reply arrived, the stream of tokens had to be sorted into text, thinking, and tool calls, and Python added overhead to every one of those steps each time it created an object, freed memory, or handed its work to the next task. Traffic eventually grew far beyond what the initial Python service was built for (Figure 1), and we quickly needed to find a way to make the service much more efficient.
Incremental or all at once?
Full rewrites of large production systems are often approached cautiously, for at least two reasons. First, there is no guarantee that test suites cover 100% of production code behavior, and rewriting it is risky. Second, the sheer effort required often makes such work intractable. (In 2024, after one of our engineers suggested migrating to Go or Rust, another temporarily settled the question: “We are not rewriting the API in go or rust!”)
The standard advice if you’re looking for performance gains is thus to rewrite only the parts that are slowing the system down, so that’s where we started. In Rust, we rewrote the code that parses a model’s output and called it from Python. This reduced the API’s CPU usage by about 8% to 10%. However, incremental solutions often incur added complexity, and it’s important to make sure that incremental improvements justify those costs. In our case, the gains didn’t justify the added complexity. Each token now crossed between Python and Rust three times (Figure 2), creating new sources of overhead.
As Claude became increasingly capable, we began to question whether the usual reasons to avoid a full migration still held. The effort required to undertake the project was dropping fast: by May 2026, Claude was writing more than 80% of the code Anthropic merged. We wondered if Claude could now help us write code that would empirically capture every behavior and fix in the production system. But the question became: how could we verify that the behavior of our migration was correct? Verification, not generation, had become the bottleneck.
How we verified the migration
General approaches
To verify the migration, we first needed to define what “correct” meant. You can do this in a few ways (Figure 3). If you’re lucky, you can simply write a rule. A theorem in mathematics is a precise statement, and a proof assistant (like Lean) can check each step of a proof against a fixed set of rules. Other times—and more frequently in software engineering—you define “correct” with a set of examples, like tests. For instance, when Bun moved from Zig to Rust, its test suite was written in TypeScript, so the same tests could judge both versions. In our case, neither approach was sufficient, so we used the existing system as an oracle to test parity during the migration. In this case, our existing system (the Messages API) kept changing. We were releasing new models and shipping new features regularly, which made the definition of “correct” a moving target.
Tests
Tests were the easiest place for us to start, since we already had an existing set of about 700 integration tests. However, two problems limited our ability to trust them to verify the correctness of a migration as high stakes as this (Figure 4).
The tests were coupled to the Python implementation. An integration test sends a request, then checks the reply and what was sent to other services. Our tests ran the Python API inside the test’s process and mocked services, like billing and rate limiting, in Python’s memory. The tests had to change along with the code, and Claude helped with both. Now, a “pass” could mean that the code was right, or that the test had been changed in a way that let wrong code through. We saw this happen: when we audited the ported tests, we found more than 100 weakened assertions.
The tests were incomplete. The Messages API has dozens of features, and there are far too many configurations to test them exhaustively. Just 20 binary options make over a million combinations—that translates to 12 days of tests at a second each, for one model, before we even vary the prompts inside them. In practice, engineers test a subset that tries to approximate production, but that process inevitably misses cases. Some projects, like Bun, have an independent suite that’s extensive enough to judge a rewrite. Many production services don’t; what counts as correct depends as much on how they behave as on what’s in their tests or documentation.
Detecting parity with production
Production data is sometimes “the only true test” that new code behaves like the old code. Running requests through old and new code and comparing the results is often called shadowing. To understand what we needed to compare, it helps to walk through what happens to a single request on the Messages API (Figure 5). The API validates each request, converts it into a prompt a model can read (the prompt for inference), tokenizes it, and runs it through safety classifiers. A model then writes a reply, one token at a time. On the output side, the API sorts those tokens into text, thinking, and tool calls, streams them to the customer, and counts usage for limits and billing.
The migration covered both the input and output paths. Some differences were obvious, such as when the Rust API rejected a request that Python had accepted. But many were subtle. For instance, if the Rust API left out part of a prompt, the request would still succeed and the model would still answer, though sometimes with minor differences.
Our Rust API passed about 98% of its tests in April, yet only about a quarter of inputs matched on production traffic (Figure 6). The gap came partly from cases in production traffic that our tests didn’t cover. For example, when Python writes JSON tool calls, it escapes certain characters (e.g., an emoji) by replacing them with text codes. Rust wrote those characters as is, so the two prompts differed by a few bytes. Production also weighted bugs differently. In a test suite, each case counts once, but in live traffic, a bug will show up in each request where it’s triggered, which means a single common bug can cause many mismatches.
Common tools for shadowing often assume that a request can safely run twice, or that a service is deterministic. These assumptions didn’t apply in our case. We had to ensure that a request only counted once toward a customer’s limits and bill. In addition, Claude is nondeterministic, meaning that replies can differ for the same input (Figure 7). To address this, we ran the model once and replayed the model output to Rust.
With both versions working from the same model output, we still had to compare every stage before and after the model call, and the results had to match exactly. In many systems, bad input causes an error somewhere down the stack. But since Claude accepts almost any prompt, even a small deviation can change its reply without triggering an error. We compared each step before and after the model call, so that we could pin down any deviation that might change what a customer received.
It was important to us to do this in a way that protected user privacy. To do that we had to work without reading user requests or Claude’s replies to them, which means we couldn’t easily see where the Rust and Python versions of the API differed. So instead of looking at text, we recorded the shape of each difference, such as where it happened in the service and how big it was. We did this using a few different approaches:
- Checkpointing. Both API versions handle a request using the same steps: accept or reject it, resolve its settings, build the prompt. Every step produces some result, such as an error or the pieces of the prompt, that we could compare step by step, and within the prompt, piece by piece. The first place they differed indicated roughly where the migration code differed from production code (Figure 8).
- Fingerprint. Once we knew roughly where each diff resided, we still needed a way to distinguish one bug from another. We recorded where each difference began in the prompt, as well as the length difference between the two versions. Combined with the step and piece of the prompt isolated in checkpointing, we found that bugs presented with a specific fingerprint. That is, one bug tends to leave the same fingerprint on every request it affects (Figure 9). We could then group errors by fingerprint, which helped us triage the fixes.
The result was a shadowing system that produced a small record of each difference between our Rust and Python APIs. Here is an example, with made-up values:
We could then pass this record to an agent to fix it.
Verification loops
Each request that differed left a record in our logs. A single bug could produce hundreds of thousands of nearly identical diff records. Rather than send each to an agent, we ran a script every 10 to 30 minutes that grouped them by which fields differed, then alerted a coordinating Claude agent only when it saw a new kind of record. This coordinator assigned a worker agent to own each new kind of record. It also created a new thread in a shared Slack channel where human engineers could follow along and steer it when necessary.
To tackle the many bugs, we set up a series of verification loops. A fast inner loop gave agents a sandbox in which they could test ideas and propose fixes. A slower outer loop then validated fixes in production. In addition, a memory loop recorded any mistakes along the way, saving them so that agents could learn over time (Figure 10).
Inner loop
When we first started testing this in April, we gave each agent a record that showed where the Rust and Python versions differed. Agents would guess the root cause from the code and open a pull request. Once the fix was reviewed and merged, they deployed it to staging, a preproduction copy of the Rust API where we ran the same shadow comparison on test traffic. The agents checked whether the difference was eliminated. Since every hypothesis needed a review and a deployment, feedback was taking hours or days.
We needed agents to get a signal from verification much sooner, so we moved them into their own workspace. We redesigned the inner loop so that agents could have a place to test ideas quickly and fail safely. Each agent ran in a cloud sandbox with both codebases and fast, local checks for what happens before and after the model call. The input check took a request, built the prompt that each version would send to the model, and showed what differed. Because this check never actually called the model, comparing the versions took under a second. We later added an output check, which ran both versions against a fake model. The output check passed a scripted reply, such as a tool call, through each version and compared the results.
The main limitation was that agents could only see the fingerprint, not the request behind each record. So an agent would hypothesize what had caused the difference, then write its own request or model reply to try to reproduce the difference. Since each agent was writing these itself, it could read both results in full. If the results matched, its guess was wrong and the agent tried again. If they differed the same way the record described, the agent edited the Rust until the two versions matched, then added a test that failed on the old code and passed on the new (Figure 11).
The seam between the inner and outer loops was a good checkpoint for human review. Inside the sandbox, a wrong guess cost an agent only a few seconds and had no impact on users. Agents could iterate on their own. We allowed a fix to cross into the outer loop only after it had undergone PR review; this allowed engineers to focus their attention on proposed fixes (rather than on guesses) and on the questions the verifiers couldn’t answer, such as whether a difference between the Rust and Python APIs was the result of a bug or a deliberate change.
Agents posted their fixes and evidence to their Slack threads for engineers to review. In mid-May, one of the largest remaining differences between the Rust and Python APIs came from web search. The production API retrieved the web search tool’s description from an internal service. The Rust API didn’t call this service yet, so the tool description was missing from the prompts. An agent proposed a shortcut to copy the text into files that would be readable by Rust. The prompts then matched, and the fix passed the local verifier. But an engineer turned it down because it didn’t solve the problem: Rust would still need to call the service before launch, at which point the workaround would be thrown out (Figure 12).
But the verifier rewarded matching outputs, and the agent found the cheapest way to produce them. A verifier can tell an agent whether the outputs matched, but not whether the fix is good enough to ship. That’s why human review was an important gate.
Outer loop
In the inner loop, agents debugged differences and proposed fixes. At the seam between the inner loop and the outer loop, engineers reviewed the proposed fixes, gave feedback, and approved them to deploy. The outer loop then compared Rust API deployments with and without each fix.
To make that comparison fair, we logged facts about each request we compared, but not its text, including which model and API features it used—for example, prompt caching, tools, or extended thinking. These details let agents compare requests of the same kind (Figure 13) and ask how often the bug’s fingerprint appeared on builds with and without the fix. This way, agents could monitor the new builds for the bug.
We designed the outer loop to catch problems that the inner loop missed. The inner loop simulated production quickly but imperfectly: agents could test a hypothesis in seconds, but only on requests they had written, and with a local verifier that wasn’t always configured exactly like production.
This means that sometimes a fix passed in the sandbox and production still showed the difference. The agent assigned to the bug would either see the difference in the new build’s records, or be alerted by the coordinating agent. It then went back to the inner loop and used that evidence to form a new hypothesis.
Memory loop
The inner and outer loops fixed one difference at a time. The memory loop improved how agents made those fixes. We didn’t want engineers to have to make the same correction twice, so we recorded what came out of reviews and agents’ own work and applied it to every agent that followed.
For example, when an engineer turned down the web search shortcut (Figure 12), the agent saved a note to memory explaining the decision, the reason for it, and the correct fix, which was to have Rust call the service that supplies tool descriptions.
Building that call took our team weeks, and in the meantime the bug kept producing records. Some had new fingerprints, because the missing text fell at other positions in the prompt or belonged to another tool that used the same service. Agents checked the memory note, recognized the known bug, and linked those records to it, instead of writing another workaround (Figure 14).
Other lessons became general rules for porting and writing Rust. After one agent guessed at Python’s behavior, an engineer made it a rule to port exactly what Python did. An agent then compared the existing code with that rule and found seven more places where the port had relied on a guess.
In June, an agent also went through about 60 of the team’s review comments, grouped the recurring problems and proposed rules, which an engineer edited and approved for CLAUDE.md (Figure 14).
The memory loop also encoded some lessons in permanent checks. This included new tests that failed when Rust drifted from Python, lints that banned patterns reviewers kept flagging, and later a merge check that required anyone changing the Python code to make a matching change in Rust or request an exemption.
Looking ahead
It’s increasingly feasible to migrate production systems with Claude, either for better performance or security, without taking an API offline or slowing feature development. Now that the hard part is no longer writing new code, we need to build sufficient confidence that the new system behaves like the old one.
The usual approach to migration is a bit like rebuilding the Ship of Theseus, in which the old ship was replaced plank by plank. Generally it’s been possible to replace planks while the ship still sails, but changing too many planks at once requires coming into dock. To fully stretch the metaphor, Claude now makes it possible to build an identically functioning ship alongside the first—even as the first keeps sailing and having its planks replaced. Agents kept the new ship in step as the old one changed, and we kept checking that the two behaved the same. Once we trusted that they did, we moved the passengers over a few at a time. The new ship sails faster and more efficiently than the old one.
Lessons learned
Verification is the new bottleneck. Previously, large software projects like migrations were hard to justify because they took too much effort and carried too much risk. But if writing code is no longer the bottleneck, the main constraint becomes how quickly you can verify results. In general, ambition can be increased as long as there’s a way for Claude to verify correctness.
Verification is how you earn confidence. In math, we use a proof assistant to verify that each step of a proof Claude wrote is valid. For software, tests or formal verification can help us gain similar confidence. But when these checks aren’t enough, a production system itself can also be a verifier.
Carefully design the verification signal. A verifier provides a signal that Claude can act on. We can calibrate this signal to show Claude where things went wrong. Where a proof assistant points to the failing step or a test names the failing case, a production system often offers no such signal by default. Much of our effort for this project went to building such a signal. In this case, checkpoints located each difference in the code and fingerprints helped tell one bug from another.
Build loops that let Claude autonomously act on the signal. Our inner loop was fast and ran locally in a sandbox. Agents tested hypotheses in seconds using requests they wrote themselves. The outer loop was slower and checked fixes on shadowed production traffic. The outermost loop was just prose, with corrections saved as rules in memory that could be read by every agent later in the process. Together, these loops allowed us to migrate the Messages API to Rust, which uses about 70% less CPU per request and reduces median time to first token by about 20% (Figure 15).
Keep the loops simple, so they scale with Claude’s capability. We tried to put human judgment only into what counted as correct; otherwise we left it up to Claude how to get there. Agents chose which hypotheses to test, how to reproduce each bug, and how to fix it. The verifiers judged the result. Simple loops around strong verifiers allowed us to move productively over time (Figure 16).
Move toward outer loops. At first, we had our engineers working inside the inner loop, running Claude and chasing differences themselves. Later, they moved to the seam between loops, helping to steer agents and reviewing fixes in Slack. But as the system matured, they increasingly moved to the edge, where they could provide the highest leverage, writing the rules and checks that agents worked within. As Claude grew more intelligent (and as we grew more intelligent at working with it), we evolved from directing Claude to Claude directing itself, relying on humans mainly for important judgment calls.

10 Brilliant Breakfast Ideas for Thanksgiving Leftovers

Not Lunch, Not Dinner: 10 Brilliant Breakfast Ideas That Reimagine Thanksgiving Leftovers
After the Thanksgiving feast, chances are good you’ve got a fridge full of the real treat: leftovers! While they’re easy to enjoy as lunch or dinner (or snacks), we’re actually big fans of revamping leftovers into breakfast (think: hearty omelets stuffed with turkey and cheese, or grab-and-go breakfast bars studded with cranberries). From savory to sweet, here are 10 versatile ways to work Thanksgiving leftovers into your morning meal.
1. Scoop leftover pumpkin purée into oatmeal or pancakes.
With oatmeal, just fold a spoonful right into your bowl. (Or, if you have an entire extra can of pumpkin, toss it into this big-batch slow cooker version.) The batter behind these fluffy and festive pumpkin pancakes also packs a cup of leftover purée.
Get the recipes: Slow Cooker Creamy Pumpkin Spice Oatmeal & Pumpkin Pie Pancakes
2. Top day-old stuffing with eggs for an easy breakfast hash.
The fastest way to turn something into breakfast? Crown it with fried eggs. Reheat (and re-crisp!) the stuffing on the stovetop, then use that same pan to fry up a batch of perfectly runny eggs.
Get the recipe: Bread, Sausage, and Apple Hash
3. Or make a hash using leftover greens as the base.
We also love eggs over leftover Brussels sprouts and bacon — especially in this version, which cooks everything on a sheet pan for maximum ease.
Get the recipe: Sheet Pan Brussels Sprouts, Eggs, and Bacon from Damn Delicious
4. Swirl cranberry sauce into Greek yogurt for quick parfaits.
If you’re looking for a lighter day-after-Thanksgiving option, these refreshing and slightly tart parfaits are ideal. Start with Greek yogurt and cranberry sauce, then toss in granola and chia seeds for more protein and fiber.
Get the recipe: Breakfast Parfaits With Cranberry Sauce from Well Plated
5. Make savory waffles out of mashed potatoes, cheddar, and panko breadcrumbs.
Who knew mashed potatoes could have such a delicious second life? Just mix everything together with some eggs, milk, and spices, then run it through a waffle maker. 
Get the recipe: Cheesy Potato Waffles from Diethood
6. Or turn leftover mash into crispy potato cakes.
If you don’t feel like breaking out the waffle-maker, try this slightly more streamlined version. Top with smoked salmon and a poached egg for an extra-savory treat.
Get the recipe: Cheesy Leftover Mashed Potato Cakes from The Endless Meal
7. Shred turkey and tuck it into easy, cheesy omelets.
Serve them with a side of bacon and fresh greens and you’ve got a fast, satisfying breakfast.
Get the recipe: How To Make a French Omelette
8. Fold cranberry sauce into oatmeal bars.
These bake up in just about half an hour — and can pull double duty as both breakfast or a grab-and-go snack later in the day.
Get the recipe: Cranberry Oatmeal Bars from Savory Tooth
9. Sneak sweet potatoes into pancakes.
There’s a cup of mashed sweet potatoes in the batter, but you can’t really taste them outright in the final product. So if you’re looking for a more low-key way to use up leftovers — or if you just want to sneak extra veggies into breakfast — try these.
Get the recipe: Sweet Potato Pancakes
10. Make a frittata with turkey and sweet potatoes — or just about anything else.
We’ve written before about how frittatas are one of our favorite ways to use up any leftover meat or vegetables in our fridge, and that’s especially true on the morning after Thanksgiving. Toss in turkey or ham, sweet potatoes, or spinach — and know that you can’t go wrong.
Get the recipe: Leftover Turkey and Sweet Potato Frittata from Skinnytaste

Nothing is showing off a much more detailed look at its upcoming AI earbuds ahead of their December 8th release. The Ear Note buds can record your calls, while their charging case can record, transcribe, and summarize your conversations.
Nothing offers a full look at its AI earbuds and charging case
The Ear Note buds look similar to Nothing’s previous earbuds, but now their case doubles as an AI-connected note taker for conversations.
Like the Ear 3, the Ear Note’s microphone-equipped case comes with a “Talk” button that you can use to record your own voice. There’s also a new “Record” button that can record and transcribe your conversations. In a post on X, Nothing CEO Carl Pei says the button will allow you to record calls through the earbuds, and meetings through the case as a “first step toward a hardware context layer for AI.”
As previously reported by Bloomberg, the buds will connect to an app where you can manage recordings, transcriptions, and AI summaries. They’ll also “support interactions with various AI agents, including a more basic voice-control system developed by Nothing, letting users engage in back-and-forth conversations,” according to Bloomberg.
The Ear Note buds themselves look similar to the Ear 3 and Ear 3A, but come with Nothing’s “best in-ear audio to date.” The new device, which Nothing teased earlier this month, will join some of the other AI-powered note-taking devices we’ve seen emerge recently, such as the Amazon Bee and the Plaud Note Pro. Apple also brought AI capabilities to its Watch Series 12, allowing Siri to listen to your conversations and generate recaps.

Google Wants To Make a 24/7 AI TV Channel. You'll Be the Main Character. (404media.co) 67
			
		 	
				Longtime Slashdot reader schwit1 shares a report from 404 Media: Google has filed for a patent that would use the information it knows about you to create an AI-generated TV channel full of personalized "shows" starring you and featuring customized advertisements. The patent is the latest signal of the AI video endgame: endless highly specific, customized content that is automatically generated based on the wealth of data companies and advertisers collect about you.
Tech companies regularly file for patents that they don't end up actually putting into real products, but this one, called "creating a channel of video experiences tailored to a user," describes a system that many AI companies have been clearly trying to build. It imagines something called "My Channel," which would be filled with AI content generated based on demographic data, their video watch history (presumably from YouTube), and other information it knows about the user. It would then make "the user as the protagonist for the whole video." The patent was first noticed by Patentlyze, a news site that tracks tech company patents, who shared it with 404 Media.
"You are a customized video generating model. Generate a customized video for a user where the user will play the protagonist," a hypothetical LLM prompt in the patent reads. "The generated customized video includes characteristics based on the . The generated customized video follows the style and format of the  of the user. The generated customized video sticks to the  of the user but the narrative for the generated customized video is customized. The  and  parameters are ordered from more preferred to less preferred."

This Spoon Trick Keeps a Leftover Bottle of Champagne Just as Bubbly
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

This Spoon Trick Basically Keeps a Leftover Bottle of Champagne Just as Bubbly as When You Pop It
The holidays are here, and that calls for Champagne. I’m not one to leave a bottle of bubbly only half-drunk, but sometimes I’m in the mood for a Champagne cocktail. Delicious, yes. But it always leaves me with a dilemma: What do I do with the rest of the bottle? It’s hard to keep Champagne or Prosecco from going flat unless you have one of those nifty Champagne bottle savers.
If you don’t? Well, I’ve tried wrapping my opened bottles in plastic wrap before and have even shoved the popped cork back into the bottle, which we all know won’t work. So when I saw this video that claimed that the trick to keeping your drink carbonated is just to add a silver spoon in it, I had to try it out. Could it really have been this simple all along?
How to Store Champagne with a Silver Spoon
When you’ve finished sipping on Champagne or Prosecco for the evening, drop the handle of a silver spoon into the bottle. Make sure to use real silver and not stainless steel. You don’t have to wrap or seal the lid of the bottle. Then, place the Champagne in the fridge exactly as it is, until you need it next (preferably in the next few days).
Did Storing Champagne with a Silver Spoon Work?
I was very skeptical about this trick working. I mean, how can a spoon keep a bottle of bubbly from going flat? And what I was really confused about is how the bottle stays unopened and unsealed the whole time. But to my surprise, this trick actually worked!
I took photos when pouring the first glass after I removed the silver spoon to prove that the Prosecco was still very carbonated. It appeared exactly like the glass I had poured for myself the night before, when I had first opened the bottle. I also took photos after all the foam settled to show that there were carbonation bubbles in the liquid itself.
Of course, I had to give the glass a sip to see if it still tasted bubbly and didn’t just look that way. I will admit that it tasted slightly less carbonated, which is to be expected. You can’t put the toothpaste back in the tube, and I guess the same can be said about some bubbly. I would absolutely still drink this Prosecco, but it just doesn’t taste as fresh as the freshly popped stuff. It’s a great trick if you’d like to have another glass or two the following day and don’t care if it’s slightly less carbonated. Or if you’re mixing up cocktails it will be less noticeable.
We actually tried this trick a few years ago and tested it against an open bottle with no spoon and an open bottle with a steel spoon. To my surprise, the bottle without a spoon scored the highest, to this writer. I do think the spoon trick left the drink better than some bottles I’ve stored in my fridge without anything, so despite their findings I will be keeping this trick in my back pocket all hosting season long.
It is worth mentioning that this trick has been debunked by a few sources in the past, and some people even say the real factor in keeping drinks bubbly is never letting them warm up. I’ll have to see the difference between a bottle left out and one stored the whole time in the fridge next!
