Run with the Semi-Wild Yilki Horses of Anatolia in Nur Tucker’s Captivating Photos
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
15:56:17 UTC
00:56:17 KST

Run with the Semi-Wild Yilki Horses of Anatolia in Nur Tucker’s Captivating Photos
Around the dusty plains and otherworldly volcanic rock formations of the historical region of Cappadocia, a millennia-old tradition continues. Here in Central Anatolia, Turkey, horses have been a part of daily life, civic programs, and military campaigns since prehistory. In The Untold Story of the Yilki Horse, photographer Nur Tucker captures a portrait of the region’s living heritage.
Published by Hemeria, the title delves into the beauty and tenacity of the semi-wild horses while also highlighting the lifestyles of those who herd them through rugged landscapes. Tucker, who is a British-Turkish photographer based in the U.K., has long been drawn to the vigor and elegance of horses, and the yilki breed that populates Anatolia have a particularly resonant history.
“Yilki” comes from the Turkish verb yılmak, which means “to flee” or “to be set loose.” The name “has come to describe the condition of these herds that live between liberation and loss, between domestic heritage and wild abandon,” says a statement.
In a place where horses have been bred for agriculture, transportation, battle, and more for thousands of years, their role forever changed within just the past century due to mechanization and industrialization. With motorized tractors, trucks, and cars to work the fields and provide transportation, horses essentially became redundant. Released to fend for themselves, they survived harsh winters, bred on their own, and grew into sizable herds. In The Untold Story of the Yilki Horse, Tucker composes a love letter to these striking animals, celebrating their resilience through more than 100 images.
The book is available for purchase directly from Hemeria, and it’s slated for worldwide release next October. See more on Tucker’s Instagram.

====================================================================================================

In C++, = default and {} are not the same constructor
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
15:20:15 UTC
00:20:15 KST

2 November 2026 · 3 min
You can write an empty default constructor as span() = default or as span() {}. It can have remarkable effects on performance.
struct span {
  const char *p;
  int len;
  const char *end;
  span() = default; // or span() {}
  span(const char *s, int n) : p(s), len(n), end(s + n) {}
};
The constructor span() = default on its first declaration makes span trivial. The constructor span() {} is user-provided, so span is not trivial.
An example
This span is a start pointer, a length, and an end pointer. A box holds one span. The expression box() value-initializes that span. The loop assigns a value-initialized box when i reaches n, and otherwise a span of length i + 1:
struct box {
  span s;
  box() : s() {}
  box(span in) : s(in) {}
};
void consume(box &);
void walk(const char *s, int n) {
  for (int i = 0;; i++) {
    box b;
    b = i == n ? box() : box(span(s, i + 1));
    if (i == n) { return; }
    consume(b);
  }
}
The box() path runs once, as the loop exits, and that span is not passed to consume.
With span() {}, GCC 16 (-O2 -march=x86-64) writes the three fields directly where consume expects them:
.L4:
    mov eax, ebx
    mov QWORD PTR [rsp+16], rbx
    mov rdi, rsp
    add rbx, 1
    sub eax, ebp
    mov QWORD PTR [rsp], rbp
    mov DWORD PTR [rsp+8], eax
    call    consume(box&)
    cmp rbx, r12
    jne .L4
With span() = default, GCC 16 stores the end pointer, rbx, straight into the box. The start pointer and the length go through a temporary, and the first 16 bytes of that temporary are copied back.
.L3:
    mov eax, ebx
    mov QWORD PTR [rsp+32], rbp
    mov rdi, rsp
    sub eax, ebp
    mov QWORD PTR [rsp+16], rbx
    add rbx, 1
    mov DWORD PTR [rsp+40], eax
    movdqa  xmm0, XMMWORD PTR [rsp+32]
    movaps  XMMWORD PTR [rsp], xmm0
    call    consume(box&)
    cmp rbx, r12
    jne .L3
I time the best of five runs of 100 million spans, on one core of an Intel Xeon Gold 6548N (Emerald Rapids), with -O2 -march=x86-64. My code is available.
With GCC 15 and GCC 16, the = default version is over four times slower.
Daniel Lemire, "In C++, = default and {} are not the same constructor," in Daniel Lemire's blog, November 2, 2026, https://lemire.me/blog/2026/11/02/default-and-braces-are-not-the-same-constructor/.
[BibTeX]

A few questions for the guy who went snorkeling in a Home Depot retention pond
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
15:14:32 UTC
00:14:32 KST

One night earlier this fall, a biologist and YouTuber in Florida named Joseph Ricketts went snorkeling in a retention pond next to his local Home Depot.
A few questions for the guy who went snorkeling in a Home Depot retention pond
He found it teeming with life and beauty.
He filmed what he saw and posted a video to his channel titled “Night Snorkel In a Home Depot Retention Pond.” It quickly blew up, hitting more than 2 million views as of this writing, and for good reason: It’s not at all what you might expect.
Ricketts found an aquatic world teeming with life on his dive into what is, from the surface, a stagnant pool of water filled with old beer cans. A water snake undulated through the pond, a bulge in its stomach revealing a recent meal. Tadpoles darted about, some of them half on their way to froghood. Mysterious shrimp scuttled along the silty bottom.
Ricketts, who works as a crocodile and alligator research biologist in Florida, went for his first snorkeling trip when he was 16 years old. On that trip, he came face-to-face with a manatee — and was instantly hooked. “I experienced so much joy in that moment, and I just kind of subconsciously knew I’d be chasing that for the rest of my life to some degree or another,” he told me.
Ricketts still takes a lot of joy in snorkeling, in part because of how it allows him to quickly access a totally different world hiding just under the surface of the water; his YouTube bio declares, “I’ll snorkel pretty much anywhere.” And his recent retention pond dive among the suburban detritus bears that out.
I talked with Ricketts recently about urban ecology, snorkeling, and what motivates a person to jump into the pond behind the Home Depot three miles from his house (his shortest-ever commute to a dive yet). Our interview has been edited for length and clarity.
Why snorkeling?
That’s a good question. I’m scuba certified, and I enjoy anything underwater, but snorkeling is just super accessible. I have a camera housing and a wet suit and other gear, but really all you need is just a mask and snorkel. As long as the water’s not freezing cold, you can get in there and take a look around. I really enjoy how, as soon as you immerse your head, it feels like you’re in a totally different world.
But the beautiful thing is that you’re not. You’re in the same world, it’s just this is the one that you didn’t have a vision into before. It’s kind of a meditative, calming practice. Just taking a look around at what is happening in nature and how things survive in their own environment is pretty awesome. I love it.
Okay, but why this pond?
I honestly had the idea for it a couple years ago. I’d see people out there birding with binoculars and telephoto lenses, and I started wondering if I could snorkel in there. We’ve had a drought so I couldn’t do it for a really long time, and when we finally got some rain I decided to go for it.
Tell me about the retention pond. What is it — and why is it in this Home Depot parking lot?
Florida has a lot of low-level, very wet natural ecosystems, but it’s also an extremely developed state. There are strip malls and roads and concrete everywhere! Natural wetland environments can soak up intense stormwater, but when you totally change environments and put in shopping centers and neighborhoods and pave everything with asphalt, you kind of lose that ability. That stormwater needs a place to go and collect — or it’s just going to flood parking lots and stores and houses.
So they have to put these retention ponds in; nowadays, you can’t build in certain areas unless you put in a retention pond. Not far from the Home Depot is a new-build neighborhood, and they have a retention pond that they had to put in, but they set it up with a little walkway for people that live in the neighborhood. That’s nice because there’s some wildlife that’s able to survive and live there, and people can enjoy this sort-of natural area. But it’s also not as good as the original thing that may have been there, you know?
So you were snorkeling in what’s essentially a replacement for a wetland that might once have been there.
I don’t know the history of that area exactly, but I imagine there was some form of wetland.
What was the most surprising thing that you found?
One thing that I found was a tadpole shrimp, which people call triops. It’s a freshwater crustacean that apparently is not native to Florida. When I saw it underwater, I had no context for what it is. As a biologist, people assume you just know wildlife, but people forget how much wildlife there is in the world! I know a lot about alligators and crocodiles, but there’s still a ton I don’t know. I think it’s important to sometimes be able to be like: “I don’t know what I’m seeing or why I’m seeing it” — and then hopefully I can learn about it.
But anyway, I saw this thing, I had no clue what it was, and I asked a bunch of my coworkers about it but they had no clue what it was either. So then I had to do some research online, which is how I finally figured it out.
“I think that was kind of a personal challenge I had for myself: Can I see something beautiful in a retention pond?”
Apparently they are native to the Midwest. They lay their eggs in soil, and then during times of rain, they really quickly hatch and feed and grow, and then they reproduce, lay more eggs, and then wait for the next cycle while it’s dry. It’s a real quick boom-and-bust life cycle. It’s not a native species, but I think there’s just a couple areas in this state where you can find them — and one just happens to be right there in the retention pond.
I saw that there were also more common animals there, like frogs, snakes, and dragonflies. But how do you think they all got into this parking-lot pond?
A lot of frog species adapt to urban environments and find any bit of water they can. They were probably used to a forest and wetland, and now they’ve had to adapt to what’s available currently, which is pretty limited.
I think that’s true for most of the animals you find in there. The natural world is pretty resilient, though sometimes I think we give it too much credit for how resilient it can be.
What do you mean by that?
Life survives, but the way things originally were does not necessarily survive, and I think that’s something that’s really worth protecting. The way some people interpreted the video was like: ”Wow, look at all this life persisting even in this place!” And it was amazing and there is beauty there, but you can’t just accept that and then get rid of all our natural wetlands. We need untouched, expansive ecosystems.
It can be cool to be excited about this little retention pond and what lives in here, but coupled with that also needs to be excitement for protecting huge untouched habitat as well. Both are full of worthy things.
Do you think the retention pond, now that it’s there, is worth preserving?
I think so. One thing I didn’t really get to dive into with the video too much is the idea of a nature desert. I don’t know if I’m making that term up, but it’s kind of related like a food desert: Depending on your demographic or where you’re born, that might be the only bit of nature you have access to. It’s a privilege to be able to travel to a national park or a big state park or something, and not everyone has that opportunity. Your only exposure to nature could be a retention pond that’s in the middle of a city somewhere. And so I do think those places are very worth protecting, because I think it’s important that regardless of your background, if you’re a kid, you have a place that you can go to and feel safe and be exposed to wilderness even if it’s very small urban wilderness.
Do you think people should be snorkeling in more retention ponds?
[Laughs] I don’t recommend that people do that! There are some retention ponds where you could encounter some nasty stuff.
It was definitely a little bit of a stunt. But I also want to help people think about ecological ideas that they might not otherwise think of, while still enjoying the watch.
Another thing I want to get at is so many people responded like: “That’s gross, how could you do that?” But my question is why are we just totally okay with having these huge bodies of water that are gross and contaminated in our everyday lives? Clean water should be the norm!
Still, it’s a place that should be valued and thought of differently.
Do you feel like your own perception of urban ecology or urban environments changed after this?
I think so. That’s another reason why I love snorkeling: I feel like somehow each time I do it, it changes me a little bit. I didn’t know that much life was out there, and I didn’t know what a lot of those creatures were when I first got in. It deepens my personal connection with my local Home Depot in a way I hadn’t really thought would happen five years ago, but here we are.
Do you think you’ll do more urban snorkeling? I know you just went to the Dry Tortugas, which is a very different environment from Home Depot.
I think I am going to look into it. I’m not going to become the retention pond snorkeling guy; I hope to diversify my snorkeling more than that. But I definitely think this is an idea that resonates with people, and it gives me an opportunity to explore some interesting ecological and even philosophical concepts.
I’ve got some other ideas that I’ve been waiting for the right time to film. There’s a place up in the Carolinas that’s a Frisbee golf course with a little creek running through it, and there are these amazing minnow spawning events that happen in the creek, so maybe one day I’ll be out there in the creek filming minnow spawning while people are playing Frisbee golf nearby.
What are you hoping people take away from the video?
One thing that I hope people take away from a lot of my videos is just a sense of wonder at what’s out there in the natural world, and maybe a broader definition of beauty. I think beauty can be found in unexpected places and from unexpected creatures. I think that was kind of a personal challenge I had for myself: Can I see something beautiful in a retention pond? And do I have the skill with my camera to present that so that maybe other people catch a little glimmer of it too? And I think it happened, which is really exciting.
But at the same time, I hope people realize how important it is that big expansive habitats are protected and conserved too, and that it’s not just about the big animals like polar bears and manatees, which are kind of easy to care about, but also little rare invertebrates or spiders or snakes, all of which I believe have inherent value and are worth protecting.

====================================================================================================

Your only exposure to nature could be a retention pond that’s in the middle of a city somewhere. And so I do think those places are very worth protecting, because I think it’s important that regardless of your background, if you’re a kid, you have a place that you can go to and feel safe and be exposed to wilderness even if it’s very small urban wilderness.
Do you think people should be snorkeling in more retention ponds?
[Laughs] I don’t recommend that people do that! There are some retention ponds where you could encounter some nasty stuff.
It was definitely a little bit of a stunt. But I also want to help people think about ecological ideas that they might not otherwise think of, while still enjoying the watch.
Another thing I want to get at is so many people responded like: “That’s gross, how could you do that?” But my question is why are we just totally okay with having these huge bodies of water that are gross and contaminated in our everyday lives? Clean water should be the norm!
Still, it’s a place that should be valued and thought of differently.
Do you feel like your own perception of urban ecology or urban environments changed after this?
I think so. That’s another reason why I love snorkeling: I feel like somehow each time I do it, it changes me a little bit. I didn’t know that much life was out there, and I didn’t know what a lot of those creatures were when I first got in. It deepens my personal connection with my local Home Depot in a way I hadn’t really thought would happen five years ago, but here we are.
Do you think you’ll do more urban snorkeling? I know you just went to the Dry Tortugas, which is a very different environment from Home Depot.
I think I am going to look into it. I’m not going to become the retention pond snorkeling guy; I hope to diversify my snorkeling more than that. But I definitely think this is an idea that resonates with people, and it gives me an opportunity to explore some interesting ecological and even philosophical concepts.
I’ve got some other ideas that I’ve been waiting for the right time to film. There’s a place up in the Carolinas that’s a Frisbee golf course with a little creek running through it, and there are these amazing minnow spawning events that happen in the creek, so maybe one day I’ll be out there in the creek filming minnow spawning while people are playing Frisbee golf nearby.
What are you hoping people take away from the video?
One thing that I hope people take away from a lot of my videos is just a sense of wonder at what’s out there in the natural world, and maybe a broader definition of beauty. I think beauty can be found in unexpected places and from unexpected creatures. I think that was kind of a personal challenge I had for myself: Can I see something beautiful in a retention pond? And do I have the skill with my camera to present that so that maybe other people catch a little glimmer of it too? And I think it happened, which is really exciting.
But at the same time, I hope people realize how important it is that big expansive habitats are protected and conserved too, and that it’s not just about the big animals like polar bears and manatees, which are kind of easy to care about, but also little rare invertebrates or spiders or snakes, all of which I believe have inherent value and are worth protecting.

Allstate suffers multi-day tech outage ahead of Thanksgiving
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
15:08:37 UTC
00:08:37 KST

U.S. insurer Allstate has been experiencing a tech outage since Monday morning, leaving customers nationwide unable to access their accounts, review their policy information, file claims, pay their bills, and more, just ahead of the U.S. Thanksgiving holidays.
The outage has been impacting both the company’s website and mobile app, TechCrunch confirmed.
The breakdown follows a report from earlier this month that Allstate was planning to lay off up to 10% of its staff as part of an AI-driven efficiency push. (The company had not confirmed the report, which was attributed to unnamed sources.)
Allstate’s outage is the latest in a series of disruptions to services such as Microsoft Outlook and Teams, the airlines, Apple Pay, and several banks. Trump administration officials have said the outages are not being caused by cyberattacks, but are rather being caused by a recent set of upgrades to systems, many of which run on older technology. Those systems are now in need of more frequent security and bug fixes as AI makes it easier and faster to discover vulnerabilities.
At present, a message on Allstate’s website advises customers trying to log in that its services aren’t available, and that the company is “working to restore access as soon as possible.” On X, Allstate is employing what appear to be automated replies to customer complaints, asking them to DM the Allstate account with more information.
Downdetector, a crowdsourced outage-reporting website, shows users began reporting Allstate’s outage from Monday morning. However, the company hasn’t yet issued a formal statement about the disruption, what’s causing it, or when it may be resolved. In the meantime, local insurance agents have been fielding calls and emails from customers who aren’t able to use the company’s online services.
The timing of the outage is particularly bad given that Thanksgiving is a big travel holiday in the U.S., which means there will be car accidents. Customers will be left without a way to get in touch with their auto insurer to file claims and get roadside assistance through Allstate’s app.
Allstate did not immediately return a request for comment. On Wednesday evening, after publication, the company sent the following statement (below). It did not answer questions about the outage’s cause or planned resolution.
Allstate customers can reach us in many ways, including online, by phone and through local offices. Some access has been restricted over the last 48 hours, but we continue to be available for customers and respond to and settle claims. To the extent service interruptions impact a customer’s ability to make a payment, they won’t lose coverage or be charged late fees.
Updated after publication with Allstate’s comment.

I checked Clojure's core with Lean and found unreported bugs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
14:56:52 UTC
23:56:52 KST

I checked Clojure's core with Lean and differential testing and found unreported bugs in HAMT and vector-of
I read Koukyosyumei's article "著名なRust製OSSをLean 4で形式検証してみたら、めちゃめちゃバグが見つかった話" (in Japanese; the title means "I formally verified well-known Rust OSS with Lean 4 and found a lot of bugs"). The author ported well-known Rust projects into a form they could prove in Lean 4, and where a proof failed, they found a bug in the original code.
Many people have used Clojure's persistent data structures since around 1.0, so I expected no bugs to remain. Still, I wanted to see whether Lean would find something there too.
hirosassa/lean-verify describes a procedure for modeling a small function in Lean 4 and checking it for all inputs, so I applied it to the part of Clojure's core written in Java.
I modeled integer and bit arithmetic in Lean and searched for counterexamples.
I checked the collections with differential testing against the java.util collections, and for Java interop I compared the same call with and without a type hint.
I found 17 bugs that I could not find in Clojure's JIRA or on ask.clojure.org.
They include reduce on vector-of, the hash collision node of the HAMT, and calls to functional interfaces without a type hint.
The HAMT itself showed no mismatch in 3 million operations of differential testing, and the vector tree showed none around its boundaries.
The bugs sat in separate code outside the trees, and in places where two mechanisms meet, such as = and hashing.
I used Clojure 1.12.7, released on 2026-11-18, and JDK 21. The links to the source point to the clojure-1.12.7 tag.
How I checked
The lean-verify procedure picks one function and models it as a Lean definition.
You write a property that compares it with a naive specification computed at a wider width, and bv_decide searches for a counterexample.
If you model Java's long and int as BitVec 64 and BitVec 32, the wraparound of addition and multiplication maps onto BitVec operations.
So do the rounding of / toward zero and the sign of %.
When Lean found a counterexample, I turned the value into a Clojure input and reproduced it in the original code. I then modeled the fixed code in Lean and proved the same property. Lean can only take small functions that reduce to integer and bit arithmetic, so I checked the large persistent data structures with differential testing.
Each test applies the same random sequence of operations to PersistentHashMap and to java.util.HashMap and compares the results.
For the HAMT, the keys mix partial collisions at each 5-bit level with fully identical hashes, and I ran 1,500 operations for each of 2,000 seeds.
For vectors, I compared against ArrayList around 32, 1056, 32800, and 1048608, where the tree gains a level.
The operations were conj, pop, assoc, and their transient versions.
I checked whether anyone had reported each bug before. I searched Clojure's JIRA through its REST API and ask.clojure.org through web search and the site's own search, and found no matching report.
reduce on vector-of never finishes
vector-of is a vector that stores its values in primitive arrays.
Clojure implements it in gvec.clj, written in Clojure and separate from PersistentVector.
If you drop from one and then reduce it, the call never returns once the vector has 65505 or more elements.
(reduce + (drop 1 (into (vector-of :long) (range 65504))))
;; => 2145354256
(reduce + (drop 1 (into (vector-of :long) (range 65505))))
;; => never returns
(reduce + (drop 1 (vec (range 65505))))
;; => 2145419760
The cause is line 79 of gvec.clj, where the seq's reduce computes the start of the next 32-element chunk.
(recur result (bit-and 0xffe0 (+ aidx 32)))
The code uses 0xffe0 to clear the low 5 bits and round down to a multiple of 32, but the mask also clears bit 16 and everything above it.
With 65505 elements, the last chunk starts at index 65504.
After that chunk, the start of the next one should be 65536, but the mask turns it into 0, so reduce goes back to the start and reads the vector again and again.
The 0xffe0 mask has stayed in place since the 2010 commit that added internal-reduce.
In Lean, I wrote the property that from a position a that is a multiple of 32, the next chunk starts at a + 32.
import Std.Tactic.BVDecide
def stepImpl (a : BitVec 64) : BitVec 64 := 0xffe0 &&& (a + 32)
def stepFix (a : BitVec 64) : BitVec 64 := (~~~0x1f) &&& (a + 32)
theorem impl_ok (a : BitVec 64) (h32 : a &&& 0x1f = 0) (hlt : a < 0x7fffffe0) :
    stepImpl a = a + 32 := by
  unfold stepImpl; bv_decide
theorem fix_ok (a : BitVec 64) (h32 : a &&& 0x1f = 0) (hlt : a < 0x7fffffe0) :
    stepFix a = a + 32 := by
  unfold stepFix; bv_decide
impl_ok fails with the counterexample a = 1073741792, and #eval stepImpl 65504 returns 0 as well.
fix_ok uses (bit-not 0x1f) as the mask, and its proof goes through under the assumption that a is a multiple of 32 below 0x7fffffe0.
dissoc on a HAMT collision node hides another key
Clojure's hash-map is a HAMT: it splits the key's hash into 5-bit pieces and uses them to walk down the tree.
Keys whose 32-bit hashes are identical share a HashCollisionNode at the bottom of the tree, and the node finds a key among them with =.
HashCollisionNode.without builds the new node with one key removed.
It should give the new node its own hash, this.hash, but it passes the parameter hash instead.
return new HashCollisionNode(null, hash, count - 1, removePair(array, idx/2));
The parameter hash is the hash of the key you pass to dissoc.
If you dissoc with a key that is = to the stored key but has a different hash, the node records the wrong hash.
In Clojure, a vector is = to a Java ArrayList with the same elements, but the two hash differently.
CLJ-1372 tracks this mismatch.
In the example below, I chose a and b so that both ArrayList keys hash to 1003.
(def a (java.util.ArrayList. [0 42]))
(def b (java.util.ArrayList. [1 11]))
[(hash a) (hash b) (hash [0 42]) (= a [0 42])]
;; => [1003 1003 1867155179 true]
(def m3 (-> (hash-map a :A b :B)
            (dissoc [0 42])
            (assoc a :A2)))
[m3 (count m3) (get m3 b)]
;; => [{[1 11] :B, [0 42] :A2} 2 nil]
When you print m3, you can see the key b, but (get m3 b) returns nil.
After the dissoc, the node records 1867155179, the hash of [0 42].
The next assoc then concludes that a, with hash 1003, does not collide with the node, and splits the two into separate branches.
A lookup of b follows the branch for 1003 and finds only a there, so it never reaches b.
In this example the collision node sits on the first level of the tree, so the key passed to dissoc needs the same low 5 bits as 1003, and 1867155179 has them.
disj on a hash-set has the same bug, while the path through the transient dissoc! does not.
I changed that line in the 1.12.7 PersistentHashMap.java to this.hash and compiled it.
With the patched class ahead of the jar on the classpath, the same example returned :B for (get m3 b).
removeIf gives different results with and without a type hint
Since Clojure 1.12, you can pass a Clojure function directly where a Java method takes a functional interface. CLJ-2864 removed the logic that read the function's return value as a Clojure truthy value.
In prior alphas, the FI adapter returns inserted Clojure truthy return logic when adapting a Clojure function to an FI with boolean return.
The compiler follows this for calls where it knows the type.
A call without a type hint goes through reflection and uses Reflector.coerceAdapterReturn.
That method still applies RT.booleanCast to a boolean return value, so the same .removeIf call gives a different result depending on the type hint.
(let [l (java.util.ArrayList. [1 2 3])]
  (.removeIf l #{1 2}) (vec l))
;; => ClassCastException
(let [l (identity (java.util.ArrayList. [1 2 3]))]
  (.removeIf l #{1 2}) (vec l))
;; => [3]
Wrapping the list in identity hides the type of l, so the call goes through reflection.
A set used as a function returns the element itself.
In the call with a known type, the adapter cannot turn the Long into a Boolean and throws.
The reflective call reads the return value as true and removes the elements.
int overflow and a negative subvec index
Clojure 1.12 added IDrop implementations that make drop faster.
The ones for string seqs and vector seqs add the current position and n as ints and then compare the sum with the length.
StringSeq.drop for string seqs is one of them.
(drop Integer/MAX_VALUE (next (seq "abc")))
;; => StringIndexOutOfBoundsException Index -2147483648
(drop Integer/MAX_VALUE (seq "abc"))
;; => ()
The Lean model gave the counterexample i = 2147483646 and n = 2147483647.
I changed the check to compare n with the remaining length s.length() - i.
Under the assumptions 0 < i < s.length()= and 0 < n, Lean proved that the fixed check matches the naive specification for every 32-bit input.
For assoc on the result of subvec, SubVector.assocN does not reject a negative index.
(assoc [2 3] -1 :x)
;; => IndexOutOfBoundsException
(let [s (assoc (subvec [0 1 2 3 4] 2 4) -1 :x)]
  [s (.-v s)])
;; => [[2 3] [0 :x 2 3 4]]
The call overwrites an element of the underlying vector outside the subvec, and returns a subvec that looks unchanged.
CLJ-962 fixed the same bug in nth, but assoc still has it.
Other findings
The other 12 bugs have a smaller impact, and I reproduced each of them on Clojure 1.12.7.
Where I found nothing
PersistentHashMap and PersistentHashSet matched java.util.HashMap over 3 million operations that mixed persistent and transient updates.
The tests also covered maps before an update staying unchanged, ArrayNode splits and merges, and collision nodes down to depth 7.
PersistentTreeMap matched java.util.TreeMap and kept the red-black tree invariants.
Vectors matched ArrayList around every tree boundary I tested.
On the numeric side, Lean proved for 64 bits that the check Numbers uses to pick the fast path for addition and subtraction agrees with the overflow check.
For multiplication, bv_decide timed out at 16 bits, so I checked 8 and 10 bits.
I proved up to 32 bits that clojure.core/mod matches the floored remainder.
Every property I proved in Lean holds for the model shown in this article. I checked that each model means the same thing as the Java code by comparing it with the source and with the reproduced behavior.

====================================================================================================

The year the web industry gave up
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
14:43:41 UTC
23:43:41 KST

The year the web industry gave up
I’ve been drafting my personal “year in review” and one topic stood out as needing its own follow-up. So let’s get that out of the way!
Twenty twenty six, what an uneventful year, eh?
That’s sarcasm. The entire year has been nothing but incessant chatbot addicts toking their token pipes and shouting: “Everything has changed! Don’t get left behind!”
Despite regularly being mistaken for a software “engineer” I am but a humble website developer. And by “website developer” I mean website developer, not React developer; very different. Not that anyone understands this anymore. I’ll be lambasted for making fallacious “no true Scotsman” arguments for asserting that real web dev has actual standards.
Nevertheless, today I’m talking about/to the real web industry.
What’s left of it, anyway.
The web industry
TL;DR: the web industry is dismantling itself from the inside.
Events of the last year have been incomprehensibly stupid. The rapid deskilling and decimation of professional talent is a shock to the system. It’s demoralising to see once-respected peers drop all pretence of care for their profession.
Every week I sigh “et tu, Brute?” as I strike off another peer from my Christmas card list.
Web professionals are cashing in their credibility to shill for the AI industrial complex. People who have spent years, decades in some cases, building a reputation as leaders within the web industry are now destroying what they’ve built. They step on the heads of those who are fighting to stay afloat. I don’t need to name names, they won’t shut up about their chatbots. You know the type. They gush AI platitudes as if the singularity is holding a gun to their head. And they’ve solved everything! Web development? Chatbot. Web design? Chatbot. The websites they’re building? Believe it or not; chatbots.
And what has the web industry actually gained after all this performative slop?
- Entry level jobs have evaporated
- Web development education is in the gutter
- Web infrastructure is getting hammered
It’s embarrassing how much the web industry has lost in so little time. It has cannibalised itself and those responsible seem oblivious to the cause and effect of their actions.
Let’s get one thing absolutely clear: there is no career in prompting. There is no scenario where replacing your work with a chatbot will have a long-term positive impact on your career. Unless you’re into S&M at the beck and call of billionaires. I mean, I don’t want to kink shame, but keep it out off the workplace, please!
The “it’s just a tool” and “inevitable” rhetoric doesn’t cut it. For heaven’s sake, even if you continue to ignore all other moral an ethical issues, at least open your eyes to the absurdity of what’s being sold here. Tokens are not the commodity, you are.
Replacing professionally trained skills with markdown files and ceding control to chatbot subscriptions is not a sustainable future for the web industry. I’m not here to debate whether chatbot-driven web design/development is “good enough”. Although I’ll note that many who claim this are confusing “taste” with the trained talent they’ve driven out of town.

The year the web industry gave up
I’ve been drafting my personal “year in review” and one topic stood out as needing its own follow-up. So let’s get that out of the way!
Twenty twenty six, what an uneventful year, eh?
That’s sarcasm. The entire year has been nothing but incessant chatbot addicts toking their token pipes and shouting: “Everything has changed! Don’t get left behind!”
Despite regularly being mistaken for a software “engineer” I am but a humble website developer. And by “website developer” I mean website developer, not React developer; very different. Not that anyone understands this anymore. I’ll be lambasted for making fallacious “no true Scotsman” arguments for asserting that real web dev has actual standards.
Nevertheless, today I’m talking about/to the real web industry.
What’s left of it, anyway.
The web industry
TL;DR: the web industry is dismantling itself from the inside.
Events of the last year have been incomprehensibly stupid. The rapid deskilling and decimation of professional talent is a shock to the system. It’s demoralising to see once-respected peers drop all pretence of care for their profession.
Every week I sigh “et tu, Brute?” as I strike off another peer from my Christmas card list.
Web professionals are cashing in their credibility to shill for the AI industrial complex. People who have spent years, decades in some cases, building a reputation as leaders within the web industry are now destroying what they’ve built. They step on the heads of those who are fighting to stay afloat. I don’t need to name names, they won’t shut up about their chatbots. You know the type. Believe it or not; chatbots.

- Entry level jobs have evaporated
- Web development education is in the gutter
- Web infrastructure is getting hammered

Unless you’re into S&M at the beck and call of billionaires. I mean, I don’t want to kink shame, but keep it out off the workplace, please!

The Verge’s guide to affordable holiday gifts under $25
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
14:33:07 UTC
23:33:07 KST

Gift-giving on a tight budget is tough, but constraints can also encourage some creative thinking. We thought for a long while (and asked other Verge staffers) about the best items out there, and we’re pretty happy with what we came up with.
The Verge’s guide to affordable holiday gifts under $25
Whether it’s stocking stuffers or a little treat for yourself, these are some of our favorite gifts under $25.
If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.
For this list, we’ve rounded up a barrage of fun gadgets and stocking-stuffer specials. You’ll find nostalgic ways to spruce up a room like Play-Doh’s Blooms, fancy ways to jot down notes like the Pilot Kakuno fountain pen, and plenty of tech-forward extras like the Time Timer Mod. It’s time to get creative with your budget buys, and this list can get you there.
Lamicall magnetic cord organizers
Lamicall’s magnetic cord organizers are simple yet effective tools. They hold the ends of your most frequently used cables so they don’t fall to the floor when you unplug them, and the low-cost accessories can be stuck to the side of your desk or kitchen counter. They also work great behind your entertainment center or wherever else is convenient.
Play-Doh Blooms
There’s regular Play-Doh for kids, but did you know that Play-Doh makes kits for adults, too? We’re not scoffing. The new Blooms sets take on Lego’s floral bouquets with more realistic-looking flower arrangements, and they include a vase. They’re equal parts nostalgic kitsch and stylish decor.
Time Timer Mod (Home Edition)
For visual learners, a visible timeline can help keep the pace moving. The Time Timer Mod provides an aesthetically pleasing 60-minute countdown to help your favorite procrastinator stay on task and maximize efficiency, no matter what they’re working on.
TP-Link Kasa smart plug three-pack
Smart plugs are the easiest way to bring older electronics into the 21st century. For the smart home fan in your life, TP-Link’s Kasa plugs are a brilliant budget-friendly buy, adding Google or Amazon Alexa voice assistance to everyday items, not to mention power schedules and remote control. Note: They support 2.4GHz Wi-Fi connectivity only.
For the giftee who likes to keep things on the level (literally), this striking 12-inch leveling tool offers the traditional bubble measure alongside LED lights that turn from red when the level needs adjusting to green when it locks in. It’s just a slightly more satisfying way to ensure you’re level before drilling or hammering.
Aothia desk mat
Sometimes a little change can make a big difference. Aothia’s desk mats bring aesthetic and functional improvements in an array of stylish color options, with a smooth faux leather surface that protects your desktop and can bring some much-needed order to your desk layout.
These magnetic word tiles are a classic for a reason. Sure, silly phrases or profound thoughts on a fridge door won’t change the world, but they could brighten a housemate’s day. If anything, slowing down a few times a day to read these fun messages as you open the fridge is a welcome break that we all could use.
If you’ve got a scribe on your list who prefers the feel of paper instead of digital screens, give their pencils or fountain pens something to dig into with the Midori MD Note pad. Cream-colored paper, wide lines, and 185-degree binding combine for a great way to jot down anything that comes to mind.
These dimmable, color-changing bulbs offer Wi-Fi connection, simple setup, and Matter compatibility so they can seamlessly fit into Google Home, Amazon Alexa, Samsung SmartThings, and Apple Home smart home systems. Best of all, these smart features come at a price that makes buying multipacks more affordable than other brands.
These Ikea pegboards are a great gift for anyone looking to organize a bedroom, office, or dorm room. They’re available in multiple sizes and colors, and they can be presented in your space in multiple ways, be it mounted on the wall or stood on a desk. Extra accessories make it even easier to organize doodads.
The comedy streaming service created by CollegeHumor alumni offers endless, ad-free entertainment for the improv lover in your life. With multiple shows from some of the top names in comedy, and a business model that encourages profit sharing for creators, it’s a feel-good way to bring more laughter to someone’s life.
Wuben G5 rechargeable flashlight
Everyone needs a good flashlight, and perhaps your recipient’s next one should ditch the replaceable batteries. Wuben’s rechargeable G5 has a pocketable, spy-tech-inspired design, USB-C charging, and adjustable brightness. More importantly, the 400-lumen light is super compact, making it easy to stow or clip onto your bag.
Nalgene Narrow Mouth water bottle (16-ounce)
Not all reusable water bottles are created equal. Give the gift of a narrow-mouthed bottle, like this 16-ounce Nalgene pick, which makes it easy to take measured sips on the go. Take this advice from someone (me) who bought the wide-mouthed version and regularly spills water all over themself.
This moldable material is more than just glue. It’s useful for practical tasks, like fixing drywall, crafting your own cable management system, or just to build for fun. Sugru glue comes in multiple colors and serves as a smart solution to any number of household problems, especially for people who don’t have access to a 3D printer.
Ynesoer automatic soap dispenser
This once-futuristic automatic soap dispenser has become affordable enough for anyone to own. This particular model is one that a Verge staffer owns and likes, thanks to its customizable portions for the perfect hands-free wash. It’s especially great if you cook a lot and don’t want to touch your soap dispenser.
Maxracy garlic roller
For the chef on your list, these convenient garlic peelers make it easy to strip off the skin without crushing the cloves. In other words, it does the most annoying part of dealing with garlic on your behalf. If only it were this easy to tell them they need to take the garlic down a notch in their red sauce.
This truncated version of the popular Wingspan card game offers an easier point of entry in terms of cost and complexity. Players act as bird enthusiasts collecting various types of birds through multiple rounds for their wildlife preserve. Designed for one to five players, each game takes around 30 minutes or less.
These aren’t your grandma’s measuring spoons. Unless, of course, your grandma is super hip and fun, in which case they’re an excellent gift for her, or... she may already have them. With snazzy designs in vivid colors, these silly spoons spice things up in more ways than one.
For those who aspire to use something better than the plain Bic or Paper Mate pen, this affordable fountain pen is great for the aspiring calligrapher on your list. Its design is light and ergonomic, and it includes a single ink cartridge to get them started. This could be the start of a really fun hobby.
Gear Aid Heroclip
Anyone looking for a good stocking stuffer should grab this handy carabiner-style clip. While not everyone will have a use for one, you’ll probably know who on your list will dig its aircraft-grade aluminum frame that supports up to 50 pounds. It even swivels to double as a hanger in a pinch.
Show your support for our journalism while also sprucing up that coffee mug cabinet with a fresh addition. These Verge-branded 15-ounce mugs offer minimalist style and plenty of space for that extra pour you’ll need to make it through the workday, until daycare pickup, or during a long and lazy Sunday.
Sometimes, a straight, regular USB-C cable just won’t suffice. This cable is a fantastic choice for two reasons: First, it’s coiled, letting it expand from around 1 foot to 5 feet; and second, the right-angle connector makes it easier to plug into some difficult-to-reach ports instead of bending.
Get your favorite Pokémon lover a fun piece of merchandise they can piece together. With one of 15 different models (including Slowpoke, our favorite), this model kit includes four different eye sticker options, and at about 4.5 inches long, it fits neatly on a desk, a side table, or on a curio shelf next to other beloved tchotchkes.
Magnetic drawing board for toddler
Finding it hard to shop for that young niece or nephew? We’ve got you covered with this fun and colorful magnetic drawing board, which can be used to focus their creative energy. And maybe, just maybe, it’ll keep them from turning loose on the living room wall with a Sharpie set or crayons.
In the days before Bombas, socks used to be a lame gift. Now, gifting the right pair can make you the hero, especially if you choose pairs like these merino wool socks, which are machine-washable and designed to keep the chill at bay during those cold winter months as well as brisk nights and mornings.

Todoist confirms 6% of customers’ details were stolen in data breach
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
14:20:28 UTC
23:20:28 KST

Task management app Todoist said on Tuesday that a hacker gained access to its production systems and stole customer data, including email IDs, password hashes, and account creation dates. The company said around 6% of its customers were affected and blamed stolen credentials for the breach.
Todoist said the attacker used stolen credentials to access the company’s version control system and source code between November 22 and 23. The hacker then changed employee access controls and reached the app’s cloud infrastructure via an employee’s work account.
The hacker downloaded credentials of early Todoist users whose passwords were stored using an older hashing method, but didn’t change any source code, the company said. Todoist said it has blocked the attack path and reset passwords as a mitigation step, as well as disabled the Todoist for Outlook email add-on.
For a small number of people, about 0.001% of its userbase, the hacker also searched through tasks, comments, and attachment names to look for credentials for other services, like API keys and access tokens.
The hacker also pulled Todoist API tokens and Slack integration tokens, and looked for emails, addresses, and attachment file names through the Todoist for Outlook extension if it was connected to accounts and had permission to read emails.
The company said it has sent a notice to those affected and asked them to reset their credentials and change their login details in other places with the same password. The company is asking users to also update API tokens, reconnect integrations, and revoke or replace any access keys, recovery phrases, or credentials they had stored in Todoist comments.
Todoist said it has also reset its internal access structure and how remote command execution works in its cloud environment to prevent such incidents in the future.
Todoist’s founder and CEO Amir Salihefendić apologized to users about the incident on X, and said the company will take a closer look at why it happened.
The to-do app’s data breach is the latest in a burgeoning list of data breaches in which hackers use stolen credentials to access data and systems at enterprises. So far this year, we’ve seen several companies fall victim to this form of attack, including Uber and Klue.

Amazon S3 Files
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
14:19:21 UTC
23:19:21 KST

Amazon S3 Files
The first and only cloud object store that provides fully-featured, high-performance file system access to your data
What is S3 Files?
S3 Files is a shared file system that connects any AWS compute directly with your data in Amazon S3. It provides fast, direct access to all of your S3 data as files with full file system semantics and low-latency performance, without your data ever leaving S3. That means file-based applications, agents, and teams can now access and work with S3 data as a file system using the tools they already depend on. You no longer need to duplicate your data or cycle it between object storage and file system storage. Now, file-based tools and applications across your organization can work with your S3 data directly from any compute instance, container, and function using the tools your teams and agents already depend on.
With S3 Files, Amazon S3 is the first and only cloud object store that provides fully-featured, high-performance file system access to your data. S3 Files gives you the performance and simplicity of a file system with the scalability, durability, and cost-effectiveness of S3. There are no data silos, no synchronization complexities, and no tradeoffs. File and object storage, together in one place without compromise.
Benefits
Your applications, agents, and users expect to work with data as files and folders. With S3 Files, everything that needs to work with your S3 data as files can do so directly. Python libraries, ML frameworks, CLI utilities, and shell scripts all work with your S3 data with no custom connectors and no new APIs to learn.
S3 Files delivers low latency and up to multiple terabytes per second of aggregate read throughput. Performance is optimized specifically for file workloads through intelligent caching, minimizing latency and maximizing throughput. S3 Files intelligently loads your active working set onto high-performance storage, delivering low-latency access while keeping costs proportional to what you're actively using.
Organizations duplicate data across storage systems when workflows require both object and file access. S3 Files eliminates duplicate storage and the pipelines required to keep separate systems in sync. Your data lives in S3 and stays in S3, accessible through both file and object interfaces simultaneously. One copy, one place to manage, zero synchronization overhead.
S3 Files is built on S3's pay-as-you-go model with no provisioned capacity and no minimum commitments. Your workload scales automatically within S3 for high-performance, scalable use cases. Storage scales automatically as your data grows, from a single dataset to petabytes, with costs proportional to your active working set and no infrastructure to manage. S3 Files delivers up to 90% lower costs compared to cycling data between S3 and separate file systems.
Data Points
- up to 90% lower costs compared to cycling data between S3 and separate file systems
  90%
- file system IOPS per bucket
  10M+
- multiple terabytes per second of aggregate read throughput
  4TB/s+
- compute resources can simultaneously access the same S3 file system
  25K
How S3 Files works
S3 Files works like a traditional high-performance file system that can be accessed by any Linux-based compute resource, but its view of files and folders reflects what's in your S3 bucket. S3 Files is built using Amazon EFS, which intelligently loads your active working set onto high-performance storage. This delivers low latencies for frequently accessed data while keeping costs proportional to what you're actively using. When you read files, S3 Files lazily loads portions of file metadata and contents onto high-performance storage. Data that doesn't meet your configured file size threshold is read directly from S3, with no file system storage involved. When you write data, your writes are sent to the highly durable high-performance storage and then synced back to S3 to keep your bucket consistent. Data that hasn't been accessed within a configurable window (1 to 365 days, defaulting to 30) automatically expires from this storage, so you pay only for what you're actively using while your authoritative data always remains in S3.
Features and capabilities
Any new or existing S3 bucket can be accessed as a file system, with no data migration required. Your existing file-based applications and tools work exactly as they do today. No code changes, no custom connectors, and no new APIs to learn. Use a prefix to scope exactly what your file-based workloads need, so only your active dataset is placed onto the file system’s high-performance storage. Changes sync automatically back to your S3 bucket. Your full dataset stays in S3, available on demand, while your workloads work with precisely the data they need. With S3 Files, you can perform all file operations through a standard NFS file interface with low latency.
Access the same S3 bucket from multiple compute resources simultaneously through a shared file system. Built for collaboration, S3 Files supports up to 25,000 active connections across any AWS compute resource including Lambda, EC2, ECS, EKS, Fargate, and Batch. They all can read from and write to the same data in real time, with NFS close-to-open consistency. This makes S3 Files ideal for collaborative workloads where multiple users, applications, or agents need shared access to data at the same time.
S3 Files delivers up to 90% lower costs compared to cycling data between S3 and separate file systems. It keeps costs proportional to your active working set by intelligently loading only the data you’re actively using onto high-performance storage. You configure the file size threshold and retention window, and S3 Files automatically manages what stays on high-performance storage versus what’s read directly from S3. For large reads (1 MiB or larger), data is streamed directly from S3 even if it resides on the high-performance storage. Since S3 is optimized for high throughput, these reads incur only standard S3 GET request costs with no file system charge. When you write data, S3 Files batches your writes on the high-performance storage before committing them to S3, reducing S3 request costs. Data that hasn’t been accessed within your configured window automatically expires, so you pay only for what you’re actively using while maintaining complete access to your entire dataset in S3.
S3 Files gives you complete access to your entire S3 bucket through both file and object interfaces simultaneously. Your file-based applications work exactly as they do today. Your object-based applications and workflows continue unchanged. S3 Files intelligently caches the data your file workloads need for high performance, while the full scale, durability, and economics of S3 remain the foundation for your data store. No modifications, no tradeoffs, and nothing to reconcile between two separate systems.
Featured industries
Life Sciences and Healthcare
Life sciences organizations process petabytes of sequencing data from S3 through bioinformatics pipelines that require file system operations to create alignment files, merge results, and update variant calls. Healthcare providers store medical images in S3 and provide file system access for diagnostic workstations and PACS systems. Research organizations enable collaborative analysis of clinical trial data while maintaining S3 as a single source of truth with complete audit trails and regulatory compliance through encryption and access controls.
Financial Services
Quantitative researchers build and test models against petabytes of historical market data, running simulations and backtests that demand shared, low-latency access to live datasets. Maintaining separate local copies of that data created version drift, duplication costs, and slower iteration cycles. With S3 Files, every researcher on the team works from the same live S3 dataset simultaneously, with low latency for intraday risk calculations and results immediately available to every downstream workflow. Research cycles accelerate and the entire team always works from a single source of truth.
Media and Entertainment
Production teams, VFX studios, and render farms depend on file-based tools to access and process footage and assets throughout the production lifecycle. Managing separate storage systems for active production and long-term archival adds cost, complexity, and friction to every project. With S3 Files, artists and editors work directly from S3 with the tools they already use, while the same content remains available via S3 APIs for transcoding and CDN delivery, all from one copy with no synchronization required. Productions move faster and content is always where every workflow needs it.
Manufacturing and Engineering
Engineering teams run CAD workflows that generate and update large files throughout a run, storing results in S3 for long-term retention, version control, and cross-team collaboration. Getting results from simulation tools into S3 for shared access previously required manual transfers and pipeline overhead. With S3 Files, simulation tools write results incrementally to S3 as runs progress, and teams across global locations see updates in real time through a shared mounted bucket. Every iteration is preserved automatically through S3 Versioning, so engineers spend less time managing data and more time iterating on designs.
Use cases
AI agents don’t just read data, they act on it, generate new data, coordinate with other agents, and maintain state across tasks. Agents depend on file-based tools like Python libraries, CLI utilities, and shell scripts, and they need a place to read inputs, write outputs, persist memory and logs, and share intermediate state with other agents in a pipeline. S3 Files gives agents all of that in one place. Each agent accesses the same S3 file system and works with shared data using standard file operations, with no service-specific APIs to learn and no coordination layer to build. S3 becomes the shared operating environment for your entire agent fleet, not just the place data ends up when the work is done.
ML workloads have a specific problem that neither object storage nor traditional file systems alone solve well on their own. Training datasets live in S3 because of its scale and cost, but preprocessing pipelines, feature engineering scripts, and training frameworks all expect to work with files. The result is a copy-and-stage step before every run: pull data from S3, move it to a file system, process it, then push results back. That step adds time, cost, complexity and another failure point to every iteration. S3 Files eliminates this overhead. Data scientists and ML engineers can work with their S3 training data directly through a file system and run their pipelines in place. Preprocessing writes back to the same bucket automatically. Multiple training jobs can read from the same dataset simultaneously without duplication. Faster iterations result in rapid time to quality insights, because the data is always current, always in one place, and always ready to use.
Modern workflows that span applications, users, pipelines, and tools are built to read and write files. They browse directories, open datasets, append results, and save outputs using standard file operations. Connecting these applications to S3 previously required copying data out of S3 first, because S3's API-based model was incompatible with software that expects a file system. S3 Files removes that barrier. Your existing applications mount your S3 file system and work with your data exactly as they do today, with no code changes, no custom connectors, and no data movement. The application does not know it is talking to S3. It just works.
Make your S3 data natively available on any compute resource. With S3 Files, your bucket now appears as a mount to your compute resources, giving researchers, analysts, and data scientists the ability to browse directories, open datasets, run simulations, and save results using the tools they already work with. Your teams access S3 data directly from any compute resource using standard file operations, with all members reading from and writing to the same bucket simultaneously.

The company develops pharmaceutical treatments and agricultural products intended to address challenges associated with a growing global population. At Presidio, we view Amazon S3 Files as a key enabler for AI-driven workloads by consolidating file and object storage into a single foundation that eliminates duplicate systems and operational complexity. This becomes especially powerful as customers advance generative AI, advanced analytics, and agentic use cases, enabling low-latency access to shared datasets and helping teams move efficiently from development to production.”
Ken Ordini, VP of Engineering, Presidio
SciSpace
SciSpace is an agentic AI research platform that helps researchers and students find, read, and write about scientific literature. Every conversation spawns an autonomous agent that searches papers, generates documents, and delivers finished files to the user.
"When an AI agent writes a file, the user expects to open it immediately. At SciSpace, that happens about 27,000 times a day. S3 Files gives every agent session a POSIX file system backed directly by S3, so agents write files with standard operations and users see them seconds later. No sync pipeline, no duplicate storage, no custom serving logic. Files survive sandbox eviction, scale without per-session provisioning, and researchers get results twice as fast. We eliminated an entire infrastructure layer and redirected that engineering effort into building more capable agents."
Afraz Syed, Full Stack Developer, SciSpace
Snorkel AI
Snorkel AI is a frontier AI data lab that helps teams build the data and environments behind high-performing frontier and agentic AI. The company combines platform technology with research-driven data development to create datasets, benchmarks, evals, and custom solutions for real-world AI systems.
"At Snorkel AI, we are focused on building frontier datasets. Our work spans the full AI lifecycle, from curated datasets like Snorkel Data Series for the most challenging agent tasks to evaluation, reinforcement learning, and agent simulation at production scale. With S3 Files, our teams, agents, and evaluators can access data in S3 through a file interface without copying data between systems or waiting for sync jobs. This is especially valuable for reinforcement learning and agentic workloads, where we run many environments and simulations in parallel, support shared workspaces across multiple agents, and execute long-horizon task sequences at scale."
Rustem Feyzkhanov, AI Foundations Engineering Manager, Snorkel AI
Torc Robotics
Torc Robotics is a leading autonomous vehicle software company revolutionizing freight with level 4 autonomous class 8 trucks. With its TorcDrive virtual driver software, the company is commercializing self-driving trucks for safe, sustained innovation in the freight industry.
“Amazon S3 Files will enable our data scientists to access machine learning training data directly in S3 using standard file operations, effectively treating S3 as a local drive for our autonomous vehicle workflows. Our developers can work with petabytes of sensor data, simulation results, and model checkpoints directly in S3 as our foundational data layer. This simplifies data access and processing to reduce the time and complexity in building our Physical AI pipeline, enabling Torc to deliver our AV 3.0 TorcDrive verifiable AI stack, data loop, and world simulation for training and validation on the path to safe, scalable autonomous trucking."
Fiete Botschen, Director of Engineering, Machine Learning Model Development, Torc Robotics
Qube Research & Technologies (QRT)
Qube Research & Technologies (QRT) is a global quantitative and systematic investment manager that leverages advanced mathematical models and high-performance computing to identify trading opportunities across global markets. The firm's researchers analyze vast datasets in real time, combining historical market data with live feeds to develop and refine sophisticated trading algorithms that drive investment decisions.
"At QRT, our research cycle depends on rapid, iterative experimentation. Our researchers explore petabytes of tick-level market data, alternative datasets, and proprietary signals to build and backtest systematic strategies across asset classes. Historically, this required maintaining separate high-performance file storage alongside S3, forcing teams to constantly synchronize data between our central data lake and compute workstations. S3 Files changes that equation. Our researchers can now work with data directly in S3 from their workstations, running backtests, analyzing results, and iterating on strategies across larger datasets, all without copying data or managing ETL pipelines. This collapses a multi-step workflow into a seamless research loop. For us, that means faster time-to-insight, fewer redundant storage costs, and more engineering effort focused where it belongs: building better systematic strategies.“
Jon Fautley, Head of Cloud Infrastructure, QRT

Amazon is reportedly in talks to end Muse AI shopping block
Amazon is concerned that Muse AI could have an impact on its advertising revenue, according to Fortune.
As reported by Fortune, Amazon is concerned that it could lose advertising revenue by allowing Muse on its site, since it depends on actual humans seeing ads across its online marketplace. A Sculptural Playground in Shanghai Is Designed for Human-Powered Play
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
14:11:15 UTC
23:11:15 KST

A Sculptural Playground in Shanghai Is Designed for Human-Powered Play
In Shanghai’s Jing’an Sculpture Park, a monumental new installation by Brooklyn’s Wade and Leta has a clear goal: to get people up and moving in the same space. Titled “Catching Feelings,” the interactive artwork doubles as an all-ages playground with the artists’ signature sculptures and geometric forms. A central tower holds a quartet of slides, while other spinning and writhing elements are designed for more unstructured interaction.
“Catching Feelings” is one of 36 projects in the ninth edition of the Jing’an International Sculpture Project, an annual project helmed by the UCCA Center for Contemporary Art. In comparison to the artists’ earlier works, the bold, kinetic playground is decidedly analog, with any movement powered by the people interacting with its components rather than embedded motors or motion generated by the wind.
“We’re interested in the life and activity around our work—the behaviors it causes, the elations it brings,” the artists say in a statement. “We watched the same grandfather return with his granddaughter to play hide and seek; an 84-year-old and his partner used the space as the backdrop for their video recordings; new friends were made on our ground spinners; we even glimpsed a man in a business suit having a go on the slide.”
“Catching Feelings” is presented alongside monumental works by Olafur Eliasson, Marshmallow Laser Feast, and many others on view through December 31. Find more from Wade and Leta on their Instagram.

Amazon may be having a change of heart about Meta’s AI agent, Muse. The two tech giants are reportedly discussing a potential deal that would allow Meta’s popular AI agent to connect directly to Amazon’s store.
The talks would mark a significant reversal from just two months ago, when Muse users discovered they were unable to purchase products on Amazon and instead received an error message when attempting to check out.
According to a Wednesday report by Fortune, citing people familiar with the discussions, Amazon and Meta have held multiple rounds of talks about the matter. Two key issues remain under negotiation: how search results would be presented and how advertising would be handled under a potential agreement.
One of Amazon’s biggest concerns is how third-party shopping agents like Muse could affect its advertising business. In October, Amazon CEO Andy Jassy argued that few customers were making purchases through outside shopping agents.
Amazon may also have been waiting for Muse to mature before agreeing to integrate it with its marketplace. If Muse places the wrong order or creates a problem with a purchase, Amazon could ultimately be left dealing with frustrated customers and unhappy sellers.
Fortune reported that executives are concerned that third-party shopping agents could weaken Amazon’s direct relationship with consumers, according to people familiar with the matter.
Any potential agreement between Amazon and Meta would reportedly require approval from both companies’ CEOs, Fortune also reported.
Meta and Amazon have not yet responded to TechCrunch’s requests for comment.

Flipkart CEO Kalyan Krishnamurthy to step down next week amid IPO uncertainty
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
13:08:19 UTC
22:08:19 KST

Kalyan Krishnamurthy (pictured above), who steered Flipkart through Walmart’s acquisition in 2018 and helped build it into one of India’s biggest e-commerce companies, is stepping down as chief executive next week after more than 11 years with the company.
On Wednesday, Flipkart said it would announce Krishnamurthy’s successor in the coming weeks, and Chief Product and Technology Officer Balaji Thiagarajan will lead the company until a replacement is found.
A Walmart spokesperson told TechCrunch that Krishnamurthy’s last day at Flipkart will be December 4.
Krishnamurthy joined Flipkart in 2015 from Tiger Global, one of the company’s early investors, and became its CEO in 2017. He remained at the helm after Walmart acquired a controlling stake in the company for $16 billion in 2018.
Under Krishnamurthy, Flipkart said its business grew more than five-fold. The company has more than 500 million registered users and over 1.4 million sellers, including those on Shopsy, its low-cost shopping platform. The company’s marketplace offers over 150 million products across more than 80 categories.
Flipkart has of late moved into newer segments, including quick-commerce through Flipkart Minutes and, more recently, food delivery through a venture called Eat-in by Minutes. The expansion is targeted at Indian consumers who are increasingly turning to quick-delivery services for everything from groceries to electronics.
Thiagarajan joined Flipkart in September last year to lead its product and technology teams. Before that, he had founded Kasu.ai, a Bay Area startup working on AI assistants for corporate finance. He also previously worked at Google, Microsoft, and Uber in senior engineering positions, per his LinkedIn profile.
Krishnamurthy’s exit comes amid uncertainty around Flipkart’s IPO. In March, the company moved its headquarters from Singapore to India as part of its preparations to go public, but in May, Walmart reportedly told Flipkart to delay its IPO and focus on becoming profitable first.
Walmart declined to comment beyond its announcement when asked what prompted Krishnamurthy’s departure, and whether the leadership change would affect Flipkart’s IPO plans. Flipkart did not respond to a request for comment.
Founded in 2007 by Sachin and Binny Bansal as an online bookstore, much like its bigger rival Amazon, Flipkart eventually expanded into other categories. Under Krishnamurthy, it has grown into India’s largest online retailer by gross merchandise value, ahead of Amazon.
“Both Flipkart and Walmart are better because of Kalyan. We are grateful for his leadership, the governance he instilled and his many contributions to the company,” Chris Nicholas, president and CEO, Walmart International, said in a statement.

The questions Jensen Huang and Nvidia will still have to answer about Megaspeed
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
13:00:00 UTC
22:00:00 KST

Jensen Huang’s return as CEO to Nvidia hasn’t eased the scrutiny the company faces. The chipmaker still needs to address multiple inquiries into allegations that its advanced GPUs were smuggled into China by a customer, the Singapore-based data center operator Megaspeed, in violation of U.S. export rules designed to slow China’s development of frontier AI models.
The central question for investigators is what Nvidia knew about where those chips ended up and whether Huang’s affair with a Megaspeed executive gave the company reason to doubt Megaspeed’s assurances. Huang’s return doesn’t settle that. U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche has suggested Nvidia isn’t a target of an investigation, but California’s attorney general and Congress are pressing ahead, and the Securities and Exchange Commission is reportedly investigating, too.
The U.S. government opened an investigation into Megaspeed in 2024 and requested information from Nvidia about its billions of dollars in sales to the company, concerned that some of the chips Megaspeed bought for data center projects in Malaysia were in fact being diverted to China. Singapore also began investigating similar allegations in 2025. Megaspeed has denied any wrongdoing.
Diversion of this kind would be a fairly routine issue for a company like Nvidia, whose in-demand chips are the targets of smugglers and resellers. When it comes to compliance and liability for export-control violations, Nvidia and other chip sellers need assurances that the end users of their chips are obeying the rules. However, they also cannot ignore other information that might call the reliability of those assurances into question, export-control attorneys told TechCrunch.
And that’s why Jensen Huang’s affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang was such a problem for Nvidia, likely even more so than her alleged role as a source for Chinese intelligence agencies. If Nvidia personnel, from lower-level employees up to the CEO, had reason to believe that statements from Megaspeed weren’t reliable, the attorneys said, that would increase the company’s legal risk.
“Government investigators likely would want to know, notwithstanding whatever formal assurances were exchanged between the parties, what anyone at Nvidia actually knew about the end use and end users of the chips,” Dan Fisher-Owens, an attorney at the firm Berliner Corcoran & Rowe, told TechCrunch last week.
The concern Nvidia’s board faced when it decided to oust Huang is that he knew more about what Megaspeed was doing with Nvidia’s products than was reflected in any of the compliance statements. The Wall Street Journal reported that Alice Huang and her husband, Kang Wei, were part of a network of businesses that funneled chips to a company in Shanghai. Kang also reportedly introduced his wife to Jensen Huang, whom he had known for a decade.
On Monday, the U.S. government added Megaspeed and its affiliates to a list of companies restricted from U.S. trade due to national security concerns. The U.S. also issued new rules last week barring large data centers that purchase U.S. chips from serving customers in China and other nations seen as problematic by the U.S. government. Nvidia, meanwhile, said it would stop selling licensed chips to China and laid out plans to hire its first compliance officer and create a new board committee focused on export controls.
Nvidia’s board said it reinstated Huang on Monday after receiving “input from senior levels of the United States government” that left it confident his return posed no national security risk. President Donald Trump later said he called into the meeting to give them that assurance.
In a news conference Tuesday, Blanche said that Nvidia was “a good corporate citizen” and suggested it is not the target of an investigation, but Blanche declined to answer questions about the government’s investigation into Megaspeed, which the Justice Department said was ongoing as recently as last week.
“When you say the export investigation is still ongoing, I don’t know what you’re talking about,” Blanche said.
Other authorities may be more demanding. Nvidia faces investigations by California Attorney General Rob Bonta and, reportedly, the SEC, both tied to its corporate disclosures. While many details of the investigation still aren’t known, short sellers complained about the company’s decision to stop breaking out its exports to Singapore in financial filings after journalists raised concerns about chip diversion.
Members of Congress from both parties say they want answers from Nvidia, with many citing alarm about China and some questioning the White House’s role in Huang’s return. Three committees in the House of Representatives will privately interview Huang and Nvidia’s top lawyer, Tim Teter, next week. Teter reportedly intervened in the Department of Commerce’s investigation of Megaspeed last year.
“We remain committed to getting answers on the mass smuggling of Nvidia products, their diversion for Chinese military purposes, and their key role in advancing our foremost adversary’s AI capabilities,” the Republican chairs of House committees focused on foreign affairs, intelligence, and China said in a joint statement.
Rep. Brian Mast, the chair of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, has also demanded that the Department of Commerce share extensive data regarding its investigation into chip smuggling, including by Megaspeed and its affiliates.
The Senate Banking Committee will also question Huang in a public hearing on December 3.
“Mr. Huang, Nvidia executives, and the company’s board still owe Congress answers about the company’s compliance with U.S. export controls,” Senator Elizabeth Warren, the ranking Democratic member on the committee, said in a statement. “The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what ‘input’ was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement.”
