The loneliest peptide party in New York
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
12:37:55 UTC
21:37:55 KST

This is Optimizer, a weekly newsletter sent from Verge senior reviewer Victoria Song that dissects and discusses the latest gizmos and potions that swear they’re going to change your life. Opt in for Optimizer here.
The loneliest peptide party in New York
The peptide curious are looking for answers, and finding dubious products instead.
On a rainy fall evening, an experimental art venue in Chelsea Market had a small line out the door. There are always lines in New York City. Viral food trends, gallery exhibitions, pop-ups, and parties. The folks in line looked like your average joe, ready for a Friday night on the town. You wouldn’t look twice — except, perhaps, for a man with a wizard beard and a laser sword strapped to his back.
Inside, the man with a laser sword stood in the middle of the cavernous bottom floor of a two-story warehouse strewn with large bean bags. On the wall were psychedelic projections of jellyfish, shrimp, and other sea creatures. A bizarre choice, considering peptides won’t induce any trippy vision quests, and not a single person was getting groovy. The industrial beams had posters with QR codes that you could scan to get samples of Afterglow, a new type of peptide product. According to seemingly discarded pamphlets around the venue, Afterglow isn’t a standard injectable or oral peptide. The company makes strips that dissolve under the tongue. The “Afterglow Peptide Field Guide” also described what each sample does, dosing recommendations, and potential side effects. At the upstairs bar, bartenders mixed custom cocktails called Afterburn — complete with bioluminescent veggie nectar — and Margatrutide. (For the record, a bioluminescent veggie nectar cocktail tastes as awful as you think.)
Welcome to a peptide launch party.
Peptide parties are old news in San Francisco, but they’re somewhat more novel in New York City. Milling around the edges of the venue were various reporters and photographers. We were all trying to get a sense of why anyone had bothered to come out. Not that many did. Despite the venue’s size, the main floor had plenty of empty space.
One person I spoke to was a fencer with sore joints. They’d heard that one of the peptides might help with recovery. Another had a father in the wellness industry and had heard vague things about how effective peptides could be. One woman, staring at a projected jellyfish, merely shrugged. Her friend was more into “wellness stuff” and she had no plans, so, why not check out a party? A man, staring into his bag of Afterglow samples, was mostly interested in weight loss but scared about the safety of gray market products. A few feet away, another man was furiously reading the Afterglow pamphlet. “I like to know what I put in my body,” he said.
The only problem was he didn’t remember which sample he had tried.
A common refrain: Well, this beats doing research online. This, at least, was a social event where you could theoretically connect with other people. And there were clusters of strangers speaking to each other. Mostly, near the bar. Everywhere else, people stared awkwardly on their phones, their eyes occasionally roaming the room without meeting anyone else’s gaze for more than a second or two. After a while, they’d disappear into the back room and reappear with a goody bag full of peptide samples.
After 90 minutes, I decided to leave. For the umpteenth time that evening, I’d walked up to someone, asked about peptides, and got a noncommittal “I really don’t know anything — I just came to learn more.” (When asked if they had, they invariably shrugged and pointed to the goody bag.) It was clear that the only peptide evangelists here were likely associated with the launch. The whole thing, to me, seemed as if someone had tried to draw a cool party from memory. The last thing I saw was a person draped in a ghost sheet sitting alone in the corner.
As it turned out, the man with the laser sword was a performance artist who’d been invited to attend. Out of journalistic integrity, I asked if he had ever tried peptides. He told me he was more interested in the unifying energy of the universe. When I got up to leave, he waved and said, “Giggles.”
If I were peptide curious, the Afterglow launch party was a pretty awful way to learn anything about these substances. To be fair, Afterglow’s Partiful invitation mostly described this event as “an environment great for aura farming” and the “hard launch of [its] first of many experiments in culture.” Nevertheless, as part of the press, I did walk away with a smattering of Afterglow’s products. In total, I got packets of what Afterglow calls Charge (NAD+), Rejuvenate (glutathione), Wolverine Blend (BPC-157 / TB-500), Strength Blend (CJC-1295 / Ipamorelin), Glow Blend (GHK-Cu / BPC-157 / TB-500), Connect (PT-141), GLP-2 (tirzepatide), and GLP-3 (retatrutide). Or at least that’s what the packaging says. There’s no way to truly verify gray market peptides for safety or efficacy because they’re unregulated products.
First of all, last I checked, GLP-1 medications do not upgrade like iPhone models. Afterglow’s pamphlet correctly lists that your body has “three hormone switches”: GLP-1, GIP, and glucagon. The accompanying descriptors of what each does are broadly correct, though lacking in nuance. For example, there is a hormone called glucagon-like peptide 2, but it’s not tirzepatide. The “real” GLP-2 is a hormone related to intestinal health. Tirzepatide is still considered a GLP-1 medication that’s a dual agonist because it works on two hormones (GLP-1 and GIP.) The GLP-2 moniker is a misnomer popularized by ill-informed influencers. The same goes for retatrutide, which, as of this writing, is still an unapproved GLP-1 medication that works on three hormones (GLP-1, GIP, and glucagon) and is therefore a triple agonist. There is no such thing as GLP-3.
For all the samples, there were no instructions regarding dosing on the packaging — just another QR code leading to Afterglow’s product page. Those pages happen to contain instructions for how to use the strips, and the amount of product within the strips. There are disclaimers about how little studied the compounds are, but nothing about interactions, side effects, or what to expect while taking. Just a throwaway line to talk to your doctor.
All that information, you’d have to find on the pamphlet given at the party. (Ironically, some “important cautions” on the pamphlet state to avoid taking samples with alcohol, at a party where the booze was flowing.) Provided you didn’t throw it out. One would hope that information is on a box if you were to buy directly from the seller. But for all intents and purposes, what I just described is what a clueless partygoer would have to go on.
If everything in that pamphlet were 100 percent factual, perhaps a conscientious partygoer could feel reassured. On that front, I have bad news. The pamphlet calls PT-141 an “FDA-approved peptide that boosts sexual desire for both men and women.”
PT-141, also known as bremelanotide or Vyleesi, is FDA approved. But only for premenopausal women suffering from hypoactive sexual desire disorder. It is not approved for post-menopausal women, men, or for general sexual performance enhancement.
Another concern: How does one evaluate a sublingual peptide strip? Specifically, is this delivery method even scientifically proven effective in humans? Spoiler: It’s not! A collapsible FAQ menu on Afterglow’s site states, “We don’t know yet. Nobody has published how much of most peptides reaches the blood from a strip compared with an injection. We’re funding studies to measure exactly that, and we’ll share what we find.”
As for why you’d want to even try a sublingual strip? Afterglow’s website suggests it’s more convenient than injections or pills. No refrigeration, no needles, and no need for learning how to reconstitute a drug. Just pop a strip under the tongue and go.
And good luck trying to find reputable sources of information on your own. Afterglow says it’s funding its own research, but its site has no reviewable documentation just yet. A Google search brings up a questionable AI overview, a handful of links from dubious med spas, a couple of dead links, and one PubMed study in mice. Most of the links also primarily discuss the difference between oral peptides in pill form and injectables — so not terribly relevant here.
I’d consider it fair game if I were confident that anyone at the party I went to did their due diligence. As it stands, I talked with over a dozen people who were mostly there for vibes, booze, and freebies.
Not all peptide parties are created equal. Some happen on WWII cargo ships, complete with panel discussions about peptide supply chains. Another “peptide 101” talk I attended in New York happened at the back of a bitcoin bar. There, most attendees also knew very little about peptides, but at least they got treated to a PowerPoint presentation about the basics. There was a dubious demonstration of how to inject oneself that would’ve probably made any pharmacist or doctor scream. But at the very least, peptides were defined and attendees could ask questions. (The answers to those questions were on the right track with regard to the dangers of the gray market. They were less reliable about scientific rigor and why “anecdotal evidence” is not the same as clinical evidence of safety.)
But a striking, recurring theme is just how much is unknown. These substances aren’t well-studied in humans, so there’s not much evidence as to long-term benefits or side effects. The peptide curious know shocking little about the substances they’re supposedly interested in. Likewise, it’s incredibly difficult to get reliable, unbiased data on gray market interest or usage, though there have been attempts to quantify this surging demand in recent months. Case in point, telehealth provider Hims & Hers just conducted a consumer survey of roughly 2,000 American adults. Of those surveyed, 68 percent said they had little to no knowledge about peptides. Less than 18 percent were even able to identify that peptides are chains of amino acids. Even so, 40 percent said they were interested or very interested in trying injectable prescription treatments — including peptides — if offered by a telehealth service.
Even so, peptides are everywhere. The word appears in copious ads for skincare creams, weight loss drugs, and supplements. Looksmaxxers, influencers, and celebrities like Gwyneth Paltrow and Joe Rogan talk about them in podcasts, documentaries, and social media as if they’re miracle shortcuts. Hims & Hers has even announced it’s launching a service called Peptide Plus — nevermind that the vast majority of what it’ll offer are not actual peptides.
In this kind of environment, of course more people will become peptide curious. Which makes it even more concerning that the most readily available information sources are not medical or scientific experts well-versed on the topic at hand. There are dubious links online summarized into less-than-critical AI overviews. There are pamphlets handed out at weird parties, where there’s more booze and schmooze than education. Influencers branding themselves as experts as they flash crudely summarized studies in a bar back room. Industry panels that are inaccessible to the average consumer, full of jargon and assumed knowledge of the science at hand.
It’s clear something needs to be done until general knowledge improves. Until then, the parties will continue.

Hazel (YC W24) Is Hiring a Product Designer
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
12:00:34 UTC
21:00:34 KST

AI-enabled procurement for government
Our mission at Hazel is simple and powerful. We use AI to help U.S. government purchasing teams buy at the speed of need with unparalleled efficiency and cost-effectiveness. It is a $2.7T problem every single year, and Hazel is at the forefront with Federal and State, Local, and Education (SLED) agencies.
Hazel has identified a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to solve this by deploying cutting-edge AI to the public sector and unlocking the full potential of our nation. We empower our partners to move with expediency against the most pressing challenges of this century. Hazel builds AI for the public interest.
Hazel is transforming government procurement: how government agencies purchase goods and services from commercial vendors. Our federal partners use our AI-forward bid evaluations solution to assess vendors more accurately, reliably, and quickly. With the federal government under increasing pressure to accelerate acquisitions, our tools give them a real edge.
Our COTS products, led by Document Builder and Evaluations, serve state & local governments, schools, and special authorities. The users are procurement officers working through long, high-stakes documents and decisions, and they deserve software that is as clear and trustworthy as it is powerful.
As Hazel's contract Product Designer, you will elevate the user experience and visual design of products government teams rely on daily. Hazel has built a substantial product based on deep understanding of our users' needs. In this role, you will refine our UI/UX and establish standards for our engineering team going forward.
There are three main deliverables:
Federal. Upgrade the UI/UX of the bid evaluations solution we are deploying for our largest federal contract.
Commercial. Improve the UX of our COTS product flows and define consistent UI elements to unify the product design.
Playbook. Deliver a practical style guide and Figma component set so Hazel engineers can ship consistent, high-quality UI after the engagement ends.
Audit Hazel's existing workflows and identify the highest-impact UI/UX improvements
Design high-fidelity screens, flows, and interaction states for complex, document-heavy workflows, including how users review and act on AI output
Work directly with Hazel's founders and engineers, sharing work early and iterating quickly
Hand off production-ready designs and review what ships to ensure quality
Build a reusable set of Figma components and documented patterns
Write a practical style guide our engineers can follow for new features, including accessibility basics (Section 508 / WCAG 2.1 AA)
Proven experience as a product designer or similar role, with a portfolio of shipped work
Strong skills across UI/UX
Experience designing complex, workflow-heavy B2B applications (dense tables, long forms, multi-step reviews and approvals)
Experience working within and contributing to a design system
Ability to improve an existing product without starting from scratch
Fast turnaround and independence: you ship in days, not weeks
Expert in Figma or similar tools
Excitement about bringing innovation to the public sector
Has worked at a startup or startup-like environment
Building a design system from scratch and documenting it for others
Front-end coding experience (React, TypeScript)
Designing AI features, including surfacing sources and letting users review and correct model output
Document-centric interfaces: reading, annotating, or comparing long documents
Working with or adjacent to the public sector
Accessibility work to Section 508 or WCAG 2.1 AA
Federal acquisition or source selection
$10,000 - $20,000/month depending on experience | Contract for 3-6 months, 20-40 hrs/week
Remote, with potential working sessions in person with the team. All travel expenses will be paid.
U.S. Citizenship required
Opportunity for future projects or a longer-term role
You will set the design standard for Hazel. Your work will ship to federal evaluators and to procurement teams across states, cities, and schools. The style guide you leave behind will shape every feature we build after.
You'll work side-by-side with inquisitive and driven founders as well as a world-class and dynamic founding engineering team, with frontline exposure to our government partners.
Hazel is a mission-driven organization founded by engineers and built around delivering world-class innovation to our government partners. We strive for excellence, deliver impact, and have fun doing it.
You care about mission. Hazel partners with agencies supporting everything from public school teachers in the classroom to warfighters in the field. We believe in products which change America for the better.
You want a startup that is structured without being rigid, quality-obsessed without being slow, and product-led without losing the vision. You take ownership: shaping the experience, talking to customers, and wearing many hats.
You solve problems. There's no more time to opine on strategy and policy when our nation faces existential challenges, today. We are workhorses, not showhorses.
You are always learning. Nobody has all the answers, and the world is ever-evolving, so we must be adaptive. We bring the best practices available from all domains to meet the challenge.
You believe in team. Our team grows together, supports one another, and elevates our shared successes. We are more effective together.
Our mission at Hazel is simple and powerful. We use AI to help U.S. government purchasing teams buy at the speed of need with unparalleled efficiency and cost-effectiveness. It is a $2.7T problem every single year, and Hazel is at the forefront with State, Local, and EDucation (SLED) and federal government agencies.
Government procurement has moved at a glacial pace for decades, stovepiped by complex processes, regulatory capture, and overworked civil servants. The impacts are severe. Our founders saw at Palantir and BCG how lowly procurement challenges fueled wildfires across California, compromised critical U.S. national security systems, and wasted billions of dollars.
Hazel has identified a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to solve this by deploying cutting-edge AI to the public sector and unlocking the full potential of our nation. We empower our partners to move with expediency against the most pressing challenges of this century including climate sustainability, great power competition, and more. Hazel builds AI for the public interest. The founders August and Elton saw this problem firsthand at Palantir and BCG, and they have enlisted a deep bench of investors from both tech and government to deploy one of the first real commercial applications of generative AI in the public sector.

Windows Loudness Equalization, reverse-engineered and ported to PipeWire
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
11:34:25 UTC
20:34:25 KST

Windows' Loudness Equalization for PipeWire, reverse engineered from WMALFXGFXDSP.dll. It evens out the volume of each application, so quiet and loud parts play at a similar level.
- modules/loudness/: the PipeWire module and the DSP plugin. It processes every app's playback stream.
- modules/limiter/: the output limiter of Windows' audio engine (AudioEng.dll), on every playback device. It keeps the boost from clipping.
- app/: an optional window (GTK3) to turn it on or off, globally or per app, and set the release time.
Requires PipeWire ≥ 1.4, and GTK 3 for the window.
# Arch:   sudo pacman -S pipewire gtk3 pkgconf make gcc
# Debian: sudo apt install libpipewire-0.3-dev libspa-0.2-dev libgtk-3-dev pkg-config make gcc
make
sudo make install
systemctl --user restart pipewire pipewire-pulse wireplumber
To install only one part, use make -C modules/loudness, make -C modules/limiter or make -C app. To uninstall, run sudo make uninstall and restart the same services.
Open Loudness Equalization from your application menu, or run pw-loudness-eq.
The settings live in a PipeWire metadata object, so they can also be scripted:
pw-metadata -n loudness-eq 0 enabled false                               # turn off
pw-metadata -n loudness-eq 0 release 6                                   # release time, 2 (fast) to 7 (slow)
pw-metadata -n loudness-eq 0 disabled-apps '["Discord"]' Spa:String:JSON  # leave these apps alone
pw-metadata -n loudness-eq                                               # show settings
Changes apply immediately and are kept across restarts.
Defaults are in /usr/share/pipewire/pipewire.conf.d/60-loudness-eq.conf. To override them, copy it to ~/.config/pipewire/pipewire.conf.d/.
Settings changed at runtime are saved in ~/.local/state/pw-loudness-eq/state.conf and take precedence over this file.
The loudness module sets audioconvert.filter-graph.90 on each app's playback stream, so the plugin runs inside that stream, before its volume and before mixing, like a Windows stream effect.
The limiter module does what Windows' audio engine does on every playback device: after the mix and the software volume, it runs the output through a look-ahead limiter. It's always on, independent of Loudness Equalization, as on Windows.
Not processed: JACK clients, streams with more than 8 channels, and Flatpak apps that use PipeWire directly (those going through PulseAudio are processed).
A broadband automatic gain control. Despite the name, it doesn't equalize the spectrum.
- Frame size N = 2^⌈log2(fs/100)⌉ (512 at 44.1/48 kHz), with 50% overlap.
- Each channel is windowed with w[n] = 16384·(1 − cos(2π(n+½)/N)) / (N/2) and run through a real FFT to get the power spectrum.
- Power is summed into 25 bands with edges at 100, 200, 300, 400, 510, 630, 770, 920, 1080, 1270, 1480, 1720, 2000, 2320, 2700, 3150, 3700, 4400, 5300, 6400, 7700, 9500, 12000, 15500 and 24000 Hz. Channels are summed together.
- Loudness L = 1.72 · Σ max(E_b − T_b, 1e-32)^0.25, with T = 1000, 100, 31.6, 10, 3.16, 2, 1, 1, .316, .316, 1×11, 4, 10, 100, 1000.
- Target gain = min((1300/L)^1.6, 10), so at most +20 dB.
- When the target is lower than the current gain, the gain jumps to it instantly. Otherwise it moves toward it by w = (N/128)·(44100/fs)·0.1^min(r,3)·0.316^(min(r,7)−min(r,3)) per block. At r = 4 that's a time constant of about 4.6 s.
- The gain is crossfaded linearly across each half-frame. Latency is N samples.
The boost can push pure tones such as a whistle well above full scale; the limiter catches that.
Ported from CAudioLimiter in AudioEng.dll. audiodg.exe puts it on every render device, after its effects, before the device.
- The output is delayed by L frames (64 at 44.1/48 kHz; 16, 32 or 128 at other rates) to look ahead. That's the device's added latency.
- When a frame entering the delay peaks above the tracked peak (initially 0.985), the gain ramps down linearly over those L frames to 0.985 / peak, reaching it as that frame leaves. All channels share one gain.
- The gain only recovers once the signal peaks at half the tracked peak or less: it rises by e^(2.205/fs) per frame (about 19 dB/s) while the tracked peak decays by the same factor.
On Windows the device's software volume comes before the limiter; in PipeWire it comes after the device's filter graphs. So the plugin gets the volume of each channel (kept in sync by the module), measures peaks on the signal as it will be after the volume, and applies only its gain. Once PipeWire applies the volume, the result is the same as Windows'. Hardware volume comes after the limiter on both.
make check                                              # DSP and plugin tests, both modules
modules/loudness/build/leq-wav [-r release] in.wav out.wav       # process a file offline

====================================================================================================

Microsoft declares ‘Code Red’ after huge Outlook outage
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
11:13:02 UTC
20:13:02 KST

This month’s Exchange Online and Microsoft Outlook outage has prompted the software giant to declare an unusual “Code Red” internally, Reuters reports. The nearly 24-hour outage took down email for millions of customers, and was triggered by a bug that had been sitting on Microsoft 365 networking gateway servers since July without any impact to servers.
Microsoft declares ‘Code Red’ after huge Outlook outage
Microsoft is now holding daily meetings with the engineering group responsible for Microsoft 365 services.
In a memo to the teams responsible for Exchange Online, Microsoft 365 Core president Jason Henderson revealed the outage was the most significant in history for Microsoft’s customers. “Unfortunately, all the work we have done over the years was not sufficient to protect us on Thursday,” said Henderson. “Code Red means that this is the highest priority work for our team.”
As part of this Code Red declaration, Microsoft is focused on load shedding, geographic traffic partitioning, and faster recovery mechanisms. Microsoft now has a target of resolving future outages within an hour, and will reportedly deploy AI agents to help engineers diagnose issues. Microsoft is now holding daily meetings with the engineering group responsible for Microsoft 365 services, alongside weekly reviews.
In a separate memo, Amanda Silver, Microsoft’s corporate vice president of M365 Core and Work IQ, said engineers had to work through a “fog of war” to help resolve the outage. I reported last week that Microsoft engineers had a running bridge call during the entire outage, and at one point it hit the maximum of 1,000 participants.
Microsoft engineers had to exchange messages across 11 Teams chats and reportedly “struggled to maintain a cohesive picture” during the outage because of the fragmented internal tracking systems. Sources tell me the outage was marked as “sev0,” the highest level, which indicates a catastrophic level of service disruption. Engineers struggled to get servers back online because they had entered maintenance mode and became unresponsive due to unusually high CPU loads.

Is Nvidia's Legal Trouble Over - Or Just Beginning?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
11:00:00 UTC
20:00:00 KST

Is Nvidia's Legal Trouble Over - Or Just Beginning? (nypost.com) 67
			
		 	
				America's Justice Department "is still investigating Nvidia," reports the New York Post, adding that Monday President Trump posted on Truth Social that Nvidia should also "continue its thorough internal investigation of the Company's compliance with U.S. export controls in order to ensure that it is fulfilling its legal obligations and Patriotic Duty to protect U.S. National Security."
But President Trump also told The New York Post Monday in an exclusive interview "that he's willing to pardon Jensen Huang if necessary — but that he believes the reinstalled Nvidia CEO should be fine without one and should 'tell the truth' to Congress when he testifies next week...
Huang doesn't need to invoke his Fifth Amendment rights to avoid self-incrimination when he sits for an interview with the House Foreign Affairs Committee next Tuesday and appears before a hearing of the Senate Banking Committee next Thursday, Trump added...  
The president said that Nvidia's success was essential to the US retaining an edge in artificial intelligence — and that he's even open to the company giving the American government an equity stake, noting Intel handed over 10% of its value last year.  "I thought about it. I said, 'You know, maybe I should do it,' but I didn't bring it up at this point. It's probably not too late," Trump said. 
Earlier a source familiar with Jensen Huang's comeback "told the New York Post that President Trump and Vice President Vance 'became convinced that Jensen was a singular figure protecting Open Source models and preventing the closed-source model companies from becoming too powerful.'"
And CBS News reports that Nvidia's Jensen Huang "still has security clearance, sources say."  (Although revoking Huang's clearance "was discussed..."
It is not uncommon for executives at tech companies that do classified federal work to have security clearances... [U.S. Attorney General Todd] Blanche said the government relies on a "collective consensus" among federal agencies to assess national security risks and determine whether somebody is able to access sensitive government information.
An Nvidia spokesperson declined to comment on Huang's clearance.
Asked whether the Justice Department investigated Jensen Huang before Mr. Trump assured Nvidia's board that he had not engaged in wrongdoing, Blanche said he would have informed the president if the department had evidence Huang had committed a crime. "To the extent that that Department of Justice had information that he was committing crimes or something like this ... that's something that I would have certainly told the president had it been true," Blanche said. Pressed on how the department could rule out potential criminal liability while export-control investigations remained ongoing, Blanche said he could not predict whether future investigations might produce different findings.
"I can't predict the future," Blanche said.
  
Jensen Huang is still testifying before the U.S. Congress next week, reports The Hill:
The Republican chairs of three different House committees stressed the need to address concerns regarding national security despite Jensen Huang's reinstatement as Nvidia CEO..   "The NVIDIA board's brief internal investigation and reinstatement of Jensen Huang do not erase the national security threats and questions that must be addressed," House Foreign Affairs Committee Chair Brian Mast (R-Fla.), House Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party Chair John Moolenaar (R-Mich.) and House Intelligence Committee Chair Rick Crawford (R-Ark.) wrote in a joint statement obtained by NewsNation... 
"We remain committed to getting answers on the mass smuggling of NVIDIA products, their diversion for Chinese military purposes, and their key role in advancing our foremost adversary's AI capabilities," it continued.
 
U.S. Senators are also promising to grill Nvidia's Jensen Huang, reports Politico, and to pass new legislation regulating future chip exports:
"The controversy with Nvidia ... is just the tip of the iceberg, and it's not resolved because Jensen Huang has his job back," Senate Intelligence Chair Tom Cotton posted Monday.  "Congress has much to investigate," he said, "and we will pass overwhelmingly bipartisan chip export-control bills, including my Chip Security Act, one way or the other." Nvidia General Counsel Tim Teter is also expected to testify Dec. 2, and one House Democratic aide said lawmakers are already "considering" asking other Nvidia board members to provide information....
Sen. Kevin Cramer, a member of Senate Banking, predicted that between Democrats outraged by Trump's backing of Huang and GOP wariness toward China, an export control measure would pass by a margin large enough "to overcome a veto."  Even if Huang ends up not participating in the hearings, or if Republicans back away under pressure from Trump, Democrats like incoming Banking Chair Elizabeth Warren could turbocharge their investigation plans using their new subpoena powers next year.  "Chairman Elizabeth Warren and a number of Banking Committee Republicans ... are going to put the screws to these people like they've never had the screws put to them before," Cramer said in an interview. 
Jensen Huang's scandal revealed that American export-control policy "remains riddled with loopholes, hobbled by incapacity and infighting, and divorced from clear strategy," according to Foreign Affairs (the magazine published by the nonprofit Council on Foreign Relations think tank). 
While the magazine applauded a recent tightening of restrictions, it suggests more action from Congress since the current administration's "lax enforcement of past controls — and internal divisions over the new ones — raise serious questions about its willingness to follow through."
As a starting point, Congress should pass the Remote Access Security Act, which would give the administration clear legal authority for controlling remote access to data centers powered by U.S. compute. While implementation will require close congressional oversight, getting the right legal structure in place is an important first step. Next, it should work with industry and technical experts to fund and fast-track "on-chip" technologies — basically, features that can verify a chip's location and counter smuggling and diversion. Beyond sorting out the technical feasibilities, Congress will need to work with industry to understand the implications of on-chip security mechanisms for global competitiveness and the potential surveillance risks should China develop similar controls since it produces much of the world's legacy chips.   Along with these technical fixes, Congress should seize on the Megaspeed scandal to strengthen oversight. The AI Overwatch Act, which may be included in the annual defense bill, would codify existing export controls on advanced chips into law, preventing the current or a future administration from reversing them. It would also empower Congress to review and block advanced chips sales to U.S. adversaries. 
Congress can go even further by establishing a new investigative committee or inspector general to strengthen oversight of sensitive chip export-control policies. [Another suggestion the article makes?  "[C]ondition advanced chip sales on the buyer's ability to protect them from diversion and guard against misuse by malign actors looking to skirt safety and security rules."]  Together, these actions would go a long way toward combating the circumvention that let firms such as Megaspeed thrive...  Congress has a role in ensuring that the president faithfully executes the laws and is supported by the right resources, oversight, and strategic priorities. The United States' AI lead may hinge on whether it meets the moment.

Why Automated Proof-Solving Is the Same Old Dead End
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
08:23:34 UTC
17:23:34 KST

Computer Science > Cryptography and Security
  [Submitted on 19 Nov 2026]
Title:The Industrialization of Noise: Why Automated Proof-Solving is the Same Old Dead End
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:Nearly fifty years ago, DeMillo, Lipton, and Perlis argued that mathematical proofs are not static, isolated structures of formal logic, but the products of a vibrant, evolving social process of human critique, internalization, and shared intuition. In October 2026, OpenAI challenged this thesis by releasing a massive repository of 722 automated mathematical manuscripts, claiming full or partial solutions to 372 longstanding open problems. This paper examines the immediate crisis through the lens of the original DLP framework. We argue that the sudden, automated overproduction of dense, unreadable proofs represents an industrial pollution of the mathematical ecosystem. By examining the three fatal structural pillars of this automated approach (Specification, Regress, and Utility), we demonstrate that decoupling the act of mechanical solving from the human social process of understanding produces a sterile repository of dead logical artifacts.
The resulting industrialization of unexamined manuscripts is self-fulfilling. Flooding the literature guarantees that the finite supply of human attention will lose the race to separate signal from noise. In this sense, posting a result is not the same act as submitting it to the community processes by which mathematics is vetted, digested, and incorporated into shared knowledge.
Additional Features

Smart People Who Give a Shit Use Tools
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
07:46:43 UTC
16:46:43 KST

Anyone who has worked on payment rails or old distributed systems knows the exact feeling Justin Michael describes in his post this week. You peel back the curtain on some piece of infrastructure the economy quietly runs on, expecting Swiss watchmaking, and find duct tape, twenty-year-old batch files, and three people in the whole company who know why you never reboot box four on a Tuesday. He calls them “smart people who give a shit,” and he’s dead right that they are the main reason the lights stay on.
Halfway down the page, though, the essay takes a turn I’ve been seeing a lot lately, and it bugs me. Michael points to the recent run of big outages (Microsoft 365, United, major banks, Apple) and blames leadership for trying to swap those people out for generative AI. Fair enough; there’s probably plenty of truth in that. But he doesn’t stop at bad management. By the end, the tools themselves have become the acid dissolving whoever picks them up. “Every prompt moves them one step further away from the system they used to know inside and out,” he writes. Every generated line robs engineers of the struggle that makes them smart and the ownership that makes them care.
The implication is pretty hard to miss: if you’re using AI, you’re giving up on giving a shit.
I use these tools every day on systems where bugs cost significant sums of money or wedge production queues, and my experience has been almost the exact opposite. More than anything else, LLMs amplify who you already are. Deeply curious, caring people can dramatically amplify what they produce and how thoroughly they understand it. Lazy bums can, for now, coast as meat proxies for a chat window, ferrying tickets into a prompt box and clicking merge on whatever falls out.
Think about what “giving a shit” actually meant on a normal engineering team before 2024. Mostly, it meant fighting a losing war against the clock. You’d spot a nasty race condition in a state machine and know you should model it in TLA+, or write a property test that hammers every interleaving, or go read the forty-thousand-line service downstream to be certain your assumption held. Half the time you didn’t, because doing it properly took two days you didn’t have and three other fires were waiting. So you eyeballed the diff, wrote a unit test for the happy path, left a slightly nervous comment, and hoped nobody touched that file for a while. Caring was rarely limited by how much people wanted to do the right thing. It was rationed by how fast one human could read and type.
When you hand a capable model to that engineer, they don’t suddenly shrug and rubber-stamp the first draft. They finally write the TLA+ spec. They pull on the threads they were always curious about, digging into why a gnarly legacy subsystem behaves the way it does instead of treating it as a black box. They build a real simulator for the flaky third-party API nobody ever had time to mock properly. They have the model trace every caller of a legacy function across a sprawling monorepo before changing its contract, instead of grepping for five minutes and praying. They write the exhaustive edge-case tests and run mutation testing against the parts that keep them awake at night.
The kind of rigor that used to be reserved for people writing avionics software or working at Bell Labs is suddenly cheap enough to pull off on an ordinary Thursday. Calling that “caring less” has the whole thing backwards.
Then there is the other side of the amplifier. Anyone who has reviewed a 1,500-line pull request written by a meat proxy who clearly hit “accept all,” got a green linter, and blanked out when asked to explain line 400 knows exactly what Michael is furious about. (I’m certainly guilty of opening massive AI-assisted PRs myself, though in the service of giving a shit, so the line count by itself isn’t the crime.) Prompt, paste, and pray is very real, and it produces miserable software. I’ve written before about the specific sinking feeling of staring at 800 lines of generated code you have no mental model of and deploying it with crossed fingers.
There’s a subtler hazard too, especially for people early in their careers: if you never wrestle with a weird bug or read a stack trace yourself, you never build the scar tissue that tells you when something smells wrong. And LLMs make laziness unusually tempting because of how they fail. A compiler yells at you loudly when you screw up; a model fails in smooth, confident prose that looks totally reasonable until production catches fire. That’s a genuine risk, and pretending it isn’t would be silly.
Still, look closely at what’s actually broken in that scenario. The trouble comes from shipping code nobody verified, without building a mental model, in an organization where nobody feels on the hook when it breaks. We had that exact engineer long before ChatGPT showed up. Ten years ago they pasted the top Stack Overflow answer without reading the comments, pulled in a bloated dependency to avoid thinking for twenty minutes, and stamped “LGTM” on every review after thirty seconds. Now they can coast as a human clipboard for a model, at least for a while, until the production bugs pile up or someone realizes a cron job could do the clipboard part cheaper. Take away their LLM tomorrow and they won’t suddenly start caring about ACH edge cases. They’ll just go back to shipping carelessness at a slower clip.
Look at Michael’s own example of ACH. The people keeping banking rails alive aren’t chiseling NACHA files into stone tablets to stay spiritually close to the metal. Their care shows up in reconciliation scripts, monitors, static checks, runbooks, and custom tooling they built because unaided human attention breaks down on systems this messy. Every jump up the abstraction ladder (compilers, garbage collectors, search engines, high-level frameworks) brought the exact same warning that we were moving another step away from the machine and losing the people who really understood it. Sure, far fewer programmers today can hand-assemble x86 than in 1985. Meanwhile, the systems we have to keep upright grew by several orders of magnitude. Without stronger leverage, you don’t get a golden age of artisanal engineering where everyone holds the whole codebase in their head. You just get more critical systems that nobody understands at all, because they long ago outgrew what one unaided brain can hold.
There is a bigger elephant in the room about all those outages, too, and it predates LLMs by a long shot. A huge share of the instability we are watching right now is the bill coming due for forty years of insufficient engineering rigor. Giving a shit does not make people infallible. Some of the smartest, most conscientious engineers in the industry spent decades building critical infrastructure in memory-unsafe languages, gluing together under-specified protocols, skipping formal verification, and shipping state machines whose edge cases lived only inside one person’s head. Heroics and tribal knowledge kept the wheels on, right up until they didn’t.
Plenty of the vulnerabilities and failure modes knocking systems over today have been sitting quietly in the wild for years, or even decades. We got away with them because nobody had the time or budget to poke every dark corner of a sprawling legacy stack, and because change moved slowly enough for manual patching to keep up. Only now is the issue being forced, by automated bug-hunting that never sleeps, by decades of accumulated coupling, and by systems that have finally grown too tangled for human heroics to paper over. Blaming all of that on AI slop is a comforting story, because it implies we can just go back to the old way of working and everything will steady out. The old way of working is how we ended up with a world where bad updates and long-dormant bugs can wobble major banks, airlines, and cloud providers in the same week.
None of that lets leadership off the hook. Where Michael is completely on target is what’s happening in the executive suite. Leadership looking at an LLM and thinking, fantastic, now we can lay off the prickly senior engineers who push back on bad ideas and replace them with a $200-a-month subscription, really is a slow-motion train wreck, and it makes a fragile landscape much worse. To someone who has never been paged at three in the morning, a fragile system held aloft by quiet competence and a pile of plausible-looking slop look identical on a quarterly slide deck.
That disease, though, is decades older than generative AI. It’s the exact same management fantasy that gave us “let’s fire the expensive in-house team and outsource the core platform to the lowest bidder,” “let’s cut QA because developers can test their own stuff,” and “let’s lay off twenty percent of engineering while keeping the roadmap unchanged.” AI didn’t invent the belief that engineers are interchangeable output factories. It just handed executives a shinier, cheaper-sounding excuse to run the same playbook again.
That’s why lumping AI-using engineers in with the executives hollowing out their companies does real harm. Telling the story as “AI makes engineers dumb and indifferent” accidentally writes bad leadership an alibi: shucks, the new technology didn’t live up to the hype, who could have known? And on the way there, it sneers at the people in the trenches who are grabbing every piece of leverage they can find to keep increasingly absurd systems from falling over.
If you want to know whether someone gives a shit about a system, asking whether they used an LLM is the wrong question. Ask what happens when the code they merged breaks at two in the morning. Ask whether “the model wrote it” flies as an excuse in code review. Ask whether they have the time, standing, and backing to tell a VP that a deadline is reckless.
Companies lose smart people who give a shit when they strip away ownership, punish dissent, and reward shipping volume over reliability. Give those same people real stakes and a good model, and they don’t check out. They finally get to build the guardrails, proofs, and fixes they never had the hours to build before.
Michael is right that capitalism has its hand squarely on a hot stove right now, and that the rest of us are getting burned while executives relearn that you can’t automate away accountability. I just wish we’d aim the anger at the people making that bet, instead of pretending that giving a shit requires typing every character by hand.
Cover: Jan Collaert I after Jan van der Straet (Stradanus), Nova Reperta: The Invention of Book Printing, c. 1600. CC0, source.

Amazon Drone Crashes On Garage After Delivering Shampoo, Draws FAA Probe
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

Amazon Drone Crashes On Garage After Delivering Shampoo, Draws FAA Probe (arstechnica.com) 116
			
		 	
				The Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) has opened an investigation into an Amazon Prime Air delivery drone that struck a fiber-optic cable and crashed onto a residential garage in Michigan after delivering shampoo. The drone lost a propeller and became entangled in power lines, prompting Amazon and local utility workers to coordinate its removal. Amazon offered to help repair the damaged roof and gave the homeowner a $100 gift card. Ars Technica reports:  Amazon Prime Air currently operates a fleet of battery-powered MK30 drones that can deliver packages weighing up to five pounds and have onboard sense-and-avoid technology to avoid obstacles. However, the drone clearly failed to avoid the thin fiber-optic cable in this case -- so hopefully the FAA investigation would take that into account along with Amazon delivery drone procedures.
The FAA preliminary accident and incident notification briefly describes the incident as "UAS struck a fiber cable, a residential garage, and became entangled in power lines during takeoff." That notice was posted on November 23. The National Transportation Safety Board is also gathering information about the incident but has not yet decided whether to open its own probe, according to CNBC. That US government agency holds primary responsibility for investigating aviation accidents and other transportation incidents. 
Amazon said the Michigan crash was the first involving its drone delivery operations near Detroit, but federal regulators are already investigating several incidents in Arizona and Texas involving collisions with a construction crane, an internet cable, and an apartment building.
An Amazon spokesperson said Prime Air drones make nearly 6,000 flights per day and have delivered "hundreds of thousands of packages this year."

====================================================================================================

Swift Death of Statelessness
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
06:23:09 UTC
15:23:09 KST

Swift Death of Statelessness
My drive home from work is always spent in total silence. The monotony of Route 101 (it's not a flex) has an uncanny way of clearing cognitive noise. Today, somewhere in that quiet stretch of highway, reflecting on my work in the Kubernetes ecosystem, it struck me just how completely our industry is reinventing itself.
For 15 years, cloud computing was worshiping a statelessness as an absolute deity. Build stateless containers, spin them up dynamically, let them die without consequence. And this architecture was simple, resilient, and economically predictable.
And then AI happened, violently dismantling prior art. Reasoning models and multi-turn agentic workflows are inherently stateful. Every interaction carries a prompt history, a context window, and an expensive KV-cache. When you route a prompt to an arbitrary node using standard round-robin load balancing, you discard that cache. You force saturated GPUs to recompute tokens that were already processed milliseconds ago.
In economics, we call this pure deadweight loss. In distributed systems, it's referred as cache thrashing. The architectural frontier of networking is no longer about blindly moving packets from point A to point B. It is an economic allocation problem: balancing network transport latency against the marginal cost of GPU recomputation.
We spent a decade decoupling state from compute. Now the laws of physics and economics are forcing us to design intelligent proxies to put them back in the same room.

====================================================================================================
